# CLAUDE.md

This file provides guidance to Claude Code (claude.ai/code) when working with code in this repository.

## 作業のルール

- **命名とTypeScriptの書き方は `DEVELOP/CODING_STYLE.md` が一次情報。** 新しいコードと変更範囲を
  同文書へ揃える。既存コードに残る違反を踏襲しない。
- **全体像はコードではなく文書から掴む。** 「どこで何が起きているか」「誰が状態を持つか」「いつ走るか」
  は `DEVELOP/OWNERSHIP.md` / `DEVELOP/CALLSTACK.md` / `DEVELOP/SPEC.md` / この CLAUDE.md が一次情報。
  当たりを付けてから必要なファイルだけを読む。手順は `.claude/skills/overview/SKILL.md`(`/overview`)。
- **検証は変更箇所に対応させる。** 既定は `npm run typecheck` のみで、これは常に走らせる。
  `npm run test:physics` は `src/physics/` を触ったときだけ。ヘッドレス実行検証(`/verify`)は
  ユーザーが実行時の動作確認を明示的に求めたときだけ。変更と無関係な検証に時間を使わない。
- **リファクタリングの判断基準は `/refactor`(一般方針)と `/refactor-fixed`(このプロジェクトで確定済みの
  責務境界)。** `/refactor-fixed` に書いてよいのは、**プロジェクト全体・数十ファイルに及ぶ横断的な規則**
  (命名規則、update と sync の分離、physics/ の純度など)だけ。2〜3モジュール間の責務の調整は、
  `/refactor` の原則から外れない限りここに書かない — それは CLAUDE.md と `DEVELOP/OWNERSHIP.md` が担う。
  横断的な規則そのものが変わったときだけ `.claude/skills/refactor-fixed/SKILL.md` を同じ変更セットで
  書き換える(追記ではなく、全体が整合するように書き直す。古い判断は残さない)。
- **HUD/UI/DOM/CSS(パネル・ウィンドウ・ボタン・入力欄・タッチ操作)に触れる変更は、書き始める前に
  必ず `/ui-design` の手順を通す。** トークン・ウィジェット・オーバーレイの開閉規則・置き場4種・
  タッチ対応の規約は `DEVELOP/DESIGN-RULES.md` が正本。
- **改名は痕跡を残さない。** コードにも文書にも歴史的経緯を書かない。「旧」「former」「previously」
  「〜だった」の類は禁止。互換用の旧名エイリアスも残さず、旧名は全文検索して 0 件にする。
- **コメントは `/comment` の方針に従う。あらゆる変更で、書く前と書き換えた後に必ず見直す。**
  大原則は「そのモジュールの責務外に言及しない」。書いてよいのはモジュール/関数の「なにをするか」
  「どう使うか」と、相当に非自明な実装の理由だけ。「どう実装しているか」「なにをしないか」
  「誰が使うか」「以前どうだったか」「何を変えたか」は書かない。冗長なコメントは書き換えのたびに
  増えるので、変更セットごとに自分の書いたコメントを点検する。逆に、**関数・メソッドの直前の
  呼出規約コメントと、10行以上の関数の文脈コメントは、無いこと自体が不足**とみなす。大規模な変更を
  終えた後は `/comment-cleanup` で既存コメントを一括点検する(過剰と不足の両方向)。
- **関数・機能の追加要求を受けたら、書き始める前に必ず `/add-feature` の手順を通す。** まず既存の
  類似実装を探し、再利用可能な形で適切なモジュールにあればそれを呼ぶ(追加実装しない)。そうでなければ
  適切なモジュールへ再利用可能な形で実装し、**既存側もその呼び出しに置き換えるまでを同じ変更セットで**
  行う。数行の処理でも例外にしない。
- **共通化するかどうかは、参照箇所の数ではなく「今後も使う可能性があるか」で決める。** これはコード
  からは判別できないので、`DEVELOP/SPEC.md`「実装される可能性のある機能」節を見て、決まらなければ
  ユーザーに問う。ユーザーが可能性に言及したものは同節へ記録する。
- **`memos/hedalu244/refactoring_todo.md` は todo リスト。** 経緯は残さず、完了した項目は消す
  (そこで下した責務配置の判断は、消す前に CLAUDE.md / `DEVELOP/OWNERSHIP.md` の該当節へ移す)。
- **デバッグ用のコードは、頼まれたことだけを、頼まれた形でそのまま書く。** デバッグ用コードの価値は
  「何が起きてほしいかがコードを読んだだけで自明」であることにあり、それを損なう実装は本番コードの
  過剰実装より悪い。よって以下は**要求されない限り一切書かない**: URL クエリ・環境変数・設定値による
  分岐、有効/無効の切り替え、件数や強度のパラメータ化、毎フレーム走る仕掛け、状態(カウンタ・フラグ・
  「一度だけ」の保守)、失敗時のフォールバック。値は定数を直書きし、呼び出しは1箇所から1回にする。
  「デバッグしやすいように気を利かせる」判断そのものが要求の逸脱にあたる。

## 設計文書 — 更新は義務

**`src/` を変更したら、同じ変更セットの中で下記の文書も更新する。**「あとでまとめて」は禁止。
判定手順と全面再生成用のプロンプトは `.claude/skills/develop-docs/SKILL.md`(`/develop-docs`)にある。
`src/**/*.ts` を編集すると PostToolUse フック(`.claude/hooks/remind-after-src-edit.mjs`)が
コメント方針の自己点検と文書の同期判定を促す。

- **この CLAUDE.md**(下記 Architecture / Controls) — モジュールの責務と「なぜそうなっているか」の解説。
  ファイルの新設・削除・移動・改名、責務の移動、キー割り当てや操作系の変更、定数の意味が変わる変更で
  更新する。**古い記述を残すより消す方がよい。**
- `DEVELOP/CALLSTACK.md` — per-frame 呼び出し依存木(update / sync / render)。**呼び出し順と実行条件の
  一次情報。** 毎フレーム走る関数の追加・削除・改名・順序変更・条件変更で更新する。
- `DEVELOP/OWNERSHIP.md` — インスタンス保持木と状態の正本(source of truth)。クラスの new 位置・
  フィールドの所有・参照共有・キャッシュが動いたら更新する。
- `DEVELOP/SPEC.md` — 「どう振舞うべきか」の仕様。プレイヤーから見える挙動・数値が変わったら更新する。
- `DEVELOP/DESIGN-RULES.md` — UI/デザインの規約(トークン・ウィジェット・オーバーレイの開閉規則・
  置き場4種・タッチ対応)。`theme.ts`/`hud/widgets/`/`hud/overlay-manager.ts` を含む HUD 側の
  構成が変わったら更新する。

役割の切り分け: この CLAUDE.md は**散文の解説**(責務と理由)、DEVELOP/ の各文書は**機械的な事実**
(順序と所有)。両者が食い違ったら **DEVELOP/ を正とし、CLAUDE.md を直す。**
リファクタリングのやることリストは `memos/hedalu244/refactoring_todo.md`、論点ごとの todo は
同フォルダの各 `*_todo.md` を参照する(こちらも CLAUDE.md より新しい)。

## Current state

A playable LEO (low Earth orbit) shooting game: TypeScript + Webpack + npm, Three.js `WebGPURenderer` (via the `three/webgpu` entry point). Real-scale/real-time Earth orbit (player starts at 420 km / 97° inclination), body-frame RCS translation, manual rotation with RCS damping and a prograde-hold autopilot, time warp, lead-marker gunnery against enemies that shoot back with lead-aimed plasma, shell casings and destruction debris on accurate orbital physics, win on destroying all enemies, lose on being shot down or on atmospheric reentry (drag decay → adiabatic-heating overheat or dynamic-pressure breakup). Also: Earth-shadow lighting, target-board bullet-pass markers, an orbit-planning map mode ([M]: Earth-centered camera, with the camera's frame and the planned trajectory's frame selected independently on two separate panels, click the planned trajectory to place a maneuver node — several nodes can be chained, each frozen as an absolute post-burn state — W/S/A/D/Q/E adjust Δv, [N] auto-warps to the next node, BURN guide shows remaining Δv until the actual orbit matches the planned elements within tolerance), and five stages (stage 2 = Molniya-orbit enemies, unlocked once stage 1 has been cleared — tracked via `unlock-manager.ts`'s `UnlockManager`, which persists per-stage clear counts to localStorage and toasts newly-unlocked stages; stage 0 = near-combat training scoreattack, stage 00 = infinite survival, CREATIVE = the least-restricted stage, which decides no win or loss and lets ships/enemies/pickups/bases be placed at will; stage 0/00/CREATIVE are always unlocked; `?stage=` URL param skips the select screen). Each frame is `Game.update` → `Game.sync` → `Game.render`; the render phase draws `cameraSystem.activeCamera` — combat chase cam (near 2 / far 6e7) or the map camera (near = `dist / OVERVIEW_CAMERA_NEAR_RATIO` clamped to at most `CELESTIAL_SHELL_RADIUS × cos(screen-diagonal half-FOV) × OVERVIEW_CAMERA_NEAR_SHELL_MARGIN` (=0.9), so it scales with the current zoom distance instead of a fixed value while never clipping the star/celestial-grid shell — even at the screen's corners — at extreme zoom-out / far = `dist × OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_RATIO` clamped to `[OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_MIN, OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_MAX]` = `[1.5e10, 1e16]`, so it too grows with zoom rather than clipping distant objects at a fixed range) —. Stars/sun/moon billboards follow the active camera position, and the star/celestial-grid shell radius is fixed at `CELESTIAL_SHELL_RADIUS` (=1.35e10, 10% inside the far-plane floor so shell geometry never sits on it) independent of the far plane, so the map zoom-out doesn't exit the star shell.

`memos/mikanixonable/dev.md` is explicitly marked as human-authored only ("この文書は人間のみが記入できる") — do not edit it. Read it for project context, but leave modifications to the user.

### Naming: render / update / build / sync (no `draw`)

一般の命名とTypeScriptの書き方は `DEVELOP/CODING_STYLE.md` が正本。この節はフレーム処理の責務境界を
解説する。

Per-frame function names must reflect what they actually do, consistently across the codebase:

- **`render`** — reserved for functions that literally call the three.js renderer's `render(scene, camera)` (`Game.render` delegates to `render/pipeline/render-pipeline.ts`'s `RenderPipeline.render`, which is the one that actually calls it, once per pass). A function that only constructs or mutates scene state is not a "render" function even if it runs in the render phase (e.g. `Player.syncPlayer`, `EnvironmentScene.sync`).
- **`update`** — advances or mutates logical/simulation data (not three.js mesh state directly).
- **`build`** — registers new meshes/objects into the scene (construction, not per-frame).
- **`sync`** — matches the transform/appearance of already-registered meshes (or HUD DOM elements) to logical data that was already computed elsewhere. Prefer plain `sync*` names; don't tack `Render` onto a sync method name (e.g. `syncCamera`, not `syncRenderCamera`) — it blurs the render/sync boundary this rule exists to keep clear.
- **`draw`** is never used — rename on sight.

The rAF loop in `main.ts` makes this split structural: it calls `Game.update(dt)` → `Game.sync()` → `Game.render()` as three separate top-level phases. **Only `update` takes `dt`** — nothing in the sync phase may advance anything, so no `sync` anywhere takes one (a display-side refresh cadence, e.g. the six `hud/hud.ts` panel classes, holds a `performance.now()` deadline instead). `update` must not touch THREE.js objects; `sync` builds the frame's `FloatingOrigin` and pushes already-computed logical state into meshes/DOM; `render` only issues draw calls. Keep sub-functions on the correct side of that line — state that merely happens to be read at render time still belongs with the system that owns it.

### Commands
- `npm run dev` — start webpack-dev-server at http://localhost:8080
- `npm run build` — production build to `docs/` (webpack's `output.path`, with `clean: true`). **`docs/` is gitignored**, so a local build leaves no diff and publishes nothing. Publishing is `.github/workflows/build.yml` alone: a push to `main` runs typecheck+build, then force-adds the built `docs/` (`git add -f`) onto a `release` branch it force-pushes, and that branch's `docs/` is what GitHub Pages serves. The bot's commit carries `[skip ci]` so it can't trigger itself.
- `npm run typecheck` — `tsc --noEmit`
- `npm run test:physics` — regression tests for `src/physics/*.ts` (pure functions, no DOM/THREE deps): compiles `tests/physics/*.test.ts` + the physics modules to CommonJS via `tsconfig.test.json` (outDir `tests/dist`, gitignored) and runs them in node with `node:assert/strict` (no extra devDependency). Covers vec3 smoke tests, elements.ts Kepler round trips (`stateFromOrbitalElements`⇄`orbitalElementsFromState`, `trueAnomalyAt`/`positionOnOrbit`/`velocityOnOrbit`, `nodeAnomalies`'s textbook ascending-node direction/antipodal nodes/coincident-plane null/circular-orbit finiteness), dynamics.ts's `stepRK4` one-period drift and epoch advance, `j2Accel` RAAN regression rate, attitude.ts kinetic-energy conservation + quaternion norm, atmosphere.ts density-table boundary/monotonicity, kepler-orbit.ts's Kepler-equation round trip, velocity-vs-central-difference agreement (static/planet-scale-secular/precessing orbits), and rotation-basis omega-vs-finite-difference; satellite-orbit.ts's node/perigee precession direction and period, two-body-equivalence with an empty perturbation table, velocity agreement with perturbation terms included, the m-only/f-only double-counting guard, and the Moon's geocentric distance range; ephemeris.ts's `Ephemeris` class composition (Earth pinned exactly to the ECI origin, the sun-distance range against Earth's orbital eccentricity rather than a fixed value, a barycenter invariant — the mass-weighted average of Earth/Moon ECI positions and velocities against the barycenter derived independently from `PlanetOrbit` — the ~4,673 km/sidereal-month barycenter offset between the actual sun ECI position and where it would be if Earth followed a pure Kepler orbit, both Sun-Earth and Earth-Moon `lagrangeAt` distance ratios against their published values — Earth-Moon L1 0.15093 / L2 0.16783, Sun-Earth L1 0.00997 / L2 0.01004 in orbit-radius units, which is what catches a regression to the Hill-radius approximation — and the lunar equatorial-inclination sweep 18.3–28.6° over a node period via `orbitNormalAt('moon', …)`), attractor.ts's `attractorAccel` (the central-gravity case at the origin body, a zero-distance body leaving only the origin-correction term, and the differential-tidal formula for the moon/sun), `strongestAttractor` (LEO picks earth, the moon/earth handoff at 30,000 km/50,000 km from the moon, and the attractorAccel-not-raw-μ/d² comparison that keeps the sun from winning at 2.6×10⁵ km), `localOrbitPeriod` (LEO ≈5,580s / low lunar orbit ≈7,066s, pinned to measured values), `reachedBody` (a 20 s step at orbital speed passing clean through a 500 m body, which the point test misses, detected; a near miss not; an already-inside start, a zero-length interval, the margin band, and non-finite input each resolved through the point fallback), `orbitalElementsOf` propagating its `OrbitalElements.center` into a lunar-centered `tofBetween`, and `Ephemeris.attractorsAt`'s `[earth, moon, sun]` ordering/values agreement with `positionOf`; body-orientation.ts's Cassini construction (the spin axis sitting exactly `MOON_OBLIQUITY` from the ecliptic pole, the spin axis/orbit normal/ecliptic pole triple product vanishing, the resulting 6.688° spin-to-orbit-normal separation held across time, and the long axis leading/lagging the true Earth direction by several degrees rather than tracking it), `degree2Accel`'s agreement with the previous Y-axis-only J2 formula to machine precision, its equivariance under rotating position and body axes together, its agreement with the central difference of the degree-2 potential (the oracle for `C22`, which has no closed secular rate), the `C22` sign flip over a quarter turn about the pole, and the lunar J2 magnitude 3.96e-4 m/s² at a 100 km lunar orbit; srp.ts's zero-coefficient and fully-shadowed short circuits, the sun-to-body direction, the magnitude at 1 AU against `SOLAR_PRESSURE_1AU`, the inverse-square falloff and the linear shadow scaling, plus a `stepDynamics`-level check that a sunlit body is pushed and one in the umbra is not (the pure function being right does not prove it is wired in), halo.ts's linear frequencies and the third-order amplitude constraint against the ISEE-3 halo, shadow.ts's `sunlitFactor` (full daylight with no occluder, an Earth-umbra center reading exactly 0, penumbra monotonicity sweeping outward across a body's angular disk, the Moon's own shadow producing a genuine eclipse, an occluder on the far side of the querying point from the star producing no shadow, an occluder disk sitting entirely within the star's disk reading the closed-form annular value `1 − (r_occ/r_sun)²`, and every result staying within 0..1 across several simultaneous occluders), projection.ts NDC/screen round trips, kinematic-state.ts's `orbitAxes`/`fromOrbitAxes` orthonormality (including the eccentric-orbit case where `radOut` leaves r̂), frame.ts inertial⇄rotating round trips (both rotating frames, including the moon staying pinned to +X in its own frame), dynamics.ts's `stepDynamics` agreement with a hand-written legacy central-gravity + third-body + J2 composition to machine precision (plus thrust and drag effects, and a one-revolution low-lunar-orbit drift check), deque.ts ring-buffer push/pop ordering, capacity growth, and out-of-range error handling, state-queue.ts's out-of-order push discarding, age/minCount-bounded cleanup, and Hermite-interpolated lookup, dynamic-trajectory.ts's `sampleInterval`/`keepDuration`-gated history recording, `at()` accuracy against direct re-integration, and `reset`/`prevState` invariants, and trajectory-features.ts's reporting a spurious periapsis) and `findEquatorCrossings` (a coarse-sampled analytic Kepler ellipse's ascending-before-descending sign/zero-latitude correctness on an inclined orbit), `shape.test.ts`'s `radius >= max(axes)` invariant across every shaped body plus Saturn's/Jupiter's `Rp/Re` ratio and two triaxial axis-ratio spot checks, and `game/celestial/point-field.ts`'s clustering at three longitudes 120° apart relative to Jupiter). `tests/physics/n-body.test.ts`'s mutual gravitation among `Attractor`-shaped bodies (an independent analytic two-body period check, total-momentum conservation for two- and three-body systems, convergence to the point-mass limit as a body's mass goes to zero, and full regression against the existing `dynamics.test.ts` suite), `spatial-grid.test.ts`'s `SpatialGrid.neighbors` agreement with a brute-force distance filter (no pair within one cell size goes missing) and its no-duplicate-return guarantee; `astronomical-time.test.ts`'s canonical J2000 TDB pin/JD agreement, 5-digit-year Gregorian round trips with no `Date`, leap-year century/year-zero edge cases, J2000 landing on noon 2000-01-01 TT/TDB, agreement at e=0.751, published node-regression/perigee-advance periods entering with the right signs, Saturn's inner satellites referencing the Laplace plane rather than the ecliptic — Mimas's orbit normal sitting over 26° from the ecliptic pole — Phoebe retrograde against both Saturn's spin pole and the ecliptic pole, and Iapetus's/Phoebe's ecliptic inclinations against published values); and `small-bodies.test.ts`'s 32 registered small bodies (`lRate` against Kepler's third law, Sedna's velocity-vs-central-difference agreement at e=0.860, every eccentricity within the elliptic solver's <0.98 assumption, and radii finite/positive and circumscribing wherever a `shape` is present); `body-visibility.test.ts`'s `lagrange.ts` stability thresholds (Routh's critical mass ratio matching the `27mu(1−mu) = 1` root, triangular-point stability flipping across it, and the collinear-point clearance reproducing the registry's own threshold on both sides) together with `visibleBodyIds` (the set-shaped statement of the same class/focus admission rule `MapVisibilityPolicy` applies per id — no production caller reads it, the test suite pins it)/`systemChainAt`/`systemMembersAt` (stars/planets/small bodies visible by default, a focused body's parent, children and siblings surfacing with no toggle set, a class toggle admitting that whole class, depth counted from the star, an unregistered or `undefined` focus still leaving stars/planets visible, the system chain walking moon→earth→sun by camera position and collapsing to the star alone near it, and `systemMembersAt` excluding a star's own children while never repeating the body it starts from); `shortcut-hint.test.ts`'s `shortcutKeyLabel` special-casing; and `plan.test.ts`'s `segmentDurationFrom`/`nodeTimeRange` (a lunar-orbit segment sized from the moon-centered state and μ, a transfer orbit near periapsis not being shortened to the circular period of its periapsis radius, and the node time range following whatever span the `DisplayDurationSource` reports) plus the anchor invariant itself (`anchorOr` returning the caller's own state until the first node freezes one and the frozen one after, `frozenData()` being `null` on both sides of that node's life, the anchor falling with the last node whether it is removed or consumed, and `revision` staying monotonic across an empty↔non-empty round trip). Where no clean theoretical value exists (RK4 drift, J2 rate, table-boundary continuity), the test pins the *measured* current behavior with a loose margin rather than an idealized bound — run this before/after any change under `src/physics/`.
- `npm run export-assets` — regenerates the pre-baked assets under `src/assets/`: `tools/export-models.mjs` writes the ship/debris/ammo meshes to `src/assets/models/*.json` (the authoring source for the primitive assembly `render/ships.ts` used to do at runtime). It pulls the values it must agree with out of `src/` through the TypeScript compiler API rather than copying them — `RCS_NOZZLES` from `render/rcs-nozzles.ts` and `RADIATOR_HINGE` from `render/radiator-hinge.ts` — so a baked asset and the code that reads it cannot diverge. Not part of `build` — run it only when the models or the nozzle table change. `Object3D.toJSON()` mints fresh UUIDs on every run, so **all** of `src/assets/models/*.json` comes back dirty even when only one mesh changed; revert the files whose diff is UUIDs alone rather than committing the churn.

There is no other automated test suite wired into npm beyond `test:physics`.


### Architecture

Simulation state lives in ECI coordinates (Y axis = north pole), SI units (m, m/s), as plain `{x,y,z}` data (`physics/vec3.ts`'s `Vec3` is a **branded type** — build it with `v3()`/the helpers, never with a bare object literal).

`KinematicState` is a **timed** state vector — `{t, r, v}`, where `t` is the absolute simTime epoch — and it is the *single* type used for every "state at a moment": entity state, entity history, predicted-trajectory samples, and maneuver-plan nodes. There is deliberately no second type carrying the same information (the former `TrajectorySample`, `LineSample` and `PlannedNode.time` were folded into it), so a state and its time can never be passed as two arguments that disagree. `stepRK4` advances `t` along with `r`/`v`, which is why `toFrameState` takes no separate time argument.

**The base data types are immutable, with no exceptions**: `Vec3`, `KinematicState` (`physics/kinematic-state.ts`), `Quat`/`Attitude` (`physics/attitude.ts`) are all `readonly` and are advanced by *replacement*, never by in-place mutation — `stepRK4` returns a new `KinematicState`, `stepAttitude` returns a new `Attitude`, and every owner assigns the result (`entity.state = …`, `entity.att = …`). This is a correctness invariant, not a style preference: `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory` **pushes the outgoing state onto `history`** on every `step`/`reset`, and `game-entity/game-entity.ts`'s `GameEntity.orbitalElementsAround(center)` memoizes its result on `state`'s object identity plus `center.id` — both can only stay correct because nothing else can change the state behind its back. Consequences: defensive copying is unnecessary (`vec3.clone()` does not exist), and **everything under `physics/` is a pure function returning fresh values** — there are no `*Into`/`out`-parameter variants and no module-level scratch buffers. Those existed (軽量化計画 A-1) and were deliberately removed. The cost is real but small: in an isolated Node benchmark `stepRK4` went 1520 → 2190 ns/step and `stepAttitude` 218 → 414 ns/step, which at a typical ~400 live entities works out to roughly +0.35 ms per frame in the `update` phase — not distinguishable from run-to-run noise in the actual game (~5 ms update phase). The immutability that buys is what makes the `elements` memo correct, so the trade is intentional. If the entity budget grows enough for this to matter, the answer is a physics LOD (軽量化計画 B-1), not reintroducing mutable scratch into `physics/`. Rendering uses a **floating origin**: `game/floating-origin.ts`'s `FloatingOrigin` is rebuilt every `Game.sync` with its position (`r`) at the active camera's ECI position (`CameraSystem.activeCameraPos`, already resolved by that frame's `cameraSystem.update()`) and its velocity basis (`v`) at the player's velocity, and `RtoThreeV3`/`VtoThreeV3` are the *only* bridge from logical `Vec3` to `THREE.Vector3` — so f32 GPU precision never sees absolute magnitudes, at LEO or at the map view's astronomical-unit zoom-outs. Every `sync` takes the `fo` and must not use `player.state.r`/the camera position as the render origin directly (both are semantically distinct types from the `fo` they happen to agree with in combat view). Combat camera: near=`COMBAT_CAMERA_NEAR`=2m, far=`COMBAT_CAMERA_FAR`=6e7m — no logarithmic depth needed (see comment in `const.ts`).

Physics runs on the main thread (per-entity central-gravity two-body integration is cheap).

`src/game/` is an orchestrator (`game.ts`) plus subfolders grouped by concern: `player/` (player ship state), `game-entity/` (the entity base and derived classes, one file per class), `simulation/` (the array that holds live entities, plus the passive, non-AI/non-input systems that drive it each frame — stepping, prediction, collision, hit detection), `camera/`, `plan/` (maneuver planning and its future-trajectory display, independent of whether the map is open), `stages/` (per-stage behavior), `hud/` (DOM overlay), `marker/` (screen-projected markers), `vfx/`, and `input/`.

**`*Ctx` snapshot objects are a rejected pattern here** — there are none left, and adding one back is not on the table. Every one of them has been expanded into explicit arguments or shared references.

- `src/main.ts` — entry point: WebGPU scene init (`initScene`, showing `loading-overlay.ts`'s loading overlay while the WebGPU pipeline compiles), an error overlay fallback, and the rAF loop (`startAnimationLoop`). It owns `Hud`/`AudioEngine`/`Bgm`/`WorldSfx`/`UiSfx`/`PauseMenu`/`SettingsView` (`initHud`) — these are constructed before `Launcher`, and so before the title screen and before any `Game` exists, because the title/select screen needs them too. `initHud` also takes the `RenderPipeline` `main.ts` builds immediately after `initScene`, threading it straight into `PauseMenu`'s constructor as the `DebugTargetHost` its 描画 tab's `GraphicsPanel` writes `debugTarget` through. `main.ts` never constructs a `Game` itself and holds no reference to one: it builds `Launcher` (right after `initHud`, see `src/launcher.ts` below) with every constructor-time resource a launch needs — `Hud`/`GameScene`/`AudioEngine`/`Bgm`/`WorldSfx`/`UiSfx`/`PauseMenu`/`SettingsView`/`UnlockManager`/`FrameSections`/`GraphicsSettings`/`RenderPipeline`/`SaveSlots`/`SnapshotService` — wires `PauseMenu.onPauseMenuOpenChange`/`SettingsView.onOpenChange` to `launcher.current?.pause()`/`.resume()` (reading the launcher's own `current` getter each time rather than a local variable, since there is no `Game` yet on a title-screen frame) and `onBgmVolumeChange` to `Bgm.setVolume`, constructs `SaveBrowser`/`PerfMeter` against `launcher` itself (satisfying `SaveBrowser`'s `CurrentGameSource`) and wires `onQuitToTitle`/`SaveBrowser.onLoadSnapshot`/`SaveBrowser.onSlotSwitched`/`PauseMenu.onOpenSnapshots`/`onOpenPerfWindow` as one-line delegations to `Launcher`/`SaveBrowser`/`PerfMeter`, then calls `await launcher.start()` once before ever calling `startAnimationLoop` — deciding *when to discard the running `Game` for the next one*, and *building the next one*, both belong to `Launcher` alone, so `main.ts` contains no `location.*` call of its own — the only one anywhere is `src/fatal-error.ts`'s own reload button (below), which both `main.ts`'s top-level `main().catch(...)` and the rAF loop's own `try`/`catch` call into on an unrecoverable exception. `startAnimationLoop(launcher, ...)` reads `launcher.current` once per frame: a frame where it's still `null` (before `start()` has resolved, or between a discard and its replacement) only advances `lastTime` and reschedules itself; otherwise it drives `Game.update` → `SnapshotControls.handleInput` → `Launcher.handleInput` (reads its own held `current` rather than taking that frame's `game` as an argument) → a check that `launcher.current` is still the same `game` (a restart triggered from inside that very call disposes it synchronously, so the rest of the frame — `PerfMeter.handleInput`/`AutoSave.update`/`Launcher.update`/`Game.sync`/`Game.render`/`GpuTimings.resolve` — is skipped outright rather than touching the now-disposed instance) → `PerfMeter.handleInput` → `AutoSave.update` → `Launcher.update` → `Game.sync` → `Game.render` → `GpuTimings.resolve`, with the optional `PerfMeter.record(game, ...)` measuring around `sync`/`render` while the window is open. `GpuTimings.resolve` runs whenever a `Game` still exists at that point in the frame, and after the render phase's own clock mark — it must drain the renderer's timestamp queries every frame, and its cost must not be counted as render time.
- `src/loading-overlay.ts` — `showLoading()`/`hideLoading()`: the loading screen shown while WebGPU initializes (`main.ts`'s `initScene`) and while a launch's `Ephemeris` is being built (`launcher.ts`'s `initEphemeris`). Module-level state tracks whether it's currently shown, so both callers can call either function without coordinating — `showLoading()` while already shown and `hideLoading()` while already hidden are both no-ops.
- `src/fatal-error.ts` — `showFatalError(title, message, error)`: the full-screen overlay shown when initialization or a run transition cannot continue — `main().catch(...)`, the rAF loop's own `try`/`catch`, and `Launcher`'s own `fail(err)` (below) are its three callers. A second call while one is already up is a no-op (checked by `#fatal-error-overlay`'s presence), and its one interactive element is a button that calls `location.reload()` — the sole navigation left anywhere in the codebase, since every other run transition now discards and rebuilds a `Game` in place rather than leaving the page.
- `src/launcher.ts` — `Launcher implements RunTransitions, CurrentGameSource`: the one place that decides **to discard the running `Game` and move to the next one** — restart, return to title, load a snapshot, switch save slots — by disposing it and constructing its replacement in the same page, so nothing under `game/` ever calls `location.*` (a `Game` has no way to observe or refuse its own `dispose()`, so the discard itself never reaches it). It also **holds the `Game` of the current run**, as a private field exposed through the public `current: Game | null` getter (`CurrentGameSource`, `game/hud/save-browser.ts`'s own interface) — the one window every other module that needs to reach "whichever `Game` is running right now" reads through, rather than closing over the reference from the moment it was constructed. Constructed by `main.ts` right after `initHud()`, taking every resource a launch needs to build a `Game` with: `Hud` (also for the `ResultScreen` it owns to register with `hud.overlayManager`), `GameScene`, `AudioEngine`/`Bgm`/`WorldSfx`/`UiSfx` (the audio set a `Game` is built with, plus — `AudioEngine`/`Bgm` — the two `Game` never sees: `startRun` wires the fresh `Input`'s `onUserGesture` to `audioEngine.unlock()`+`bgm.autoStart()` **per run**, since `Input` is rebuilt with each `Game`, and `endRun` silences the engine loop and stops the music the moment a run decides), `PauseMenu`, `SettingsView`, `UnlockManager`, `FrameSections`, `GraphicsSettings`, `RenderPipeline`, `SaveSlots`, `SnapshotService`. `start(): Promise<void>` (called once by `main.ts`, before `startAnimationLoop`) resolves a stage and starts the run: the private `resolveStage()` picks what launches without the title screen — `?title=1` forces the title screen outright; `?stage=<id>` (via `findStageClass`) resolves directly to that stage's class — no separate URL flag for CREATIVE, since it is a `StageId` like any other (`?stage=creative`); otherwise it falls back to the active save slot's last-played stage (the module-private `resumableStageClass`, returning that stage's class or `null` — resumable only while the stage is still valid, unlocked, and non-`hiddenFromSelect`) — and only shows the title screen (the private `selectStageScreen()`, wrapping `selectStage` with the escape/close/settings callbacks built from `resolveStage`'s own held `hud`/`pauseMenu`/`settingsView`) when none of that resolves anything. The private `endRun()` is the sole teardown a transition opens with — `this.game?.dispose()` then `this.game = null` then `this.resultScreen.close()` (idempotent, see `hud/result-screen.ts` below) then `this.resultShown = false` then `this.pauseMenu.toggle(false)` — safe to call with no run in progress, which is what lets every transition method below call it unconditionally. The private `startRun(stageClass, snapshotId?)` calls `endRun()` first, then resolves the private `initialSaveFor(stageClass, snapshotId)` (the snapshot a launch should start from — an explicit `snapshotId` takes priority when the caller has one; otherwise it falls back to the active slot's latest snapshot for that stage only when this launch resumes the slot's own last-launched stage (`SaveSlots.activeSlot().lastStageId === stageClass.id`), and calls `SaveSlots.discardAfter` once a resolved id actually loads), builds the launch's `Ephemeris` via the module-private `initEphemeris` (showing `loading-overlay.ts`'s loading overlay around `stageClass.createEphemeris(...)`), draws `earthSpinPhase0` (`initialSave?.earthSpinPhase0 ?? Math.random() * 2π`, the sole `Math.random()` call anywhere in this path), assigns `this.game = new Game(...)`, calls the private `noteLaunched(stageClass)` (records the launch via `SaveSlots.noteLaunch` and remembers `stageClass` for `restart()`/`loadSnapshot()`'s own use), and finally calls `bgm.resume()` so the previous run's `endRun`-triggered silence (via `update()`, below) picks back up for the new one — `Game`'s constructor is still handed the already-resolved class directly, never an id. `handleInput(input)` (called from the rAF loop right after `SnapshotControls.handleInput`, so it only ever sees the input edges that frame's `Game.update` left unconsumed) takes `[R]` only while `this.game !== null && !game.activeStage.isPlaying` and calls `restart()`. `update()` (called from the rAF loop right after `AutoSave.update`) closes a decided run out exactly once — a private flag guards it — silencing the engine loop/BGM (`WorldSfx.setThrust(false)`/`Bgm.stop()`), calling `SaveSlots.noteRunEnded(activeSlotId)` (clearing the slot's `lastStageId` so the *next* launch of the same stage can't mistake a snapshot taken moments before this decision for a run still in progress — see `save-slots.ts` below), and showing the result screen it owns (`ResultScreen`, `hud/result-screen.ts` below) with `game.activeStage.result` — falling back to a generic win/lose result derived from `phase` alone whenever a restored save carries a decided `phase` but no saved `StageResult` (`StageResult` itself is never persisted). Neither `handleInput` nor `update` takes a `Game` argument any more — both read `this.game` directly, since `Launcher` now owns that reference rather than the rAF loop handing it one each frame. `restart()`/`returnToTitle()`/`loadSnapshot(id)`/`switchSlot()` are the sole entry points that discard the running `Game`; each stays a synchronous method (`RunTransitions`'s own shape, and what the DOM click handlers that call them expect) that fires off its own `startRun`/`selectStageScreen` promise chain without awaiting it — `restart()` re-runs `startRun(this.launchedStage)` (a no-op with nothing launched yet); `returnToTitle()` calls `endRun()` before showing the title (`selectStageScreen()`) so the closed-out run and its result screen never linger under it, then starts whichever stage is chosen; `loadSnapshot(snapshotId)` re-runs `startRun(this.launchedStage, snapshotId)`; `switchSlot()` calls `endRun()` then starts the newly-active slot's own resumable stage, falling back to `selectStageScreen()` when it has none. Every one of the four catches its own promise chain's rejection (an `Ephemeris` pack fetch failing, most plausibly) through the private `fail(err)`, which reports it via `src/fatal-error.ts`'s `showFatalError` rather than leaving `current` silently stuck wherever the failure left it. `Launcher` constructs its own `ResultScreen(hud, this)`, so the result screen's two buttons and `[R]` all funnel through the same `restart`/`returnToTitle` methods, and its own registration with `hud.overlayManager` (input-gating only, `closeOnEscape`/`closeOnOutsideClick` both false) is what keeps map/combat pointer input from reaching the background world the moment it's shown.

  `main.ts` also owns `SnapshotControls` (`snapshot-controls.ts`, below), constructed after `Hud`/`PauseMenu`/`SaveBrowser`/`SnapshotService` exist and driven from the rAF loop, and wires `SaveBrowser.onLoadSnapshot`/`onSlotSwitched` to `Launcher.loadSnapshot`/`switchSlot`.
- `src/snapshot-controls.ts` — `SnapshotControls`: `[F5]` (clip the current moment as a named manual snapshot, via `SnapshotService.capture`, refused with a hint once `!activeStage.isPlaying`) and `[F9]` (open/close `SaveBrowser`, closing `PauseMenu` first since the two share the same `system` overlay band). It does not handle `[Esc]` — closing the browser via Escape is `hud/overlay-manager.ts`'s `OverlayManager`'s own job (the browser registers itself as an overlay on `open()`, see `save-browser.ts` below, and `Game.handleInput` dispatches Escape to the topmost registered overlay). Owned and constructed by `main.ts` (after `Hud`/`PauseMenu`/`SaveBrowser`/`SnapshotService` all exist) and called from `startAnimationLoop` as `snapshotControls.handleInput(game.input, game)` immediately after `game.update(dt)` — so it only ever sees the input edges that frame's `Game.handleInput` left unconsumed, the same "later module never sees a taken edge" contract every `Input.takeKey` caller follows. `Game` exposes its `input: Input` publicly for exactly this call; `Game` itself knows nothing about snapshots.
- `src/game/stage-select.ts` — `selectStage(unlockManager): Promise<StageClass>`: the title screen, tabbed (via `hud/widgets/tab-bar.ts`'s `TabBar`, which renders correctly here even though this screen sits outside `#hud` since `widget-style.ts`'s selectors aren't `#hud`-scoped) by each non-`hiddenFromSelect` entry of `stage-dictionary.ts`'s `STAGE_CLASSES` and its own static `selectGroup`, collected in first-seen order rather than two hardcoded tabs — currently ステージモード (0/00/1/2, gated per-stage by that class's own static `isUnlocked`, checked through `UnlockManager.isUnlocked`) and クリエイティブモード (CREATIVE alone, `Stage.selectGroup = 'クリエイティブモード'`, unlocked like any stage that doesn't override `isUnlocked`). No `Stage` instance is ever built to read this — every field the screen shows (`selectLabel`/`selectSub`/`selectLockedSub`/`selectKeys`/`selectGroup`/`hiddenFromSelect`) is read straight off the class, and choosing an entry resolves the promise with that class itself. `launcher.ts`'s `Launcher.resolveStage` calls this only once `?title=1`, `?stage=`, and the active save slot's resumable last stage have all been checked and none resolved — `?stage=creative` skips the screen entirely, the same shortcut every other `StageId` gets. The corner debug-stage link resolves `StageDebug` directly rather than going through `STAGE_CLASSES`, and the per-stage select-screen keyboard shortcuts (`Stage.selectKeys`) live here too, and match regardless of which tab is currently active.
- `src/frame-sections.ts` — `FrameSections`: the per-frame accumulator of how long each *section* of `update` took. **Owned by `main.ts`**, which constructs it and hands the same instance to `Game` and to `PerfMeter` by reference — the boundaries lie inside `Game.update`, but the aggregation, the formatting and the decision of whether to measure at all belong outside it, so `Game`/`Simulator` only mark boundaries (`enter`/`exit`) and hold no totals of their own. A section is a **numeric index** (`SECTION`'s values, 13 of them) rather than a string: `enter`/`exit` sit on the hot path of every frame the window is open, and an index lets the accumulator be a flat `Float64Array` with no hashing or per-call allocation. **`enabled` gates every clock read** — while false, `beginFrame`/`enter`/`exit`/`endFrame` return before touching `performance.now()`, so a closed load-inspection window costs the running game nothing; `PerfMeter.open`/`close` is the flag's only writer, the same "measuring costs nothing while not shown" rule `PerfMeter.on` follows. Sections are never nested: the integration's three constituents (軌道積分 = `Simulator.substep`, 接触 = `ContactPhysics.resolveSubstep`/`resolveBelt`, 姿勢 = `Simulator.stepAttitudes`) sit alongside the rest in one flat array and are distinguished only by an indent in `SECTION_LABELS`, since a real parent/child structure would need the accumulator to know which sections sum into which. `otherMs()` is whatever fell outside every section — `update`'s own total (`beginFrame`→`endFrame`) minus the sections' sum, with those three integration constituents left out of that sum so time inside 積分 is not subtracted twice. `Simulator` marks its own three boundaries rather than having `Game` bracket `advance` from outside, since they lie inside the substep loop and only that class knows where they fall. Nothing under `src/physics/` is instrumented — a measurement point there would be paid for by every caller including the tests, and the granularity `update` needs is already reachable from the game layer.
- `src/gpu-timings.ts` — `GpuTimings`: the per-frame accumulator of how long each *render pass* took on the **GPU** [ms], the sibling of `frame-sections.ts` for the draw side. Same shape — a numeric index (`GPU_PASS`'s values, `gbuffer`/`lighting`/`material`/`world`/`composite`) into a flat `Float64Array`, an `enabled` gate whose only writer is `PerfMeter.open`/`close` — and one difference: **the values arrive asynchronously.** `resolveTimestampsAsync`'s own return value is only the whole frame's total, though — three.js's WebGPU backend separately keeps a duration per render call, keyed by an opaque per-call `uid`, in `backend.timestampQueryPool['render'].timestamps`; that map isn't reachable through `@types/three`'s public `Backend` type, so the one place `GpuTimings` reads it (the private `renderTimestampPool` method) does it through a single narrow structural cast. Turning those uids into per-pass durations is a producer/consumer handshake spanning the frame: `beginPass(id)` (called by `RenderPipeline.render` immediately before each `renderer.render()` call it issues) declares which pass the *next* render call belongs to; a module-private `PassInspector extends InspectorBase`, installed as the renderer's own `inspector` in the constructor, is the one hook three.js offers into `render()`'s per-call uid — its `beginRender(uid)` override fires synchronously inside that same `renderer.render()` call and records `uid → the just-declared pass` before resetting the pending pass to `null`, so a `render()` call three.js issues with no preceding `beginPass` is simply left unattributed rather than stealing another pass's slot. `resolve()` matches its pending uids against whatever the pool resolved, zeroing `elapsedMs` and writing the matched durations only once at least one uid actually matched — an unresolved frame leaves the previous readings in place rather than reporting a spurious 0. **three.js never clears `timestamps` itself**, so every uid it has ever seen would otherwise accumulate there for the renderer's whole lifetime; `resolve()` clears the pool unconditionally at the end of every call, and separately caps its own pending uid→pass map so an unresolved uid can't accumulate there either. Owned by `main.ts`, constructed with the `WebGPURenderer` (the timing is a fact about the renderer, the same reasoning that keeps `PerfMeter`'s draw-call counts out of `Game`). **`resolve()` is called every frame from the rAF loop regardless of whether the window is open** — three.js allocates two timestamp queries per pass and only a resolve drains them, so skipping it while closed would exhaust the pool and corrupt the readings once it reopened; the `enabled` gate therefore governs only whether `beginPass`/the inspector callback do any bookkeeping and whether a resolved match is stored. It is called *after* the render phase's own `t3` mark so the measurement's own cost cannot land inside the `render` row it sits beside. `supported` stays false on a device whose WebGPU adapter has no `timestamp-query` feature, and the readout prints 未対応 rather than a fabricated 0.
- `src/perf-meter.ts` — `PerfMeter`: the load-inspection window, opened with `[F3]` or the `[Esc]` menu's 負荷を表示 item (and open from the start with `?perf=1`). Owned by `main.ts`, not by `Game` — the measurement points are the rAF loop's own `game.update`/`sync`/`render` call boundaries. `handleInput(input)` takes `[F3]`; `main.ts`'s rAF loop calls it right after `snapshotControls.handleInput`/`launcher.handleInput`, alongside the other input consumers that only see whichever edges `Game.update` left untaken that frame. **`on` is `true` exactly while the window is open**, so a closed window costs the running game nothing: `main.ts`'s `perf.on ? performance.now() : 0` never reads the clock and `record` is never reached. The constructor also takes the `WebGPURenderer` `main.ts` already holds via `GameScene`, and `hud.overlayManager` (its `PropertyWindow`'s own registration point) — draw-call/triangle counts are a fact about the renderer, not about `Game`'s simulation state, so `Game` gets no renderer reference of its own to route them through and `PerfCounts` carries none of it. The constructor takes no `Game`/`PerfCountSource` reference of its own: `record(counts, updateMs, syncMs, renderMs, now)` takes that frame's `PerfCountSource` (`main.ts`'s `game`, i.e. `Launcher.current`) as its own leading argument and forwards it to the private `flush(counts, now)`, the same "read the current run through an argument, hold nothing" shape `AutoSave`/`SnapshotControls` already followed — a `Game` a `Launcher` builds fresh for a later run is picked up automatically, with no reconstruction or re-wiring of `PerfMeter` itself. Display is a `hud/property-window.ts` `PropertyWindow` in the `window` overlay layer (draggable, 📌-clippable, sections foldable), refreshed every 500 ms — `syncRows`'s own differential update is what keeps the window off the frame budget it is measuring. The three rows carrying no `group` (fps / frame / warp) are always visible; the rest fold into フレーム時間 / update内訳 / 描画 / 計画軌道 / 予測 / シミュレーション / 暦キャッシュ / 表示 / エンティティ / メモリ sections, collapsed by default. The update内訳 group is one row per `frame-sections.ts` `SECTION` plus a trailing その他 row for `otherMs()`; a section no frame passed through still prints as `0.0ms` rather than being omitted, since a missing row and a zero one mean different things when reading where a frame went. Opening and closing the window is also what flips `FrameSections.enabled`, so those rows exist exactly while something is looking at them. Durations read as fill-character bars (`████░░░░ 12.3ms`) against a 16.7 ms budget; the 描画 section's draw-call/triangle counts have no such budget and read as plain `avg N max N`. The 描画 group also carries one `GPU <pass>` row per `gpu-timings.ts` `GPU_PASS` — **the GPU's own execution time, which is a different number from the `render` row's CPU wall clock** (that one only measures how long it took to submit the commands), which is the whole reason the two are shown side by side. `record` reads `renderer.info.render.drawCalls`/`.triangles` right after that frame's `game.render()` call and before `WebGPURenderer`'s `autoReset` zeroes them again next frame, so the value read is always exactly that frame's own draw calls. `Game.perfCounts()` only answers values (`PerfCounts`); the aggregation, the differencing of `Ephemeris`'s cumulative cache counters against the previous flush, and the formatting are all this module's.
- `src/game/game.ts` — orchestrator, and **only** that: it constructs and wires the subsystems and decides the per-frame call order. Owns `isPaused` (write-gated behind the public `pause()`/`resume()` methods — called by `main.ts` (via the `PauseMenu` wiring above), `docking.ts` (entering/leaving the dock), and `hud/save-browser.ts` (opening/closing the snapshot browser)). It does **not** own `simTime` (that's `Simulator`), the win/lose `phase`/`result` (that's `Stage`), which of the 0..n self-flown ships is operated (that's `active-player-controller.ts`'s `ActivePlayerController`, exposed as the public readonly `activePlayers`; `Game.player` is a plain getter forwarding to `activePlayers.current`), the frame's display-window/gravity-source window (that's `display-window-manager.ts`'s `DisplayWindowManager`, held privately and read back as `displayWindowManager.current`/`displayWindowManager.attractorsAt(t)`), and it does **not** construct `Hud`/`WorldSfx`/`UiSfx`/`PauseMenu`/`UnlockManager` (all received from `main.ts` by way of `Launcher`, see above — `AudioEngine`/`Bgm` are not among them: `Game` neither holds nor decides audio lifecycle) or decide which stage to construct itself (its constructor takes an already-resolved `stageClass: StageClass` and `new`s it directly, inline, uniformly for every stage including CREATIVE — `Game` holds no stage-vs-CREATIVE branch and no `instanceof CreativeStage` check anywhere), or build the launch's `Ephemeris`: its constructor takes a completed `ephemeris: Ephemeris` and an already-resolved `earthSpinPhase0: number` as two more arguments ahead of `initialSave` — `main.ts` builds the former via `stageClass.createEphemeris(phaseOffsets)` and draws the latter (`initialSave?.earthSpinPhase0 ?? Math.random() * 2π`) before `Game` exists at all (see `main.ts` above), so neither a phase-offset map nor a random draw ever appears inside `Game`. It knows nothing about snapshots as a step applied after the fact: its constructor takes an optional trailing `initialSave?: GameSaveData` and, when present, threads each piece straight into the constructor of whichever subsystem owns it as it builds the world exactly once — `new EntityManager(scene, hud, worldSfx, markerManager, initialSave)` rebuilds `players`/`enemies`/`ammos`/`bases` from that snapshot and otherwise leaves every array empty, since a fresh launch's ships are the `Stage`'s own placement (`stages/stage.ts`'s `addPlayer`, below) — which ship is operated is not its decision; `ActivePlayerController` is constructed right after and takes `initialSave?.activePlayerId` directly, resolving it against `entities.players` itself (id match, else the first ship, else `null`) — `PlanEditor` is constructed after and takes the `ActivePlayerController` itself rather than a `Player` (its `plan` getter reads `activePlayers.current` fresh each time, so it never needs to be handed a ship directly), and `CameraSystem` itself takes no `player` argument at all, since `ChaseCamera` holds no ship reference of its own (see `chase-camera.ts` below) and reads whichever ship it's handed each frame instead, so it no longer needs to be constructed after the player is known. `initialSave?.simTime` seeds `Simulator`; the constructor's own `ephemeris` argument becomes `this._ephemeris` directly and its `earthSpinPhase0` argument seeds `EnvironmentScene` (`new EnvironmentScene(scene, ephemeris, earthSpinPhase0)`); `initialSave?.camera` seeds `CameraSystem`, `initialSave?.camera?.view` is passed to `ViewManager` as its requested initial view — resolved there through the same `canEnter` rule `setView` uses, so a launch with zero ships still falls back to the map since combat view has no meaning without one to fly, which is exactly why `ViewManager` is constructed **after** `new stageClass(...)`: a campaign stage's ship is placed by that stage's own `init`, so which view a launch can even start in is only decided once the stage has finished building the world — and `initialSave?.stage` seeds the `new stageClass(...)` call — there is no second pass and no method a caller could use to overwrite a running instance's state afterward; its `readonly input: Input` field is public specifically so `main.ts`'s `SnapshotControls` can read this frame's leftover input edges from outside.

  `update(dtRaw)` is one linear flow rather than several duplicated early-return paths: `handleInput(dt)` (edges every owner takes regardless of pause/decision state) → (unless paused) `advanceSimulation(dt)` (`entities.requestHistoryDuration(displayWindowManager.current.pastDuration)` first, so the frame's integration records enough past for the drawn past-trajectory span — the previous frame's window is enough, since a retention window one frame stale changes nothing about what can be drawn → `entities.updatePlayers` — every self-flown ship's own `behave(input, dt, simDt, entities, activeStage, ephemeris)`, folding a non-operated ship's transient commands from inside that same call rather than a separate pass, and never gated on the stage's decision, see `entity-manager.ts`/`player.ts` below → `Stage.update` → `Simulator.advance` → `displayWindowManager.resolve(simTime, player)` → board marks / `activePlayers.reclaimDead()` (unconditional, every stage) / `Docking.checkProximity` → `Predictor.update` → `EffectsSystem.update` → `PlanGuide.update`) → `displayWindowManager.resolve(simTime, player)` again (a no-op past its first call this frame unless something it's keyed on moved — see `display-window-manager.ts` below) → `EnvironmentScene.update` → `PlanEditor.update` → `Targeter.updateEquatorNodes`/`EntityManager.updateBaseEquatorNodes` (both self-gated on `cameraSystem.overviewMode`, since combat view never draws what they compute) → `MapPickables.refresh` while `cameraSystem.overviewMode` → `CameraSystem.update` → `handlePointerInput()` (the map-edit or combat-targeting pointer dispatch, gated — while paused or while an input-gating `OverlayManager` overlay is open — on nothing being dispatched at all; not on the stage's decision, so the operated ship still receives pointer input after a win/loss, until `Launcher`'s own `ResultScreen` registration starts gating it). `sync()` re-resolves `displayWindowManager` once more for the post-integration state before the camera → environment → player → entities → VFX → orbit lines → markers → HUD panels sync chain. **It does not know which key does what**: the key↔action table is `input/key-mapping.ts`'s `KEY_MAPPING`, and `handleInput` takes `[Esc]` itself — closing whichever `hud/overlay-manager.ts` `OverlayManager` overlay is topmost (`closeTopmostOnEscape`), falling back to opening the pause menu (`PauseMenu.toggle(true)`) only when nothing was open to close — and every open overlay's own item shortcut at the same priority (`Input.takeKeys` over the frame's remaining pressed codes, each handed to `OverlayManager.dispatchShortcut`), then hands `Input` to each owner in priority order (`Hud` `help` → `SimSpeedManager` `warpSlower`/`warpFaster` → `ViewManager` `toggleMapMode` (called as `handleInput(input)` — leaving map for combat is refused with a hint whenever `ActivePlayerController.current` is `null`, rather than taking a caller-supplied flag) → `PlanEditor` `deleteNode`, `autoWarpToNode` (taken only while `!editMode`, the same "WASDQE-shaped keys stand aside during Δv editing" rule) plus the six Δv-edit keys (claimed via `Input.takeHeld` while a node is selected, which is why this runs before `Player.behave` reads WASDQE — see `input/input.ts` below)), and each consumes what it owns. Keys that only make sense while playing are taken by their owner's own per-frame call further down (`Player.behave` for `rcsDampToggle`/`progradeReset`/`fineAttitudeToggle`/`progradeHoldToggle`/`throttle*`/`reload`, `CombatCameraSystem.update` for `followAttitudeToggle`/`gunsightZoom`, `CameraSystem.update` for `camera*`, `MapContextActions.handleMapRightClick`/`handleLeftClick`/`handleDoubleClick` → `PlanEditor.handleMapPointer` → `MapContextActions.handleEmptySpaceRightClick` for map clicks while editing, `Targeter.handleTargetSelectKey`/`NavTarget.handleCombatBaseClick`/`MapContextActions.handleCombatRightClick` for combat right-clicks). `MapPickables.refresh()` is called from a single site inside `update`, gated on `cameraSystem.overviewMode` and placed right before that call's `cameraSystem.update()` — building the candidate set post-integration keeps its coordinates in step with the post-integration state `sync` renders that same frame, instead of lagging a step behind under high time-warp. It constructs and drives `ActivePlayerController`, `DisplayWindowManager`, `CameraSystem`, `MapPickables`/`MapContextActions` (the frame's pick candidates and everything done with a pick — see `map-pickables.ts`/`map-context-actions.ts` below), `EntityManager` (which itself owns `EffectsSystem`, see `entity-manager.ts` below), `Simulator`, `Predictor`, `Targeter`, `NavTarget`, `PlanEditor`/`PlanGuide`, `ViewManager`, `Docking`, `SimSpeedManager`, `MarkerManager` (which itself owns the two marker sets no single object can own — `combatMarkers`/`leadMarkers`, see `marker/` below), `FrameControls` (exposed as the public readonly `frameControls`, since it's the sole entry point every other module's "move focus" action must call rather than reaching `OverviewCamera.setFocusTarget` directly — see `hud/frame-controls.ts` below), and the active `Stage` (constructed inline from the already-resolved `stageClass`, see above) — `Ephemeris` is the one exception: `main.ts` builds it before `Game` exists (see `main.ts` above), and `Game` only holds the reference its constructor is handed, exposed as the public `ephemeris` getter. `Game.perfCounts()` merges six per-owner `perfCounts()` slices (`EntityManager`/`Predictor`/`Simulator`/`PlanEditor`/`Ephemeris`/`MapPickables`) plus `displayDurationSec`/`warp`, rather than aggregating any counts itself. `Game.dispose()` is the constructor's exact inverse and the root of the disposal chain: it calls each subsystem's own `dispose()` once, in reverse construction order (later subsystems hold references to earlier ones), and undoes the three things the constructor wrote onto objects that outlive the game — `Hud`'s `creative-mode` root class, the `Input` reference late-injected into `Hud.statusPanel`, and `WorldSfx`'s two continuous ship loops. `markerManager.dispose()` runs last, so every entity and display object has already withdrawn its own marker keys by the time the pool is emptied. The rule each subsystem follows below it, and the boundary against module-scope shared resources, are `DEVELOP/OWNERSHIP.md` §1-2. Many public fields (`cameraSystem`/`activeStage`/`entities`/`simulator`/`targeter`/`activePlayers`/...) exist because `Hud`'s six panel classes (`statusPanel`/`orbitPanel`/`targetPanel`/`contactsPanel`/`globalStatusBar`/`mapScaleBadge`, see `hud/hud.ts` below) each read the whole `Game` instance directly in their own `sync(game, ...)` rather than a narrow ctx.
- `src/game/active-player-controller.ts` — `ActivePlayerController`: owns which of the 0..n self-flown ships (`EntityManager.players`) is operated, and propagates a switch to every other owner that cares — `set`/`setOrNull` fold the previous ship's transient commands and forward to `Targeter` (cleared locks) and `WorldSfx` (silenced RCS loop); `remove` additionally reaches `NavTarget` and the camera focus — from this one place, so `Game` doesn't have to fan a swap or a removal out itself. It also holds a `CameraSystem` reference, kept solely for `remove()`'s `cameraSystem.overviewCamera.clearFocusIf(ship.id)` below — switching which ship is operated is never itself propagated to the camera. It holds **no** reference to `PlanEditor` or `MapContextActions` — `PlanEditor` holds a reference to *this* class instead and reads `activePlayers.current` fresh on every access (see `plan-editor.ts` below), and `MapContextActions` notices a removed ship's own property window going stale on its own each `sync` (see `map-context-actions.ts` below), so neither needs this class to reach into it. 0..n is the general form this class already treats uniformly; it knows nothing about which `Stage` is running, and it holds no reference to `ViewManager` — a lost/deselected ship never forces a view change on its own (see `docking.ts`/`view-manager.ts` below for the two places that do decide to move to map view). `set(ship)` (a no-op if `ship` is already `current`) clears the previous ship's transient commands, reassigns `current`, and forwards to `targeter.clearTargets()`. `setOrNull(ship | null)` forwards to `set` for a non-null ship; for `null` (no ship placed yet, all lost, or an explicit "operate nothing") it clears `current` and silences the RCS loop (`worldSfx.setRcs(false)`) — a `Player | null` `player` field with no ship flown has nowhere useful to show a combat camera, but which view is showing is left untouched. `remove(ship)` clears any nav-target/combat-target/map-focus reference to `ship`'s id first, unconditionally (`navTarget.clearIfTargeting(ship.id)` then `targeter.clearIfTargeting(ship)` — `Targeter`'s `CombatTarget` union includes `Player`, so a removed self-flown ship could otherwise linger as somebody's locked target — then `cameraSystem.overviewCamera.clearFocusIf(ship.id)`), then — if it was the active one — clears its transient commands and sets `current` to `null` before `entities.removePlayer(ship)`, and finally hands off to the next living ship (private `reclaimAfterLoss`, `entities.players.find(alive)` else `setOrNull(null)`) if the removed ship was active. It does not close any open `MapContextActions` property window itself — `MapContextActions.sync` finds a removed `'player'` on its own, once `entities.findPlayer(id)` stops resolving, and closes that window from there. `reclaimDead()` sweeps `entities.players` for any no-longer-`alive` ship and `remove`s each — a lost self-flown ship is removed the same frame as any other entity, so `Game.advanceSimulation` calls this **unconditionally**, once per frame, right after `Simulator.advance` returns (not from inside the substep loop — the reference cleanup and hand-off it does belongs outside the integration itself). `Game` exposes it as the public readonly `activePlayers`, constructed before `ViewManager` (which takes it as a constructor argument — see `view-manager.ts` below) so `ViewManager` can ask it whether combat view is enterable; `MapContextActions`/`Docking` call `game.activePlayers.set`/`setOrNull`/`remove` directly rather than `Game` forwarding those calls on their behalf.- `src/game/const.ts` — all gameplay tuning constants (thrust, sim speed levels, fire rate, hit radii, ammo/belt geometry, camera near/far, prediction throttles, stage tuning...), plus `LINE_RENDER_ORDER` — the whole assignment of orbit-line draw orders in one table, since a draw order is meaningful only relative to the other lines and must not be decided line by line (see `game/orbit-line.ts` below). Its "色管理" section holds only the game world's identifying/effect colors — direction-marker colors (`COLOR_MARKER_*`, including `COLOR_MARKER_ALLY`/`COLOR_MARKER_ENEMY`/`COLOR_MARKER_HP_EMPTY`), impact/plume/destruction VFX, orbit-line and hull colors, per-stage effect colors — never a UI color; the UI's own tokens are `theme.ts`'s (above).
- `src/game/floating-origin.ts` — `FloatingOrigin`: the render origin's motion state — `r` at the active camera's ECI position (kept near-zero relative to the camera itself, which is what keeps the WebGPU renderer's single-precision `modelViewMatrix` composition from losing precision to catastrophic cancellation at map-view distances), `v` at the player's velocity for relative-velocity-facing draws — and the sole `Vec3` → `THREE.Vector3` conversion (`RtoThreeV3` for positions, `VtoThreeV3` for relative-velocity-facing draws like bullets).

`src/game/player/` — the player ship, split by concern (all owned and composed by `Player`, which exposes read-only getters for `game.ts`/HUD):
- `player.ts` — `Player extends Ship`: initial LEO placement (`makeInitialState`/`progradeAttitude`, `INITIAL_ALT`=420 km / `INITIAL_INC_DEG`=97°; `progradeAttitude`'s `inertia` is asymmetric across all three axes — `PLAYER_INERTIA_PITCH/YAW/ROLL`, held as the shared `Player.INERTIA` so the constructor's `{saved}` branch builds the same tensor rather than a placeholder — so the Dzhanibekov effect isn't debris-only, and manual angular velocity has no cap: holding an RCS key keeps accelerating the spin indefinitely), the per-frame `behave(input: Input | null, dt, simDt, entities, activeStage, ephemeris)` (unconditionally belt+HP regen via the private `updatePassive`, then — only when `input` is non-`null` — own edge keys → torque → fire → thrust; a ship handed `null` instead folds its transient commands via `clearTransientCommands()` and returns). **The absent `input` is the whole statement of "this ship isn't being operated this frame"** — no boolean and no `SimSpeedManager` reference accompany it, because "not the operated ship" and "a warp level past the physical band" are the same state and are settled once, by `EntityManager.updatePlayers`, before the call. What the caller owes is exactly one call per ship per frame; keeping the ship consistent across that call — folding a thrust latch, silencing a held trigger, still advancing the belt and HP regen — is `Player`'s own job, which is why `updatePassive` runs ahead of the `null` check rather than being lifted out to the caller and `checkLoss`/`syncPlayer` entry points, damage/loss handling (`attacked` — which now also banks `thermal.addImpactHeat()` and, when `radiator.damageFromHit` reports a newly-destroyed panel, triggers a one-shot debris break effect at that panel's tip — and `checkLoss(dt, simTime, activeStage, playerPos, attractors)`, which checks hull heat/dynamic-pressure via `thermal` and, independently, whether the hull has reached any attractor's surface via `attractor.ts`'s `reachedBody(actualTrajectory.prevState, state, attractors, C.PLAYER_MIN_ALT)` — swept over the step's whole span, so a substep tens of seconds long at high time warp cannot carry the hull straight through a body between two sampled points; the atmospheric-heating/dynamic-pressure thresholds stay Earth-specific (only Earth has an atmosphere here), but the structural-breakup threshold is body-agnostic), and composes `throttle`/`fire`/`belt`/`thermal`/`radiator`/`power`/`thrustEffects`/`rcsEffects`/`markers`. **Every `Player` owns its own `plan: Plan`** (not just the one flying in a campaign stage) — `PlanEditor.plan` is a getter forwarding to whichever ship `ActivePlayerController.set` last made active, so switching the flown ship switches which plan is being edited without either class needing to know about the other's swap. There is no subclass for a placed ship: it is a plain `Player` constructed with an explicit `name`/`state`/`id` out of `PlayerInit`, whose fourth optional field `ammo: {mags, rounds}` is the ship's initial load-out — a ship is *built* carrying the right number of rounds rather than built with a default and then reloaded, so `PlayerFire`'s field initializers are the stock load-out and only a stage that deviates from it (stage0's empty magazines, the debug stages' 20) says anything at all. And `planExecution: PlanExecutionMode` (the type itself defined in `plan/plan-executor.ts`, not here — see below — and re-exported; `'off' | 'instant' | 'powered'`, read each frame by `CreativeStage.update`/`applySimulationEvents` to decide whether and how this ship's `plan` executes itself — `'instant'` is the historical teleport-on-node-time behavior, `'powered'` drives the `readonly planExecutor: PlanExecutor` field below) is on `Player` directly rather than something only some ships have. `behave` interrupts a `'powered'` ship back to `'off'` the moment the operator gives it manual translation (`this.thrust !== null`) or rotation (`throttle.hasManualRotationInput(input)`) input, the same way manual rotation cancels `progradeHold`. `syncPlayer(fo, camera, displayTime, isActive, ephemeris, attractors, visibility?)` takes `isActive` because `EntityManager.players` can hold more than one ship (CREATIVE) — only the active one hides for the gunsight zoom and pushes `markers` (the orbital-direction/boresight/`▲` HUD markers, which describe *the* operator's frame and would be meaningless duplicated across ships). `thrustEffects`/`rcsEffects`/`reentryEffects` sync off `displayState(displayTime) ?? this.state` rather than `this.state` directly — the plume/RCS-puff/reentry-glow billboards have to sit at the same future-ghost position the hull mesh itself is drawn at, or the effect visibly detaches from the ship the moment the slider moves; when `displayState` is `null` (beyond this ship's prediction horizon) the effects are called with `visible` forced false instead of being skipped outright, so each one hides itself through its own existing absent-state path rather than `syncPlayer` needing a second one. `handleEdgePress` claims the ship-side keys (`[T]`/`[F]`/`[V]`/`[C]`/`[1]`-`[3]`/`[R]`) via `input.takeKeys()`, which consumes them so no later module double-handles. `[R]` is only consumed when a manual reload actually starts. Right after `this.thrust` is set, `behave` calls `this.invalidatePrediction()` if it's non-null — the one place a `GameEntity` invalidates its own prediction *outside* `discardPredictionIfDiverged`'s distance check, because the player's own burn needs to be reflected in the predicted trajectory immediately (a UX requirement that check's few-frame lag can't meet) and this is deliberately player-only, not a `thrust` setter, leaving room for `Enemy` thrust to someday *not* reset immediately.
- `player-throttle.ts` — `PlayerThrottle`: translation input → thrust accel (`buildThrust`, all 6 body-frame directions), manual rotation + RCS damping, and the prograde-hold autopilot (target forward = velocity direction, target up = radial-out) built on `physics/attitude.ts`'s `attitudeAlignTorque` — the PD control itself (quaternion error → body-frame axis-angle → `PROGRADE_HOLD_KP`/`_KD` torque) is a pure function there, shared with `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s burn-direction alignment rather than living only here. `updateTorque` only *returns* the body torque (`Player` writes it to `entity.torque`) and `updateThrustState` only returns the thrust vector — the actual attitude/orbit integration is `Simulator`'s. **It runs on two clocks, and which quantity takes which is not interchangeable**: the RCS output ramp (`rotationHoldTime`, over `RCS_MANUAL_RAMP_TIME`) advances on the wall `dt`, since "how many seconds has the key been held" is an operational-feel quantity a time warp must not accelerate; every fuel draw (`Ship.consumeFuel`, in `buildThrust` and in `updateTorque` alike) advances on `simDt`, since the thrust and torque it pays for are integrated over exactly that span — drawing on the wall clock instead makes Δv-per-fuel depend on the warp level. `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s `PlanExecutor` draws its own burn's fuel on the same `simDt` for the same reason, so a manual burn and an autopilot burn cost the same. Each of the 6 translation keys is independently double-tap latchable (public `updateThrustLatches`, consuming `Input`'s press edges via `takeKey`; `isThrustHeld` ORs the physical hold state with the latch set), timed against `performance.now()` rather than a `dt`-accumulated clock so a double-tap's interval judgment doesn't depend on whether every frame in between actually called it. `updateThrustLatches` is `Player.behave`'s own call — reached only when that frame handed the ship an `input` at all, so a double-tap at a warp level past the physical band neither latches nor consumes the key edge, and dropping back below the threshold can't detonate a latch that silently accumulated during the warp — not folded into `updateThrustState`, since the latter runs unconditionally whenever real thrust is being computed and the two questions ("did a key just double-tap" vs "what thrust does the current hold/latch state produce") are separate. Holding the *opposite* direction key also holds that latch cleared for as long as the opposite key stays down (not just on its press edge) — otherwise a physical hold on the opposing key while one side is latched would only cancel it out in `buildThrust`'s per-axis sum, and releasing the opposing key would make the still-latched side reappear unannounced. `stopThrust()` (called from `updateThrustState`'s no-thrust branch and from `clearTransientState`) only zeroes `thrustAccelVec` (the belt's own fictitious-force input, see `belt.ts` above) now — it does **not** touch SFX or the plume, since `thrust-effects.ts`'s `ThrustEffects.sync` reads `this.thrust` (the `GameEntity` field, whichever of `PlayerThrottle`/`plan/plan-executor.ts`'s `PlanExecutor` last wrote it) directly every frame and owns the engine-sound toggle itself; a manual stop reaches the plume/SFX only because `ship.thrust` itself goes `null` on the same path, not through a second explicit call. `latchedThrustKeys`/`lastThrustPressTime` are both cleared by `clearTransientState`. Holding any of the 3 opposite-key pairs together (`isThrustKillSwitchActive`) is an emergency all-stop: `buildThrust` returns `null` unconditionally (bypassing the per-axis sum entirely, so it also zeroes any *other* axis's held/latched thrust, not just the pressed pair's own) and `updateThrustLatches` clears every latch instead of running its per-key toggle logic that frame — a single pair stops every axis, not just its own.
- `player-fire.ts` — `PlayerFire`: gun state (mag/round/barrel counters, reload timer), `updateFireState`/`fireCycle`/`consume` (the ammo state machine, returning `ConsumeResult` = `empty`/`normal`/`mag-reload`/`barrel-reload`), `fireGun` (bullet spawn, recoil, casing eject, muzzle flash, `ScoreCounter.recordShot`), and `dropBarrel`/`spawnEjectedMagazineFrame` for reload debris.
- `belt.ts` / `belt-physics.ts` — the ammo belt display split from its physics: `belt-physics.ts`'s `BeltPhysics` is a pure Vec3/Quat Verlet-chain solver (position-based dynamics; otherwise no THREE/mesh dependency — see `BeltSection` below for the one exception) for the belt's sag/twist under the ship's thrust+spin pseudo-forces; `belt.ts`'s `Belt` owns the link meshes and derives each link's transform from `BeltPhysics`'s solved state via parallel transport. `belt-physics.ts` also defines `BeltSection extends GameEntity` — the per-link contact proxy `game/simulation/contact.ts` resolves against (`BeltPhysics.collisionSections`/`applyCollisionSections` convert to/from it each frame). It's declared here rather than in `game-entity/` because nothing outside the belt uses it, and its constructor needs only a placeholder `THREE.Object3D` to satisfy `GameEntity`'s constructor — it's never added to a scene or stepped, so that placeholder is the only place this otherwise THREE-free module touches THREE.
- `thermal.ts` — `ThermalSystem`: **the single source of truth for the hull's heat budget** — aerodynamic heating, gun firing heat, bullet-impact heat and radiative cooling all land in `updateThermal`, plus dynamic pressure and the altitude-descent-warning EMA (`updateAltitudeAlarm`); returns a `ThermalLimit` verdict. `addGunHeat(rounds)`/`addImpactHeat()` both bank their energy in joules into the shared `pendingHeat` rather than writing `hullTemp` directly, because `updateThermal` runs once per substep and the deposited energy must not scale with how many times that happens — the actual destruction (`alive = false` + effects) happens in `Player.checkLoss`.
- `radiator.ts` — `RadiatorSystem`: the two deployable radiator panels (deploy fraction and hinge-angle `sync`). **It does not own the panels' damage** — `wear` (0=intact, 1=destroyed) is handed to `update(dt, wear)` every frame by `Player`, derived from the two radiator *parts*' remaining HP, so the thing the dock repairs and the thing that stops radiating are one and the same. Panels therefore never self-heal in flight; repair is the dock's job alone. It **does not know the hull temperature** — it answers only `radiatingArea()` [m²] and `solarLoad(sunlit, sunDir, att)` [W], which `Player` feeds to `ThermalSystem.setRadiatorLoad` each frame, so the Stefan-Boltzmann T⁴ law stays owned by `ThermalSystem` alone and no temperature round-trips between the two; `radiatingArea` multiplies `RADIATOR_PANEL_AREA` (the modeled dimensions) by the separate `RADIATOR_EFFICIENCY_MULT` performance multiplier, so tuning the panels' cooling power never has to fight the "model dimensions and physics stay one number" rule that `RADIATOR_PANEL_AREA` itself follows. `foldThetas(side)` is shared by `sync` and the panel-normal math so the drawn angle and the illuminated angle cannot diverge; because an accordion's even and odd folds face different ways, `solarLoad` evaluates both orientations and splits the area between them. A deployed panel is a real contact surface, not a hit-radius adjustment: `RadiatorFold extends GameEntity` is a one-fold-wide rigid collision proxy (`collides = true`, `attachedTo = owner`, so it never contacts the ship itself or the panel's own other folds/the belt's own nodes), and `collisionFolds(shipR, shipV, att, t)` re-derives each fold's world position/velocity from the ship's current state every frame — cheap enough to place fresh rather than track, since a fold's placement is a pure function of the ship's attitude and the panel's own deploy angle. Only sides deployed past `RADIATOR_CONTACT_DEPLOY` and not fully worn contribute proxies, so a stowed or destroyed panel can't be hit. `RadiatorFold.collideWith` forwards straight to `Player.collideAtRadiator(side, other, contact, activeStage)`, which special-cases a `Bullet` contact (routed to the same `attackedByBullet` a hull hit uses, tagged with `side` so the damage lands on that panel's part) and otherwise applies the contact's own impulse to the matching `radiatorParts[side]` — deploying a panel therefore both adds a real obstacle a bullet or another rigid body can strike and, once struck, funnels the damage to that specific part; `RadiatorSystem.tipWorldPosition` reads the destroyed panel's own fold mesh (post-`sync()`) for the one-shot break effect's spawn point, and `sync()` hides a fully-worn panel's fold meshes for good.
- `reentry-effects.ts` — `ReentryEffects`: the plasma glow ahead of the ship during reentry. **Stateless** — the intensity is derived from `ThermalSystem.qdyn` every frame (`REENTRY_GLOW_MIN_Q` → `REENTRY_GLOW_FULL_Q` mapped continuously onto scale and opacity), so there is nothing to advance in `update` and only a `sync`. It orients off the *absolute* ECI velocity rather than `fo.VtoThreeV3`, since the floating origin is the player itself and the relative velocity is therefore always zero here.
- `power.ts` — `PowerSystem`: the solar panels' charge, HUD-facing as `chargeRatio` (0..1). The panels are fixed (no deploy animation), so their normal is a constant body-frame `(0,1,0)` — `update(dt, sunlit, sunDir, att)` just rotates that one normal into world space, takes `max(0, dot(...))` (unlike `RadiatorSystem.solarLoad`'s `abs`, since a one-sided panel doesn't generate power off its back face), and banks the energy into `charge`, clamped to `POWER_CAPACITY`. Entirely numeric — no `sync()`, since there's no mesh state to push.
- `thrust-effects.ts` / `rcs-effects.ts` — billboard VFX (+ the shared engine/RCS loop SFX) for the main engine plume (positioned from `render/ships.ts`'s `MUZZLE_OFFSETS`) and for the attitude thrusters. Both read straight off the `GameEntity` fields the physics side already writes — `ThrustEffects.sync(fo, playerPos, thrust, maxAccel, visible, audible, camera)` takes `this.thrust` itself (not a `PlayerThrottle`-specific direction/throttle-index pair), so a burn commanded by manual input (`PlayerThrottle.updateThrustState`) and one commanded by `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s `PlanExecutor` (which writes `ship.thrust` directly, never touching `PlayerThrottle`) render identically without either module needing to know about the other; `maxAccel` (`ship.totalThrust / ship.mass`, computed by the caller) turns the thrust vector's magnitude into a 0..1 output ratio for the plume size, replacing the old fixed 4-step throttle-index scale so a continuously-varying autopilot output (full burn vs. trim) still reads proportionately. `RcsEffects` already followed this shape — it reads `this.torque` directly, not `PlayerThrottle`'s manual-rotation state, which is why `PlanExecutor`-driven attitude holds during a burn also light the correct nozzles for free. Both take an `audible`/`isActive`-style flag (`RcsEffects.sync`'s `audible`, `ThrustEffects.sync`'s `audible`) since every self-flown ship's plume renders but only the operated ship's engine/RCS loop should be heard — `RcsEffects` builds one plume per entry of `render/rcs-nozzles.ts`'s `RCS_NOZZLES`, and picks which ones fire by the torque each nozzle *produces*: `τ_k = r_k × (−exhaust_k)` is a per-nozzle constant derived once from the table, and a nozzle fires when `dot(τ_k, torque) > 0`. Because the nozzle's own direction is fixed hardware, the plume can only ever appear at one place per nozzle — a torque-dependent exhaust direction would slide the plume continuously around its block as the commanded torque turns.
