- `player-markers.ts` — `PlayerMarkers`: the HUD markers that follow from the ship's own position/attitude — the six orbital-frame direction markers (prograde/retrograde/normal/antinormal/radial in-out, the Navball stand-in), the `┼` boresight, and the map-mode `▲` self marker. `sync(currentState, displayState, att, overviewMode, isActive, project, scale, name, ...)` takes two states rather than one: the combat-view markers (direction/boresight) always read `currentState`, since they represent the ship's real orbital frame regardless of the future-ghost slider, while `▲` reads `displayState` (`null` beyond the player's own prediction horizon, in which case `▲` just hides) — the two states coincide whenever the slider is at rest, which combat view always is. `▲` is rotated to face `displayState.v` via `MarkerManager.headingRotationDeg(displayState.r, displayState.v, project, scale)`, so a circular orbit still shows which way the ship is actually moving; combat view needs no such hint (the mesh's own attitude is visible there) and leaves the direction markers unrotated. `Player.syncPlayer` drives it (only when that ship is the active one — see `player.ts` above), so callers never see a separate marker entry point.

`src/game/game-entity/` — the entity base and derived classes, one file per class:
- `game-entity.ts` — `GameEntity` (base: mesh/HP/alive/attitude/AI-facing state, plus the fields that let an instance stand in for `physics/attractor.ts`'s `Attractor` with no conversion: `readonly id: string` (explicit constructor argument, or self-assigned by the base `EntityIdAllocator` when omitted), `name: string` (the display name every marker/list/window shows, defaulting to the id — `Ship`/`Base` overwrite it in their own constructors, and a rename writes it, so there is no second name field anywhere), `radius = 0` (physical radius [m]; 0 = point, the same value `Attractor.radius` reads), `collides = false` (rigid-contact participation — independent of `radius`, since a bullet or debris fragment has a physical size but must not bounce off the hull), `mu = 0` (gravity constant GM; 0 = no gravity — a body is a gravity source purely by `mu !== 0`, decided nowhere else), `degree2: Degree2Gravity | null = null` and `isStar = false` — both plain mutable fields (not `readonly`) precisely so a derived class can build its own field at construction, the way `Asteroid` does. `protected setGravitatingMass(mass)` is the one place a mass becomes a `mu` (`mu = GRAVITATIONAL_CONSTANT * mass`, also recording `mass` itself for rigid-contact use), so a subclass never derives `mu` by hand. `EntityManager.attractors()` filters on `mu !== 0 && alive` alone — `radius`/`collides` don't gate it, since a body's shape and its collision participation are separate questions from whether it pulls. The constructor's `addToScene` argument (default `true`) controls only whether `obj` is added to `scene` — `Bullet` and a `casing`/`fragment` `DebrisPiece` pass `false`, since those are drawn through `EntityManager`'s `InstancedPool`s instead (`render/instanced-pool.ts`, above); `obj` itself is unaffected and still receives its transform from `sync` every frame, since the pool reads it from there. Delegates the actual "time-stamped state + its sample history + advance itself" job to `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts`'s `DynamicTrajectory`, held as the public `actualTrajectory` field. `state`/`prevState`/`history` are thin forwarding getters to `actualTrajectory` — the ~20 call sites across the codebase that only ever read `.state` are unaffected by this split. The `state` **setter** forwards to `actualTrajectory.reset(...)`, the sole entry point for a discontinuous external overwrite (rigid contact, recoil); `stepActual(dt, attractors)` never goes through it — it self-gates on `alive` and hands `actualTrajectory.step(...)` the caller-supplied gravity-source array plus the entity's own `bcInv`/`thrust`/`sampleInterval(attractors)`/`historyDuration` (a getter = `max(baseHistoryDuration, requestedHistoryDuration)` — the per-class floor raised by whatever past-display span `requestHistoryDuration(sec)` was last handed, capped at `HISTORY_DURATION_MAX`; the retained sample *count* stays bounded at `PREDICT_MAX_SAMPLES` regardless, since `sampleInterval` decimates against the same window, so a longer past costs coarser interpolation rather than more memory; an entity whose `baseHistoryDuration` is 0 keeps no history whatever is requested) (integration itself is `actualTrajectory`'s job, not this class's; `Simulator.substep` decides `attractors` for this step — see `simulation/attractors.ts` below — so `stepActual`/`stepPredicted` share the same argument shape). `orbitalElementsOf(center)` is the only way to get osculating elements out of an entity — there is no bare `elements` getter, since which body an ellipse is centered on is a per-call choice (typically `attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor`), not a fixed property of the entity; it memoizes on `state`'s object identity and `center.id`, both of which only change together with an actual new orbit. `sampleInterval(attractors)` derives the history's push interval from `attractor.ts`'s `localOrbitPeriod(state.r, attractors)` (the period around whichever body pulls hardest right now, divided by `TRAJECTORY_SAMPLES_PER_REV`, falling back to a `SHIP_HISTORY_DURATION`-based value when no finite period exists, e.g. hyperbolic orbits) and is skipped entirely while `historyDuration === 0` (bullets/casings/debris never need it), `torque`/`thrust` slots the owning system writes each frame, and a default `checkLoss(_dt, _simTime, _activeStage, _playerPos, attractors)` that calls `attractor.ts`'s `reachedBody(actualTrajectory.prevState, state, attractors, 0)` — surface contact with *any* attractor, not just Earth, since a stray debris piece near the moon shouldn't pass through it; this `attractors` argument is always `Ephemeris.attractorsAt`'s analytic-body-only window, as `EntityManager.cleanup` receives from `Simulator`, never the gravity-bearing-`GameEntity`-merged one, so approaching another gravity-bearing entity is never itself mistaken for reentry). `orbitLine: OrbitLine | null` (default `null`) is the entity's own Kepler-ellipse line around whichever body it currently orbits, set by whichever subclass constructs one (`Player`/`Enemy`/`Base` all do); `syncOrbitLine(show, fo, camera, attractors, force?)` is a no-op when `orbitLine` is `null` and otherwise resolves the center via `strongestAttractor(state.r, attractors)`, syncs it to `orbitalElementsAround(center)` (hidden when `!show`), and forwards `force` straight through to `OrbitLine.sync` — the entity resolves its own center rather than being handed one, so every subclass that owns an `orbitLine` shares this one implementation instead of each re-deriving the center/hide logic. `equatorNodes: EquatorNodeMarkerPair | null` (default `null`) is the same shape again for this entity's own EqAN/EqDN icons; `ensureEquatorNodes(markerManager)` lazily creates and returns it, so a module that decides this object needs them is the one that brings the pair into being, and `dispose()` takes it down with the mesh (see `marker/equator-node-marker-pair.ts` below). `dispose()` is also **the one place a mesh's own GPU resources are released**, for every entity kind: it removes `renderObject` from the scene, drops the marker/equator-node pair, then walks `renderObject` and disposes each mesh's geometry/material **only where `userData.ownsGeometry`/`ownsMaterial` says this instance owns it**. Those flags are declared at build time by whoever mints the mesh (`render/ships.ts`'s `withDispose`, and `cloneIndependent` which marks the material it clones but never the geometry it shares with the parse cache), and an unflagged mesh is treated as sharing — the safe default, since failing to free a per-instance resource costs memory while freeing a module-cached one leaves every later entity of that kind drawing from a released buffer. No subclass overrides this traversal. `trajectoryLine: TrajectoryLine | null` (default `null`) is the same shape for the integrated future in place of the analytic ellipse — set by whichever subclass constructs one (`Player` does); `pastTrajectoryLine: TrajectoryLine | null` (default `null`) is its past-facing counterpart, drawn from `actualTrajectory`'s own retained history rather than from the prediction. `syncTrajectoryLine(show, frame, simTime, pastDuration, ephemeris, fo, camera, attractors)` skips whichever of the two is `null` and otherwise calls `TrajectoryLine.syncGeometry(show ? this.predictedTrajectory : null, simTime, null, frame, ephemeris, attractors)`/`syncTransform`/`sync` — `predictedTrajectory` itself is the argument, not a per-call spliced array, `simTime` is the line's own drawn lower bound, and `null` for the upper bound draws all the way to the prediction's own tip (unlike `plan/plan-path.ts`'s per-segment lines, which clamp the upper bound to their own segment's end — see below): `TrajectoryLine` clamps the lower bound to its baked span and interpolates its sampler there for the leading vertex, so the line's leading end tracks the entity's current position (to within `discardPredictionIfDiverged`'s tolerance) without `GameEntity` needing to manufacture a first sample or splice it onto `predictedTrajectory`'s own array. Because the array `TrajectoryLine` bakes from is `predictedTrajectory.samplesOldestFirst()`, whose reference only changes when the prediction actually advances, `TrajectoryLine`'s reference-equality re-bake guard holds across ordinary frames — passing a fresh `simTime` only re-derives the sampler's time mapping, not the baked vertices. `supersedesAnalyticEllipse(simTime, horizon, overviewMode)` answers whether this line is enough to stand in for `orbitLine` on its own, for a caller to feed `orbitLine.setSuppressed` — the view split lives here, not at the call site, since only this class knows what a visible-but-not-yet-grown trajectory line means. In map view it keeps the rule that the ellipse stays up as the fallback for as long as the prediction's tip hasn't reached the currently displayed span (a freshly placed ship, a burn that invalidated the list, or time-warp outrunning `Predictor`'s growth budget, including a truncated prediction that will never grow further), rather than disappearing the moment a two-vertex stub exists. In combat view — where only the near vicinity of the ship is ever on screen, so "covers the display span" has no bearing on what's actually visible — it supersedes the ellipse the moment a trajectory line is drawn at all: leaving both up would show two ellipse-like lines splitting apart just ahead of the ship as J2/drag pull the integrated prediction off the analytic one.

- `src/game/display-window-manager.ts` / `hud/predict-panel.ts` — `DisplayWindowManager`: "when and in what coordinate frame to look" — resolves, once per frame, the `DisplayWindow` every future-aware consumer shares (`{frame, simTime, referencePeriod, duration, pastDuration, displayTime, tickLabelMode}`) and the merged attractor window (analytic bodies plus any living gravity-bearing `GameEntity`), instead of each consumer re-deriving them. **"Window" is an overloaded word in this codebase: here it names the coordinate-frame-plus-displayTime bundle this class resolves — not a time span alone, and not a DOM window** — a line drawn in one coordinate frame and a marker projected from another would visibly disagree, and a mesh drawn at one displayTime and a marker positioned at another would point at different instants, so the two have to be settled together, once, per frame. The class owns six independent pieces of settable state: the display duration (private `durationKey: DisplayDurationKey` = `'orbit'` (default) /`'day'`/`'week'`/`'month'`/`'custom'` → `durationSec(referencePeriod)`), the *past* display duration (private `pastDurationKey: DisplayPastDurationKey`, the same set plus `'none'` (default) → `pastDurationSec(referencePeriod)`, 0 for `'none'` — the symmetric counterpart of `duration`, so the drawn span is `[simTime - pastDuration, simTime + duration]`; changing it deliberately does *not* touch `sliderT`, since the past is not a span the future-ghost cursor reaches into), the tick-label notation (`tickLabelMode`, a get/set `TickLabelMode` accessor — `'absolute'` (default) writes a time label as its UTC calendar form, `'relative'` as the signed elapsed time from `simTime`; carried in `DisplayWindow` so every future-aware consumer's labels read the same way), the future-ghost slider position (private `sliderT`), `forceCurrent` (a get/set accessor, starting `true`, flipped by `ViewManager`; while set, `resolve()`'s `displayTime` always returns the present, and the setter also zeroes `sliderT` — leaving combat view always resets the slider to the present rather than resuming wherever it was left, and `panel.onDurationSelect`/`onCustomDurationConfirm` zero it the same way, since switching how far the slider can reach mid-drag would otherwise leave it pointing at a now-meaningless fraction of the new span), and `frame` (a get/set `ReferenceFrame` accessor — the coordinate frame the future is drawn in: the planned trajectory's polyline (`PlanPath`), a ship's predicted-trajectory line, and the equatorial-node markers all bake into this one frame, independent of `OverviewCamera.cameraFrame` — see `frame-controls.ts` below for how a player selects the two separately, and `overview-camera.ts` for why the camera's own frame is a distinct value). `durationSec` holds no orbital period of its own: for `'day'`/`'week'`/`'month'` it looks the constant up in a `Record<key, seconds>` table (a compile-time-exhaustive alternative to an if-chain — adding a preset without a table entry is a type error rather than a silent fallthrough), `'custom'` reads the private `customDurationSec` instead, and only `'orbit'` returns `referencePeriod` as-is (falling back to `C.APERIODIC_ARC_DURATION` when it isn't finite/positive) — every caller resolves its own notion of "one orbit" and passes it in, so this class never has to decide whose orbit or around which body. A private `currentOrbitPeriod(player, simTime)` supplies it for the game-wide display window (the active ship's osculating period around whichever body currently pulls hardest, `NaN` with no ship — it assembles the full gravity-source array and runs `strongestAttractor`); `plan.ts`'s `segmentDurationFrom` supplies it separately for the plan's trailing segment and `Plan.nodeTimeRange`, from that segment's own start state — the two contexts can disagree about "one orbit" without either owning the other. `resolve(simTime, player): DisplayWindow` is the single per-frame entry point — it takes `simTime`/`player` as plain arguments rather than holding `Simulator`/`ActivePlayerController` references itself, and caches on those two (plus `player.state`'s identity) and a private `revision` counter (bumped by the `frame`/`forceCurrent`/`tickLabelMode` setters and every `PredictPanel` callback — duration-pill select, custom-duration confirm, tick-label-mode toggle, slider drag, reset-to-now, jump-to-time), so `currentOrbitPeriod()` only actually reruns when one of those has moved — `Game` calls `resolve` from several points a frame (see `game.ts` above), most of which are cache hits and return `current` as-is. `attractorsAt(simTime)` merges `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(simTime)` with `EntityManager.attractors()` (`simulation/attractors.ts`'s `mergeAttractors`), cached on reference equality of both inputs plus `simTime` so repeated same-frame calls return the identical array. `sync(player)` pushes the whole frame's state into the panel through a single `panel.render(state)` call — no per-field setters — built from `current` plus this class's own `durationKey`/`customDurationSec`/`sliderT`/a private `predictionCoverageRatio(player)` (the active ship's `predictedTrajectory.state.t` expressed as a fraction of `current`'s `[simTime, simTime + duration]` span, clamped to `[0, 1]`, defaulting to `1` with no ship/prediction/duration — computed fresh inside `sync` itself, with no external push)/`sliderSteps()`, and also caches `current.duration` as `lastDuration` for a jump/edit input firing later that same tick outside the frame flow (`onJumpToTime`) to resolve `sliderT` against, and pushes the tick marks `hud/tick-scale.ts`'s `buildTicks(current.duration, TICK_MAX_COUNT)` returns (positions at round multiples of a chosen interval rather than equal divisions of the span, so the panel shows scale regardless of which duration is selected). `sliderSteps()` sizes the slider's step count from `lastDuration` at roughly `SLIDER_TARGET_STEP_SEC` per step, clamped to `[SLIDER_MIN_STEPS, SLIDER_MAX_STEPS]` — the panel's `sliderT` interface stays 0..1 regardless, so no caller needs to know the current step count. `PredictPanel` is its DOM: five rows — a future-duration pill row and a past-duration pill row, both built from the same module-private `DurationPillRow` (`1周`/`1日`/`7日`/`28日` fixed pills — the past row prefixes a `なし` pill — plus a `任意…` pill that swaps itself for an inline value+unit `DurationValueInput` when pressed, confirmed with ✓/Enter or cancelled with ✕/Escape/blur; a row being edited holds off its own re-render so a keystroke can't be overwritten), a `目盛りを相対表記` `ToggleSwitch` row (two named states rather than three-or-more, so it is a switch and not a `SegmentedControl`, per `DEVELOP/DESIGN-RULES.md` §3; ON reports `'relative'` back through `onTickLabelModeChange`), a scrubber row (a `⏮` reset-to-now icon button, the range slider itself — its track background linearly gradients from the normal color to a dimmed one at `predictionRatio`, visualizing how far the player's own `predictedTrajectory` has actually reached versus how far the slider can be dragged — an always-visible absolute-UTC-date label, and a `T+`-elapsed label that swaps itself for the same kind of inline `DurationValueInput` on click, confirmed against the *current* `lastDuration` as the clamp range rather than a fixed year), and a tick row underneath the scrubber, which describes the future span alone (each tick's text-anchor picked from its own `t` position — near-0 left-aligned, near-1 right-aligned, otherwise centered — rather than a `:first-child`/`:last-child` CSS rule, since under `'custom'` the final tick is not guaranteed to land exactly at `t = 1`). `DurationValueInput` only clamps and reports on an explicit commit (Enter, blur, or its owner's `commit()`/`openWithSec()` call) — never on every keystroke — so a value being typed can't be silently mangled mid-edit; `cancel()` (Escape, or its owner's ✕) restores nothing itself, since the caller never applied the in-progress edit to begin with. The constructor wraps the panel in its own `#hud-predict-wrap` alongside a `hud/hud-root.ts`'s `buildCollapseToggle` button (`PREDICT_TOGGLE_LABELS`) — the same collapse mechanism `hud/hud-root.ts`'s left/right rail toggles use — so the PREDICT bar itself can be folded away independently of `render`'s own `visible` field: that field is view-driven (hidden outright in combat view) while the collapse toggle's `.collapsed` class is the player's own choice within map view, and the two compose rather than overriding each other. Owned by `Game` as a private field, constructed right after `EntityManager` and before `EffectsSystem` — its constructor takes only `Ephemeris`/`EntityManager` references (not `Simulator`/`ActivePlayerController`, which `resolve`'s `simTime`/`player` arguments replace instead), early enough to be handed onward to `PlanEditor`/`ViewManager`/`FrameControls`, each constructed only after it. Shared onward two ways rather than by handing out the whole class: `plan.ts`'s narrow `DisplayDurationSource` interface (`{durationSec(referencePeriod): number}`), which this class satisfies structurally — `plan-editor.ts`/`plan-display.ts`/`plan-path.ts` hold a `DisplayDurationSource`, never a `DisplayWindowManager` (they import only the `DisplayWindow` value type from it), so the plan/ folder's PREDICT-tracking dependency is exactly "how long is one display duration," nothing else on the concrete class; and the resolved `DisplayWindow` value itself, passed as a plain argument into `PlanEditor.update`/`PlanDisplay.update`/`EntityManager.syncPlayerTrajectoryLines`/`MapPickables.refresh` each frame rather than any of those holding a `DisplayWindowManager` reference. The duration scales the future-ghost slider and, through `segmentDurationFrom`, the length of the plan's trailing segment and the range a node may be dragged across.
- `ammo.ts` — `Ammo`: the orbital resupply pickup `stage-utils/logistics.ts` spawns and the player absorbs on approach (`bcInv = SMALL_DEBRIS_BCINV`, `predictsFuture = true`, `radius = AMMO_PHYS_RADIUS`, `collides = true`). `id` is always set — the constructor takes an optional override (`CreativeStage` passes its own placement id, the `{saved}` init variant passes the saved id) and otherwise self-generates one through `entity-id.ts`'s shared `EntityIdAllocator('ammo-')` when omitted, so every spawn — including `Logistics`' auto-resupply, which never passes one — has an id a caller can key a `MapPickable`/window on without inventing one at the call site.
- `debris-piece.ts` — `DebrisPiece`/`DebrisKind` (`bcInv = SMALL_DEBRIS_BCINV`): the one class backing fragments/ejected barrels/magazine frames/casings — `DebrisKind` picks which `render/ships.ts` builder runs (`barrel`/`magazineFrame`/`casing`) or, for `fragment`, which extra `checkLoss` rule applies (only `casing` pieces expire on `CASING_LIFETIME`; the rest only via the shared any-attractor surface-contact check). `fragment` pieces get `collides = false` (pass through collision untouched, though they may still carry a nonzero `radius`); the others take the caller-supplied `radius` and `collides = true`. A `fragment`'s look is not built per instance: at construction it draws a `fragmentVariant` index (0..`DEBRIS_FRAGMENT_VARIANT_COUNT`) and a `fragmentColor` (30% chance of `COLOR_SHIP_DARK_HULL`, else the caller's `accent`) from `render/ships.ts`'s fixed, pre-baked variant geometries (`debrisFragmentResources`) — `obj` itself carries no mesh, only the transform (`obj.scale.setScalar(size)` at construction, position/attitude from `sync` same as any entity), since the geometry lives once per variant in `EntityManager`'s per-variant `InstancedPool`s and `fragmentColor` is pushed as that pool's per-instance color.
- `bullet.ts` — `Bullet extends GameEntity`: player/enemy bullets and enemy plasma bolts (`Shooter`/`BulletType`), `bcInv = BULLET_BCINV`, its own loss check (**distance from the player first — `BULLET_MAX_DIST` — with the lifetime only as a backstop**, since a runaway bullet count still costs one instance slot each even though they share a draw call), and a velocity-facing (not attitude-facing) `sync` override that uses `fo.VtoThreeV3`. `predictsFuture` stays at the `GameEntity` default of `false`, so `displayState(displayTime)` is `null` for the entire time the future-ghost slider is active — bullets simply disappear rather than showing a nonsensical projected flight path, which is fine since their lifetime is a few seconds.
- `enemy.ts` — `Enemy extends Ship`: enemy AI (`behave` — burst-fire plasma at the player using `physics/intercept.ts`'s `solveLeadTime`; takes the `EntityManager` reference to read `enemies` for the per-group attacker cap and to `addBullet`; per-group attacker cap and burst state live here), damage/destruction (`attacked`, `checkLoss`), its own `GameEntity.orbitLine` (constructed and added to `scene` in the constructor), `inertiaForEnemyKind(enemyKind)` (the one source of each `EnemyKind`'s inertia tensor, shared by `enemy-generator.ts`'s spawners and by the constructor's own `{saved}` branch), and its HUD presentation (`accentColor`, and `markerItem(role, viewerPos, pos, vel, overviewMode)` returning the `GroupedMarkerItem` the screen-marker set is built from — the enemy states what it looks like, `GroupedMarkers` decides how the set is laid out; `pos`/`vel` are caller-supplied arguments rather than `this.state`/`.v` because `targeter.ts`'s `Targeter.syncTargetMarkers` resolves them from `displayState(displayTime)` — the marker has to sit at the same future-ghost position as the enemy's own mesh, or the two visibly disagree the moment the slider moves; `overviewMode` picks `Ship.hpMarkerSvg()` in combat view or `Ship.headingHpMarkerSvg()` on the map, and `vel` is what `GroupedMarkers.sync` turns into the map-view marker's heading rotation).
- `base.ts` — `Base extends GameEntity`: a docking station placed on an orbit like any other entity — its mesh (`render/ships.ts`'s `buildBaseModel`) is built and `sync`'d through the same `GameEntity`/`EntityManager` path every other entity uses, so it is a real object in both map and combat view (`EntityManager.sync` runs unconditionally, not gated on `overviewMode`) rather than a map-only marker. Its `baseState` (`money`/`inventory: AnyPart[]`/`dockedShips: DockedShipEntry[]`) is the sole store for what the base holds — a `DockedShipEntry` snapshots a docked ship's `id`/`name`/`hp`/`maxHp` for the ship-list tab plus the `player: Player` reference `Docking`/`BaseView` use to act on the real ship (`parts` is not a snapshot — it's the same array as `player.parts`, so `hud/base-view.ts` repairs land on the ship directly and only the `hp`/`maxHp` totals need writing back). Owns its own `GameEntity.orbitLine` (`COLOR_BASE_ORBIT_LINE`, same construction/`dispose` pattern as `Enemy`'s). `dispose()` additionally destroys every ship in `dockedShips` — a docked ship sits outside `entities.players` (`EntityManager.parkPlayer`), so no per-entity sweep ever reaches it and the base it is stored in is its only owner.
- `asteroid.ts` — `Asteroid extends GameEntity`: the one class that both exerts and receives gravity through the ordinary integration path — no new orbit classification, it rides `stepActual`/`DynamicTrajectory` exactly like every other entity. The constructor derives `radius`/`collides = true` (from the caller-supplied physical radius) and, via `GameEntity.setGravitatingMass(mass)`, `mu = GRAVITATIONAL_CONSTANT * mass`, so the pull it exerts and the weight it hits with can never disagree. Optional `j2`/`c22` (both default 0, i.e. a point mass) build a `degree2` field once at construction — pole = its own `att.q`-rotated model +Y, tesseral long axis = model +Z rotated the same way, the same convention `body-orientation.ts`'s `spinOrientation` uses — rather than a getter, since `Asteroid` sits outside `Simulator.stepAttitudes`' target arrays (players/enemies/casings/debris/ammos) and so never rotates after construction; a getter would rebuild the pole vector every RK4 stage for every entity for no reason. `readonly predictsFuture = true` — an asteroid grows its own future-ghost through `Predictor` exactly like a ship, so a gravity-bearing entity's predicted trajectory is available for `predictedAttractorsAt` (`simulation/attractors.ts` below) to read from rather than needing a frozen stand-in. `bcInv`/`srpCoeff` stay at the `GameEntity` default of 0 (no drag/radiation-pressure model for a bare rock). Distinct from `game/celestial/point-field.ts`/`point-field-view.ts`'s point cloud, which is display-only and neither a `GameEntity` nor a gravity source — see that module's own note.

`src/game/simulation/` — the array that holds live entities, plus the systems that actually drive it each frame; split from `game-entity/` because these are updaters *over* the arrays, not entity classes themselves:
- `entity-id.ts` — `EntityIdAllocator`: the one implementation of "mint `${prefix}${counter}` unless a caller supplies an id to restore, in which case adopt it and advance the counter past it" — the pattern `Ammo`/`Base`/`Asteroid` all need (a restored save must keep its old id, but the counter must never hand out that id again to a fresh spawn). Each class holds its own instance keyed by its own prefix; the allocator has no cross-class state.
- `entity-manager.ts` — `EntityManager`: owns the `players`/`enemies`/`bullets`/`casings`/`debris`/`ammos`/`asteroids` arrays (capped, oldest evicted on overflow via `addXxx`/`addCapped` — `players` is uncapped instead, gated by `MAX_PLACED_SHIPS` at the `CreativeStage.placeObject` call site since removal there is deliberate, not eviction), `findPlayer(id)`/`findEnemy(id)` (the id lookups ship-menu actions, `NavTarget`'s plane resolution, and the equatorial-node source list use to turn a `MapPickable.id`/target id back into a live `Player`/`Enemy`), lifetime cleanup (`cleanup(dt, simTime, activeStage, playerPos, attractors)`/`prune`, which takes `simTime`/the player position (because `Bullet.checkLoss` culls by distance) and the frame's `Attractor[]` (because every `checkLoss` now checks surface contact against all of them) — `cleanup` runs `checkLoss` over `all()` uniformly but only `prune`s the five non-player arrays: a lost ship needs reference cleanup across nav-target/combat-target/map-picker/camera-focus/plan-editor plus a hand-off to the next living ship, none of which belongs inside the substep loop `cleanup` runs in, so `active-player-controller.ts`'s `ActivePlayerController.reclaimDead()` does that removal once, from `Game.advanceSimulation`, right after `Simulator.advance` returns), and the per-entity render sync (`sync(fo, displayTime)`, forwarding `displayTime` to each non-player `GameEntity.sync` — see `game-entity.ts`'s `displayState` above; `players` is excluded because `Player.syncPlayer` does its own richer sync, driven by `Game.sync` looping `entities.players`). **It also owns the `render/instanced-pool.ts` `InstancedPool`s that draw bullets/casings/debris fragments** (`bulletBodyPool`/`bulletHaloPool`/`plasmaPool`/`casingPool`, plus `debrisFragmentPools` — one per fragment variant, since each variant is its own geometry and one `InstancedMesh` holds only one geometry — all built once in the constructor from `render/ships.ts`'s shared geometry/material getters, `debrisFragmentResources` included — see that file above), since `Bullet`/a `casing`/`fragment` `DebrisPiece` all share one geometry/material per kind or variant (`Bullet`/`DebrisPiece`'s `addToScene = false` — see `game-entity.ts` below) and instancing needs one owner sitting above these arrays. `sync` drives them after the per-entity loop: `beginFrame()` → `push` each visible bullet/casing/fragment's `obj` → `endFrame()`; a normal (non-plasma) bullet's `obj` is a `Group` holding the body and halo meshes, so `sync` calls `obj.updateMatrixWorld()` once (cascading to both children) and pushes each child into its own pool, while a plasma bullet's `obj` is the mesh itself and goes straight into `plasmaPool`. A `fragment` `DebrisPiece` is pushed into `debrisFragmentPools[d.fragmentVariant]` with `d.fragmentColor` as the pool's per-instance color, since geometry alone can't carry the accent-vs-dark-hull color choice once it's shared across every instance of that variant. `all()` is every non-player array plus `players`; the private `otherEntities()` both `all()` and `sync()` build on top of is the one place "which arrays don't include players" is answered. `attractors()` filters `all()` down to the living, gravity-bearing (`mu !== 0`) entities and returns them structurally as `physics/attractor.ts`'s `Attractor[]` with no conversion step — `game/simulation/attractors.ts` is its sole consumer. It runs no simulation itself — `all()`/the arrays are handed out by reference to `Simulator`/`ContactPhysics` (below), and to every module that only ever reads or adds entities (`Targeter`, `NavTarget`, `Enemy.behave`, `Player.behave`→`PlayerFire`, `Stage`/`stages/`, `Logistics`, `EffectsSystem`, `NanWatchdog`). `collectionRevision` (a public getter over the private counter every add/remove/prune already bumps) is how an outside cache asks whether the roster changed — the arrays `all()`/`attractors()` hand out are rebuilt in place and keep their identity, so their reference says nothing and their length can hide an add that cancels a remove. Owned by `Game` and exposed as the public `entities` field — the one window every other module shares by reference, which is also why `Stage`'s own constructor hands `Logistics` the manager directly. **It also owns `EffectsSystem`** (the public readonly `effects` field, the flash/debris spawn window `Player`/`Enemy`/`PlayerFire`/`Stage` reach to spawn VFX or add debris — see `vfx/effects-system.ts` below) rather than `Game` holding a sibling field for it: a spawned `DebrisPiece` (fragment/casing/barrel/magazine frame) is itself an entity, so the array-holder is the natural place to own its spawn window, and nesting it here also resolves what would otherwise be a construction-order cycle — building a `Player`/`Enemy`/`Base` needs an `EffectsSystem` reference, and `EffectsSystem` needs an `EntityManager` reference (`addDebris`) to spawn into — by having the constructor build the `InstancedPool`s, then `effects` (passing itself, `this`, rather than an injected `addDebris` callback — no per-owner closures), and only then use `effects` to rebuild a snapshot's roster. **It also absorbed every per-array loop `Game` used to run over `players`/the other arrays**, so the array and the code that walks it sit together: the constructor's trailing `saved?: GameSaveData` argument is what the private `restoreFromSave(save, ...)` rebuilds `players`/`enemies`/`ammos`/`bases` from, each through its own class's constructor with a `{saved, simTime}` init. **A fresh launch builds nothing here** — the starting world is the `Stage`'s own (`stages/stage.ts`'s `addPlayer`), so this class only ever restores, and it doesn't decide who's operated either; that's `ActivePlayerController`'s own resolution, from `initialSave?.activePlayerId` against the rebuilt `entities.players`. `updatePlayers(activePlayer, input, simSpeed, dt, activeStage, ephemeris)` is the single per-frame entry point for every ship's own behavior — it loops `players` and calls `ship.behave(...)` uniformly, handing the real `input` only to `ship === activePlayer && simSpeed.canShipAct` and `null` to every other ship. **That one ternary is the whole of "which ship may be operated right now"**: being the operated ship and the warp level staying inside the physical band are the same question — a ship you cannot fly is a ship you cannot fly — so they are answered together, here, instead of being re-asked as separate `can*` gates further down inside the throttle and the gun. It also derives `simDt = dt * simSpeed.simSpeed` for the attitude clock, so `Player` never holds a `SimSpeedManager` reference of its own. `Player.behave` itself is what folds a non-operated ship's transient commands (belt/HP regen still runs unconditionally inside `behave`; every ship's thermal/radiator/power still comes from `Simulator.stepEnvironment`, active or not); `clearTransientCommands()` folds every ship's continuous commands (thrust/torque/firing/thrust latches) once a frame for as long as the warp level puts the ships out of operation; `syncPlayers(...)` drives `Player.syncPlayer` over every ship, telling each whether it's the active one; `syncPlayerTrajectoryLines(...)` syncs each ship's predicted-trajectory line and suppresses its analytic `orbitLine` wherever the prediction already supersedes it; `updateBaseEquatorNodes(overviewMode, displayWindow, ephemeris)` self-gates on `overviewMode` (combat view never shows them) and otherwise re-derives every living base's EqAN/EqDN icons; `syncEquatorNodes(cameraSystem)` is the single sync driver for *every* entity's pair, looping `all()` so a pair is reached whichever module created it (which is also why `parkPlayer` disposes and drops the parked ship's pair — out of `players` it would never be reached again, and its markers would freeze on screen); `applyVisibility(visibilityPolicy, activePlayer, overviewMode, fo, camera, attractors)` folds the class-toggle visibility policy into every player/enemy/ammo/base mesh's `obj.visible` and orbit-line display flag in one pass, including each base's own `syncOrbitLine`; `perfCounts()` is the per-array entity-count slice `Game.perfCounts()` merges in; and `dispose()` destroys every entity still held in all eight arrays and empties them, then disposes the five pool groups and `effects` — `cleanup`/`prune` only ever reach entities that already died, so an entity still alive when the world ends has no other path to its own `dispose()`.

  **The active ship is just `Game.player`'s current pointer into `players`**, not a separately-tracked identity — `players` holds every self-flown ship (a campaign stage's lone one included) on equal footing, and `active-player-controller.ts`'s `ActivePlayerController.set` only ever reassigns which element `player` (the getter it backs) points at. Nothing filters the active ship out of `players` before processing it: `Simulator.substep`/`stepAttitudes` step every element of `players` uniformly, and `stepAttitudes` in particular integrates every array (players/enemies/casings/debris/ammos alike) at the same per-substep `subDt` — there is no separate, coarser attitude step for non-player entities, `EntityManager.cleanup`'s `checkLoss` pass covers `players` the same as any other array via `all()`, and `ContactPhysics.resolveSubstep` takes `entities.all()` (`players` included) as its participant list outright. The two places that still single the active ship out do so through the explicit `player` argument rather than by excluding it from an array: `Predictor.update` spends its first budget slice on `player` before round-robining the *whole* `entities.all()` (`player` included again) — the active ship can draw budget twice in one frame, deliberately, rather than being tracked and excluded from the round-robin by identity. `EntityManager.sync` is the one place `players` is genuinely skipped wholesale, since every ship (active or not) syncs itself via `Player.syncPlayer` instead (see `player.ts` above).
- `simulator.ts` — `Simulator`: the actual simulation step. Owns **`simTime`/`lastSimDt`**, `ContactPhysics`, and takes the `EntityManager` reference in its constructor rather than holding any array itself — splitting "which entities exist" (`EntityManager`) from "how they're stepped" (`Simulator`) keeps each to one responsibility. `advance(dt, simDt, player, activeStage, simSpeed, nanWatchdog)` takes the frame's `SimSpeedManager` directly and asks it for `resolveCollision = simSpeed.canResolvePhysicalCollisions` itself — the caller never decides or simplifies this beforehand, and the substep ceiling is always `adaptiveSimulationMaxStep` regardless of that gate. `advance` splits the frame's `simDt` into substeps (count = `ceil(simDt / SUBSTEP_MAX_DT)` — depends only on the simulated time this frame, never on the real frame rate, and is deliberately uncapped so the step size the constant names actually holds at every warp level); each substep (`substep`) first calls `simulation/attractors.ts`'s `attractorsAt(ephemeris, entities, t)` **once**, at the substep's own midpoint (`simTime + dt/2`) — merging `Ephemeris.attractorsAt`'s `mu !== 0` subset with any living gravity-bearing `GameEntity` (`entities.attractors()`) into a single array — then `classifyAttractors` splits that array once into the `GRAVITY_ALWAYS_COUNT` heaviest bodies (`always`) and a `SpatialGrid` the rest are binned into (`physics/spatial-grid.ts`, cell size derived from the split — see `attractors.ts` below), and finally every live entity's own `stepActual(dt, attractorsNear(entity.state.r, classified))` reads only its own 27-neighbor cell block plus `always` (`game-entity/game-entity.ts`). Assembling the classified structure once and handing every entity a lookup against the same one (rather than each entity re-deriving its own) is a correctness requirement, not an optimization: two gravity-bearing entities integrated against structures built at different moments would see each other in slightly different positions, breaking the symmetry a mutual interaction must have. The substep ceiling itself is `adaptiveSimulationMaxStep` (`simulation/time-step.ts`) over the living **ships** alone (`entities.players`/`entities.enemies`): a ship at or descending toward `REENTRY_SUBSTEP_ALT` pulls the whole frame's ceiling down to `REENTRY_SUBSTEP_MAX_DT`, since heating and dynamic pressure are non-linear in the step size and their integrated result is what decides whether that ship survives — an outcome the player observes. Every other kind (bullets/casings/debris/ammo/bases/asteroids) is left out: reaching the atmosphere only removes it, and *when* it is removed, to what precision, changes nothing about play — surface contact itself is caught at any step size by `attractor.ts`'s swept `reachedBody`. Each substep is also cut short at the next known event boundary (`nextEventTime`), which combines two sources on deliberately different cadences: the **stage's** own `nextSimulationEventTime` is asked every substep, since a `PlanExecutor` ignition time is recomputed from the ship's current Δv and acceleration on each call and can move between substeps; the **entities'** minimum is a fixed absolute deadline per entity, so it is resolved once and held (`cachedEventTime`), re-scanned only when `simTime` reaches it, when `EntityManager.collectionRevision` moves, or when the passive-warp LOD flips. The revision is what makes that safe in the direction that matters: a *removed* entity only leaves a boundary standing where nothing happens, but an *added* one carrying an earlier deadline would make a held minimum too late and let a substep step straight over the event, so "did the roster change" has to be answered by the manager's own generation counter rather than inferred from the arrays. While that LOD is on, the entities it batches into a single trailing integration (`Bullet`/`DebrisPiece`, the set `isPassiveWarpEntity` names) are left out of the scan as well — nothing is resolved at their deadlines during those substeps, so a boundary there would subdivide the step for no one. `Simulator` only iterates the arrays; the orbit integration itself is the entity's responsibility. It also owns the attitude integration for *all* entities including every self-flown ship (`stepAttitudes`). AI/input/spawn decisions live in `game.ts`/`stages/`/`Enemy.behave`, which add entities back in via `entities.addXxx`.
- `predictor.ts` — `Predictor`: a third updater over `EntityManager`'s arrays, symmetric with `Simulator` (①holds the arrays / ②steps them / ③predicts their future — `Predictor` is ③) rather than nested inside `Simulator`, so it doesn't inherit that class's `simTime` concerns or vice versa. Owned directly by `Game` and driven from `Game.update` right after `entities.cleanup(...)` (dead entities shouldn't get predicted, and it needs the post-integration real state to resync against). `update`'s `mode` argument (`'map' | 'combat'`) decides **both** the candidate set and the frame's budget: `'map'` predicts the whole `entities.all()` on `PREDICT_STEP_BUDGET`, `'combat'` predicts the active ship alone on the much smaller `PREDICT_COMBAT_STEP_BUDGET` — combat view draws only that one line, so spending resync/RK4 on entities nothing is displaying is pure waste, while dropping the ship's own prediction entirely would lose the line the view does draw. The only state it holds is a round-robin `cursor` into `EntityManager.all()` plus the `tracked`/`finished`/`discarded` counters the load-inspection window reads (`finished` counts an entity whose prediction has either reached the horizon or been permanently truncated — a truncated list can never grow again, so counting it as unfinished would leave the readout stuck) via `Game.perfCounts()`. `update(simTime, player, horizon)` takes `horizon` from `Game`'s `displayWindowManager.current.duration` (the frame's already-resolved `DisplayWindowManager.resolve(...)` result — see `game.ts` above and `display-window-manager.ts` below — how far the future-ghost slider can currently reach). **It is not gated on time warp at all**: the budget outruns real time at every warp level — `PREDICT_MIN_STEP_DT` floors the step at `SUBSTEP_MAX_DT`, so a `PREDICT_STEP_BUDGET` slice advances the active ship 10,000 sim-s per frame (600,000 sim-s per real second at 60fps) against the 131,072 the top warp level asks for. A gate here would stop growth while `discardPredictionIfDiverged` kept discarding, leaving the list permanently absent for that warp level and silently falling back to `OrbitLine`'s analytic ellipse, whose radius disagrees with the integrated trajectory under J2/third-body/drag. It first calls `discardPredictionIfDiverged` on every **predicting** entity in `all()` (the active ship included, once, since `all()` doesn't repeat it) and tallies the counters — cheap (one binary search each); `predictsFuture` is checked before the call, so a bullet/casing/fragment — the bulk of `all()` in map mode — costs nothing at all here. The `Attractor[]` that pass hands each entity is `attractorsNearInto(entity.state.r, …)` off a single `classifyAttractors` built once at the top of the frame, so the divergence tolerance's center/period lookup reads the `always` set plus that position's own grid neighbourhood instead of the whole registry; it narrows into a scratch array of its own, separate from `advanceBudget`'s. Budget spending then spends at most `frameBudget × PREDICT_PLAYER_BUDGET_RATIO` (=0.5) on `player` explicitly first, then one pass through `all()` starting at `cursor`, saving wherever it stopped as next frame's `cursor` — the round-robin **skips the active ship** (`e !== player`), since it was already served by that first slice and would otherwise draw budget twice in one frame. Each entity in that pass gets an even share of what is left — `max(PREDICT_MIN_ENTITY_STEPS, floor(budget / remaining visits))`, itself capped by the remaining budget — and whatever it doesn't spend rolls on to the next one. Handing one entity the whole remainder is what produces a discard/regrow loop: an entity served fewer steps than it takes to push its first `history` sample has an `at(t)` that answers `null` almost everywhere, so the next frame's divergence check discards the list and the work restarts from scratch. `PREDICT_MIN_ENTITY_STEPS` (=16) is the floor that prevents it, sized above the ~10 steps one sample interval costs at the 28-day preset in LEO. The player's ratio cap is skipped entirely when `all()` holds nobody but the ship itself: there is no one left to spend the remainder on, so it gets the whole frame budget. The ratio cap is what keeps the priority from becoming an exclusive claim: a prediction is also read as a gravity source (`predictedAttractorsAt`) and a collision partner, so letting the ship's own unfinished prediction consume the whole budget would make a planned trajectory's shape depend on how far the ship's prediction happened to have grown. The private `advanceBudget(entity, budgetSteps, simTime, horizon)` returns 0 immediately for an entity with `predictsFuture === false`, so bullets/casings/debris never touch either attractor lookup here at all. For a predicting entity each iteration reads the entity's current predicted-tip state (`entity.predictedTrajectory?.state ?? entity.state`) and, whenever the tip has advanced `ATTRACTOR_REBUILD_SEC` (=3600 s) past the last classification, re-derives that tip's own gravity sources — `simulation/attractors.ts`'s `predictedAttractorsAt(ephemeris, entities, tipState.t)`, which evaluates every other gravity-bearing `GameEntity`'s own `displayState(tipState.t)` at that same tip time rather than freezing it at its current real state (a body whose prediction hasn't reached that time yet — `displayState` returns `null` — is simply dropped from that iteration's array, not substituted with a stale position), then `classifyAttractors`, reusing that one classification for the intervening steps the same way `Simulator.substep` builds one per substep and hands it to every entity (an hour's worth of stale body positions is smaller than the RK4 error the prediction's own step size already carries, and holding it to a real duration rather than a step count keeps the staleness bounded when a year-scale horizon coarsens the step size), and reads `attractorsNear` off it per step — and sizes `dt` as `min(horizon, max(PREDICT_MIN_STEP_DT, localOrbitPeriod(tip.r, attractors) / PREDICT_STEPS_PER_REV, horizon / PREDICT_MAX_STEPS))` (`attractor.ts`'s `localOrbitPeriod`, `PREDICT_STEPS_PER_REV` = 600 — the period around whichever body pulls hardest at the tip, divided into a fixed step count per revolution, the same scheme `plan-arc.ts`'s `stepDt` uses with its own `PLAN_ARC_STEPS_PER_REV`; the `horizon / PREDICT_MAX_STEPS` term only bites once `horizon` grows large; the outer `min(horizon, …)` caps a single step at the whole display window, since the tip is designed to stop at or past the horizon and an uncapped local period would otherwise throw it beyond the ephemeris pack's validity range), and calls `GameEntity.stepPredicted(attractors, simTime, dt, horizon)` — looping until either the budget runs out or a call returns `false` (horizon reached or truncated). Both the step-count loop and the per-step `dt`/`attractors` sizing are `Predictor`'s own, not `GameEntity`'s, matching how `Simulator.advance` decides `subDt` and owns the substep-count loop around `GameEntity.stepActual` rather than leaving either to the entity. It never asks *why* a list is short (freshly spawned vs. just invalidated) — that indifference is what keeps discards from ever spiking frame time, since a discarded list just competes for the same budget as a new one next frame.
  **The active ship is just `Game.player`'s current pointer into `players`**, not a separately-tracked identity — `players` holds every self-flown ship (a campaign stage's lone one included) on equal footing, and `active-player-controller.ts`'s `ActivePlayerController.set` only ever reassigns which element `player` (the getter it backs) points at. Nothing filters the active ship out of `players` before processing it: `Simulator.substep`/`stepAttitudes` step every element of `players` uniformly, and `stepAttitudes` in particular integrates every array (players/enemies/casings/debris/ammos alike) at the same per-substep `subDt` — there is no separate, coarser attitude step for non-player entities, `EntityManager.cleanup`'s `checkLoss` pass covers `players` the same as any other array via `all()`, and `ContactPhysics.resolveSubstep` takes `entities.all()` (`players` included) as its participant list outright. The two places that still single the active ship out do so through the explicit `player` argument rather than by excluding it from an array: `Predictor.update` spends its first budget slice on `player` before round-robining the *whole* `entities.all()` (`player` included again) — the active ship can draw budget twice in one frame, deliberately, rather than being tracked and excluded from the round-robin by identity. `EntityManager.sync` is the one place `players` is genuinely skipped wholesale, since every ship (active or not) syncs itself via `Player.syncPlayer` instead (see `player.ts` above).
- `contact.ts` — `ContactPhysics`: rigid-sphere contact enumeration and resolution for every `collides` `GameEntity` plus the infinite-mass celestial bodies. `resolveSubstep(simTime, entities, attractors, activeStage)` runs once per substep (the warp gate `SimSpeedManager.canResolvePhysicalCollisions` is checked by `Simulator.advance` before calling, not here); `resolveBelt(dt, ...)` is separate because the belt is a ship-local simulation solved at the real frame `dt`, so it is resolved once per frame outside the substep loop. Candidate pairs are narrowed through one `physics/spatial-grid.ts` `SpatialGrid` per call — the same 27-neighbor pattern `simulation/attractors.ts` uses for gravity, applied to contact instead. **Its cell size is measured against the participants' *relative* displacement, not their absolute one**: what decides whether a pair touches is how they move with respect to each other, and in ECI every participant carries the same orbital velocity — kilometres per substep — on top of that. Subtracting the mean displacement Δ̄ over the call's participants leaves each one's reach at `radius + |Δ − Δ̄|`, and twice the largest such reach is the cell edge, since a pair that touches within the interval must end it no further apart than `radius sum + |Δa − Δ̄| + |Δb − Δ̄|`. Taking the raw displacement instead sizes every cell to the shared orbital motion, which no pair's contact depends on, and collapses the whole participant set into a handful of cells. `C.CONTACT_GRID_CELL_SIZE_FLOOR` (1 m) only guards the degenerate case where every radius and every relative displacement is zero — nothing can touch at all then, so any positive edge is correct, and the grid holds no empty cells, so a small one costs nothing. Within one substep the candidate pairs are enumerated **once** (`collectCandidates`, which is also where each pair's `contactsWith` filter runs), and a pair that does not currently touch is kept as a candidate with a `null` response rather than dropped — the parties' states change as earlier contacts resolve, so a pair that misses now may touch later. `earliestContact` then makes one pass over that array per resolution, picking the lowest-TOI unresolved candidate and re-deriving the response only for the candidates involving whichever two parties the previous resolution actually moved; a resolved candidate carries its own `resolved` flag. Any participant whose position/velocity/radius/mass is non-finite (or whose mass is not positive) is dropped before it can corrupt a grid cell index. **It resolves the impulse and hands both parties a `Contact`, and nothing else** — damage, sound, destruction and effects are each `GameEntity` subclass's own `collideWith`, so a bullet's damage and a hull's reaction are written on their own sides rather than branched on here. `Contact` is stated from the receiver's point of view (`self`/`other` swapped and `normal` flipped per side), so one resolution yields both parties' descriptions. `BeltSection` (the belt's contact proxy) is defined in `player/belt-physics.ts` and `RadiatorFold` in `player/radiator.ts`, not here. An `Asteroid` is not special-cased — it takes part on exactly the same footing as an `Enemy` or a pickup, and whether an object also pulls gravitationally (`mu`) plays no role in whether or how it bounces.
- `attractors.ts` — the gravity source array for one simulation/prediction/plan step, and the spatial classification that keeps summing it cheap. `mergeAttractors(bodies, dynamic)` is the one array-spread primitive (never mutating the `Ephemeris` ring-cache array it read from) the rest of the module and `plan-arc.ts` build on. `gravityBodiesAt(ephemeris, t)` narrows `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(t)` (the full analytic-body window) down to `mu !== 0` — a body's gravity-source status is decided by this one filter, nowhere else. `attractorsAt(ephemeris, entities, t)` merges that with `EntityManager.attractors()` (the living gravity-bearing `GameEntity`s, most notably `Asteroid`); `Simulator.substep` calls it once per substep, at the substep's own midpoint, and hands the one resulting array to every entity in that substep, so two gravity-bearing entities integrated together see each other at the same instant — computing it separately per entity would let their mutual pull disagree by whichever one moved first. `predictedAttractorsAt(ephemeris, entities, t)` is `Predictor`'s own sibling: it re-derives each dynamic entity's state at `t` via `displayState(t)` rather than reusing its current real state, dropping any entity whose prediction hasn't reached `t` yet — evaluating at the tip's own moment is the correctness requirement here, since a prediction budget pass has no shared "this substep" instant to synchronize dynamic bodies against the way `Simulator` does. A planned trajectory's own sources are `plan/plan-attractors.ts`'s (below), not this module's — this module knows nothing about plans.

  `classifyAttractors(attractors)` sorts one such array into `{always, grid}`: the `GRAVITY_ALWAYS_COUNT` heaviest bodies by `μ` go into `always`, the rest are inserted into a `physics/spatial-grid.ts` `SpatialGrid` keyed on their own position — a value comparison made once per body per step, not once per (body, query point) pair. `attractorsNear(pos, classified)` is what every entity actually integrates against: `always` plus whatever `SpatialGrid.neighbors(pos)` returns for `pos`'s own cell and its 26 neighbors — a body sorted into the grid contributes only to query points within roughly one cell of it, which is safe because a query point farther than that has an even smaller ECI origin-correction term to lose (see `spatial-grid.ts`'s own comment).

  **Both the `μ` cut and the cell size are derived from the array itself each call** (module-private `alwaysThresholdMu`/`gridCellSize`), so the two tuning constants are a *count* and an *accuracy*, not two lengths: `GRAVITY_ALWAYS_COUNT` fixes how many bodies every entity sums unconditionally, and the cell size follows as `sqrt(heaviest gridded μ / GRAVITY_NEGLIGIBLE_ACCEL)` — the distance at which that body's pull has fallen to the tolerance. The approximation rests on mass in a star system being Zipf-distributed: almost all of it sits in the star and a handful of bodies, so a cut a few ranks deep leaves a remainder small enough that its reach is short. The threshold is a `μ` **value** rather than a rank so equal-`μ` bodies (a field of identical rocks) can never be split arbitrarily by sort order; the count may therefore exceed the target on ties. Because `μ` is constant per body, the `always` set is stable frame to frame.

  Two constraints on the count. **It must cover the perturbations the game depends on** — under the default registry the Moon is only the 14th heaviest body (the Galilean moons and Titan all outweigh it), so a count below that drops the lunar term for ships outside cislunar space; at 15 there is one rank of margin. And **the `μ` order is not the order of importance**: a body's own contribution splits into a direct pull `μ/d²` and the ECI origin correction `μ/D²` (`D` = its distance from the origin body), the latter being *independent of the query point* — the Moon's is 3.8e-5 m/s², the largest non-central term in the system, which is why it must never be gridded. Any criterion keyed on the **entity's** distance from the origin is forbidden however tempting the pruning: the simulation must behave the same wherever the origin body sits, floating-point error aside. Every gravity-integration call site (`Simulator.substep`, `Predictor.advanceBudget`, `PlanArc`'s private `integrateTo`'s sizing and stepping evaluations) runs its own `classifyAttractors` → `attractorsNear` pair rather than sharing a `ClassifiedAttractors`, since each call site's array (and often its evaluation time) differs.

`src/game/camera/` — every camera holds a `viewpoint: Viewpoint` field (`physics/projection.ts` — `position`/`lookTarget`/`up`/`fovDeg`/`aspect`) and an `update` that computes the viewpoint in absolute ECI (THREE-free) and writes it into `viewpoint` — no camera returns its `Viewpoint` as a value, since a function that both mutates state and hands back a derived product is the anti-pattern CLAUDE.md's TypeScript rules ban. Pushing `viewpoint` into a `THREE.PerspectiveCamera` through the floating origin (`sync`) and building the `ProjectFn` the rest of the game reads screen positions through (independent of both the THREE matrices and `fo`) are **`CameraSystem`'s job alone** — no sub-camera has its own `sync`/`projection`. Each sub-camera also exposes a `near: number` getter (a plain derived-value read, not a THREE write, so it doesn't cross that boundary) — `CameraSystem.sync` reads the active one and is the only place it reaches a `THREE.PerspectiveCamera.near`.
- `camera-system.ts` — `CameraSystem`: owns `CombatCameraSystem`/`OverviewCamera` and exposes the active one (`activeCamera`/`activeCameraPos`/`activeCameraProjection`); owns `overviewMode` (`zoomActive` is a forwarding getter — `!overviewMode && combatCamera.zoomActive` — since only `CombatCameraSystem` holds and updates that state). Module-private `syncCameraToViewpoint(camera, view, near, fo)` and `projectionFromViewpoint(view)` are the sole place any sub-camera's `viewpoint` becomes a `THREE.PerspectiveCamera` update or a `ProjectFn` — `sync(fo)` calls the former on whichever of `combatCamera`/`overviewCamera` is active, and `activeCameraProjection` calls the latter. `ScaleFn` (`(worldPos: Vec3) => number`, metres per screen pixel at that point) is the exact sibling of `ProjectFn` — module-private `scaleFromViewpoint(view)` binds `physics/projection.ts`'s `metersPerPixel` the same way, and `activeCameraScale` picks the active camera's viewpoint the same way, so a consumer that needs to size something in screen units receives a bound function as an argument rather than reaching for the camera. `update(player, simTime, input, dt, mapPickables)` also reads the roll keys (`K.cameraRollLeft`/`cameraRollRight`, mapped to the numeric-keypad `Numpad0`/`Numpad1` — distinct from the ship radiator toggles on the top-row `Digit9`/`Digit0`) and forwards `keyRoll` alongside `keyYaw`/`keyPitch` to whichever sub-camera is active. `mapPickables` (built fresh each frame by `MapPickables.refresh()`) is threaded through to `OverviewCamera.update` so its focus resolves against the same candidate set `MapContextActions.handleRightClick` picks against — `CameraSystem` itself holds none of that set. Also owns the map focus concern (= "where the overview camera looks"): the `focusMarkers` label set — resolving a right-click against the shared candidate set is `MapContextActions.handleRightClick`'s job via `map-pickable.ts`'s `pickNearest` directly, so this class no longer wraps that lookup itself — and the **表示 panel** (`hud/view-options-panel.ts`'s `ViewOptionsPanel`, exposed as the public readonly `viewOptionsPanel` — two toggle sections: 天体クラス別 (the per-class `BodyClassToggles` icon/label/orbit-line buttons, `BODY_CLASS_ROWS` then `ENTITY_ROWS`) and 天球グリッド (`Navball`'s `CelestialGridVisibility` icon/label/... rows, wired by `Navball`'s own constructor rather than by this class — see `navball.ts` below), so there is one "what does the map show" panel rather than two; foldable via a `hud/hud-root.ts` `buildCollapseToggle` on its own heading (`#hud-view-options-toggle`, the same collapse mechanism the object list/PREDICT bar use — see `hud/hud-root.ts` below); no camera-frame or focus control of its own — origin/rotation for both the camera and the planned trajectory are `hud/frame-controls.ts`'s job, and focus is chosen via `MapContextActions`'s always-shown object list, a double-click, or a property window's "move focus" item). Every control on the 天体クラス別 section writes `overviewCamera`/`bodyClassToggles` state and nothing else, so the whole HUD wiring for that section stays inside this class; `sync(fo)` pushes the panel's visibility and highlights each frame. **That panel and the focus labels are the only DOM this class touches** — every other HUD panel's visibility belongs to that panel's own owner (`Hud`'s six panel classes — `statusPanel` for `#hud-status`, `orbitPanel` for `#hud-orbit`, `contactsPanel` for `#hud-enemies`, `targetPanel` for `#hud-target`, see `hud/hud.ts` below — `Stage`'s `StageStatusPanel` for `#hud-stagestatus`, CSS for what is purely view-driven), so no element ever has a second writer overwriting the first later in the same frame. `bodyClassToggles`' initial value is likewise read from `localStorage` (`tepui.bodyClassToggles`, `loadBodyClassToggles`) and written back on every toggle (`saveBodyClassToggles`), so it too survives a page reload alongside `Navball.gridVisibility`. The class-toggle section is one row per category (`BODY_CLASS_ROWS`: 惑星/衛星/準惑星/小天体/ラグランジュ点, then `ENTITY_ROWS`: 自艦/敵/弾薬/基地), each holding a `Button` per category's icon(●)/label(Aa)/orbit-line(⌒) `BodyClassToggles` field (the widget itself holds no on/off state — the row mirrors `BodyClassToggles` locally and calls `setOn` back, per `hud/widgets/`'s state-lives-with-the-caller rule) — orbit-line is omitted for 衛星 (no such toggle exists, see `body-visibility.ts` below) and for ラグランジュ点 (no orbit concept at all), so those two rows show only two buttons. `serialize()` mirrors `CombatCameraSystem`/`OverviewCamera`'s own `serialize()`, so a snapshot carries both sub-cameras' viewpoints regardless of which is active; the constructor's optional trailing `saved?: Pick<CameraSaveData, 'chase' | 'overview'>` argument forwards `saved?.chase`/`saved?.overview` straight into `new CombatCameraSystem(...)`/`new OverviewCamera(...)` — `game.ts` passes `initialSave?.camera` here, after `ViewManager`'s own initial view has already been resolved from that same `initialSave?.camera?.view`. `dispose()` takes the 表示 panel down and, less obviously, detaches the `pointerdown` handler this class binds to `#hud-chase-reset` — that button belongs to `buildHudDom` and outlives any one `CameraSystem`, so a handler left attached would still be there, and fire, alongside the next one's.
- `combat-camera-system.ts` — `CombatCameraSystem`: the combat view, holding its own `camera: THREE.PerspectiveCamera` and `viewpoint: Viewpoint` (the same `Viewpoint` shape `OverviewCamera` holds, so there's no second "camera view state" shape in the codebase). Owns the `[Z]` gunsight switch (`zoomActive`, read from `Input` each `update` — the key is only ever consulted while combat view is active, since `CameraSystem` doesn't call this class's `update` in map mode) and is the sole place FOV animates: each frame it picks `chase-camera.ts`'s `ChaseCamera` or `gunsight-camera.ts`'s `GunsightCamera` by `zoomActive`, calls that sub-camera's `update` (which writes its own `viewpoint`), then module-private `lerpViewpointFov` folds the picked `viewpoint` into this class's own — position/lookTarget/up/aspect snap to the target immediately, only `fovDeg` eases exponentially — so a sub-camera never animates its own FOV and a future third view only needs to expose the same `viewpoint: Viewpoint` field. `toggleFollowAttitude`/`camFollowAttitude` are thin forwards to `ChaseCamera` (the `[G]` key's receiver, same pattern as `reset()`) — the reference-frame decision itself lives there.
- `chase-camera.ts` — `ChaseCamera`: the third-person orbit view, owning only a unit quaternion `rot` and `dist` — no Euler yaw/pitch/up-vector state, so a long drag session can't gimbal-lock or drift into an inconsistent up vector. It holds **no target reference of its own**: `update(mouse, keyYaw, keyPitch, keyRoll, dt, target: GameEntity | null)` and `toggleFollowAttitude(target: GameEntity | null)` both take the entity to track as a per-call argument (the same shape `GunsightCamera.update` already used) rather than a stored reference, so switching the flown ship touches neither `rot` nor `dist` — the camera's current offset carries over instead of snapping to a default view, and only which entity the next call reads changes. `update` reads only `target.state.r`/`target.att.q` — never `alive` — and, with `target === null` (no ship operated), returns immediately (`if (!target) return;`), leaving `viewpoint` exactly where it last was — the camera freezes at that moment rather than resetting or tracking anything else; `toggleFollowAttitude` likewise does nothing with `target === null`, since there is nothing to reinterpret `rot` against. The togglable reference frame (`camFollowAttitude`, a read-only getter) decides what `rot` *means*: `true` — the default — makes it an offset relative to the target's own attitude (`target.att.q`); `false` makes it the camera's own absolute orientation in ECI, fixed in inertial space until dragged. `toggleFollowAttitude(target)` is the sole place that flips it, reinterpreting `rot` through `target.att.q` (multiply/divide) so the rendered view never jumps at the moment of the toggle. `update(...)` folds `target.att.q` into a working quaternion whenever `camFollowAttitude` holds, then applies key/drag rotations to that working quaternion before writing it back. Mouse drag rotates around `axis = normalize(cross(dragVector, viewVector))` (`viewVector` = the camera's current look direction, `dragVector` = the drag's screen delta expressed in the camera's own current right/up axes): the axis is always ⟂ the view direction by construction, so drag can never introduce roll around the view axis, and using the *current* basis rather than a fixed reference keeps the control gimbal-lock-free at any pitch. Arrow-key yaw/pitch rotate around the same current up/right axes; `keyRoll` (the numpad-0/1 keys) rotates around `view` itself (the camera's own look direction), the one axis arrow-key yaw/pitch never touch. `serialize()` writes `rot`/`dist`/`panEci`/`camFollowAttitude` into a `ChaseCameraSaveData`; the constructor's optional trailing `saved?: ChaseCameraSaveData` argument assigns `rot`/`_camFollowAttitude` directly rather than through `toggleFollowAttitude`, since that method's read-normalize is for a *live* frame switch and would reinterpret a value that is already correct for the saved frame.
- `gunsight-camera.ts` — `GunsightCamera`: the `[Z]` zoom view — stateless apart from `viewpoint`, just the ship's nose direction and position turned into a `Viewpoint` each `update`.
- `overview-camera.ts` — `OverviewCamera`: the map Earth-centered orbit camera (near=`dist / OVERVIEW_CAMERA_NEAR_RATIO` clamped so it never grows past the star/celestial-grid shell at extreme zoom-out, so the near plane tightens as the camera zooms in rather than clipping a close focus at a fixed distance. The clamp is not a flat fraction of `CELESTIAL_SHELL_RADIUS`: a shell point at view-axis angle θ from screen center clips at view-space depth `R·cosθ`, so the cap has to be `R·cos(θ_diag)·OVERVIEW_CAMERA_NEAR_SHELL_MARGIN`, θ_diag being the screen's diagonal half-FOV derived from `fov`/`window.innerWidth/innerHeight` each call — a flat `R`-fraction cap only protects the narrow region near screen center and leaves the wide-aspect edges/corners clipped/far=`dist × OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_RATIO` clamped to `[OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_MIN, OVERVIEW_CAMERA_FAR_MAX]`, so the far plane also tracks zoom instead of sitting at a fixed distance), exposing `camera`/`viewpoint` on the same footing as `CombatCameraSystem`. Each sub-camera (this one and `CombatCameraSystem`) exposes its own `far` getter — the combat camera's is the fixed `COMBAT_CAMERA_FAR` — and `CameraSystem.sync` writes the active one's `far` into the `THREE.PerspectiveCamera` every frame, the same way it already does for `near`. Its source of truth is three **`cameraFrame`-relative** vectors (`offset_r` = focus→camera, `pan_r` = focus→look-target, `up_r` = the camera's up direction), so switching to a rotating frame makes the viewpoint co-rotate for free; the ECI ⇄ frame round trip is delegated to `physics/frame.ts` and the class never brands values itself. `cameraFrame` is a read-only getter for **the frame the viewpoint is fixed to** — the frame the planned trajectory (and every other future display) is *drawn* in is `DisplayWindowManager.frame`, a separate value selected independently — and `setCameraRotation(rotatingWith: OrbitingId | null)` is the sole way to change it: it always resolves to `ephemeris.frameOf(ephemeris.originId, rotatingWith)`, keeping the frame's *center* pinned to `ephemeris.originId` regardless of `rotatingWith` (the three basis vectors are directions, not points, so only the rotation ever affects the viewpoint) and re-projects the current view into the new frame so the switch never jumps the visible camera. `update` rotates the viewpoint around the camera's *current* up/right axes (the same construction `ChaseCamera` uses) rather than around world Y, so a rolled map still yaws and pitches the way the drag looks on screen, and it re-orthogonalizes `up_r` against the just-changed view direction before applying `keyRoll` around it. Focus is a `camera/focus-target.ts` `FocusTarget` — either `{kind:'object', id}`, re-resolved every frame against the `candidates: readonly MapPickable[]` argument `update` receives (built by `MapPickables.refresh()` — Earth/Moon/Sun/Lagrange-point labels plus every living ship plus the navigation-target AN/DN icons), falling back to Earth once the id is missing for two consecutive frames; or `{kind:'point', frame, point}`, a `physics/frame.ts` `FramePoint` baked into a chosen `ReferenceFrame` and resolved back to ECI every frame via `frameTransformAt` — a rotating `frame` makes the fixed point ride that frame's own rotation instead of sitting still in ECI. `setFocusTarget(target, resetPan=true)` is the sole entry point for changing focus (replacing separate id/position setters), and `resolvedFocus` exposes the ECI position `resolveFocus` last settled on — `frame-controls.ts`'s "固定を解除" action reads it to bake a fresh `'point'` target in the star-centered inertial frame (or `ephemeris.inertialFrame` when the registry has no star) at the instant of release, so the camera keeps looking exactly where it was rather than jumping to Earth. `clearFocusIf(id)` only matches an `'object'` target, since a `'point'` target carries no id an object's disappearance could invalidate. The class holds no reference to `FocusMarkers` or any other pickable source itself. The future-ghost slider belongs to `DisplayWindowManager`. `serialize()` writes `offset_r`/`pan_r`/`up_r`, `cameraFrame.rotatingWith`, and `focus` into an `OverviewCameraSaveData` — a `'point'` focus is saved as its baking `ReferenceFrame`'s `center`/`rotatingWith` plus the frame-relative point; the constructor's optional trailing `saved?: OverviewCameraSaveData` argument resolves that frame (and `cameraFrame` itself) through `ephemeris.frameOf` rather than a literal, per `frame.ts`'s reference-identity rule, and with no `saved` builds the default look-down viewpoint at `ephemeris.inertialFrame` instead.
- `focus-markers.ts` — `FocusMarkers`: the focusable-target labels, each exposed as a `MapPickable` (`kind: 'body'`). The full label set is built once in the constructor from the active registry, **in hierarchical order** (a body, then its Lagrange labels, then its children — each carrying a `depth` so a consumer can indent without re-deriving the tree; `ObjectListPanel` uses exactly this) — one label per body, plus Lagrange-point labels for the bodies whose points are **dynamically meaningful**: `Ephemeris.hasUsableCollinearPoints` (L1/L2/L3, gated on the L1 clearance from the secondary's own surface exceeding `LAGRANGE_MIN_CLEARANCE_RATIO`) and `Ephemeris.hasStableTriangularPoints` (L4/L5, Routh's linear-stability mass ratio) are asked per body, so a system can legitimately get L4/L5 but not L1/L2/L3 — except for `body-class.ts`'s `'smallBody'`/`'dwarf'` classes, which cap out at L1/L2 regardless (too numerous for L3/L4/L5 to stay legible, and neither dynamically as interesting for these low-mass bodies). A Lagrange label carries two names, since the two readers want different forms: `name` (`地球-L1`, the secondary body's display name then the point) is what the object list and the property window read, so a point sorts and reads under its own body; `markerLabel` (`L1` over `地球`, drawn as two lines — `mk-lagrange` in `skeleton-style.ts` sizes/stacks them) is what the map marker draws, where the point itself is the thing being pointed at and the body is context. Both come from the module-private `lagrangeName`/`lagrangeMarkerLabel`, so registering a new body needs no change here. Every ordinary body's two names are the same string. Split across the two phases like `NavTarget` is: `update(t, focusId, toggles, cameraPos)` (`focusId: AttractorId | undefined` — `undefined` when the camera is fixed on a `'point'` `FocusTarget` rather than a body, in which case the policy falls back to its focus-independent rules) builds a `celestial/map-visibility.ts` `MapVisibilityPolicy` for this frame (from the registry, the toggles, the focus, and `systemMembersAt(cameraPos)` — the system the camera is physically in, so leaving a focus doesn't blank out the satellites right in front of it) and re-derives positions **only for the bodies whose `body(id).pickable` holds** — an invisible body costs no `positionOf`/`lagrangeAt` at all, which is what keeps the per-frame cost bounded as the registry grows — stamping each shown `FocusLabel`'s `showIcon`/`showLabel` from that same policy. `syncLabels(project, cameraPos)` places the markers off the subset `update()` just computed, passing `mk-poi`'s `sym`/`label` arguments as `''` for whichever of `showIcon`/`showLabel` is off (so the dot and the name can appear independently), hiding a label the previous frame showed but this one dropped, hiding one `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body, and resolving screen-space label crowding by `labelPriority` (planets/stars > dwarfs > satellites/small bodies > Lagrange points) over a uniform grid of `FOCUS_LABEL_PRIORITY_PX` cells, so label count doesn't cost O(N²). Crowding suppresses the **name only** — the icon stays, or the icon/label toggle split and the "this focus target exists" signal would both break. Each pass writes back the label's own `pickable`: an occluded label is unpickable outright, a name lost to crowding still is (its icon is on screen). `CameraSystem` calls the two back to back so the drawn markers are the same frame's coordinates. `allLabels` is exposed publicly too (the same array, id/isLagrange/name/depth) — `MapContextActions.sync()`'s parent-lookup construction reads it directly rather than the shown subset, since a row hidden on the map by a toggle must still nest correctly in the 軌道オブジェクト一覧's tree. `bodyPickables(t, visibility)` is the candidate-list half: it returns the bodies and dynamically-meaningful Lagrange points the *same* `MapVisibilityPolicy` admits (so a body the toggles have hidden is genuinely unselectable, not merely undrawn), each carrying the previous `syncLabels`' `pickable` flag — a target that was drawn nowhere this frame (occluded, or its name lost to crowding) is still returned, marked `pickable: false`, since it hasn't been turned off, it just isn't on screen to grab. An overview-camera-side concern, owned by `CameraSystem`.
- `focus-target.ts` — `FocusTarget`: `OverviewCamera`'s focus as a judged union, `{kind:'object', id}` (a body/ship/other `MapPickable`, re-resolved by id every frame) or `{kind:'point', frame, point}` (an ECI position baked once into a `physics/frame.ts` `FramePoint` within a chosen `ReferenceFrame`, re-resolved to ECI every frame via `frameTransformAt` so a rotating `frame` carries the point along). `focusTargetId(target)` is the sole way to ask "does this focus correspond to a body" — `undefined` for `'point'` — used everywhere a focus id feeds a body-keyed lookup (`MapVisibilityPolicy`, `EnvironmentScene`'s reference-line focus, `ObjectListPanel`'s row highlight). `focusPoint(ephemeris, frame, pos, t)` builds a `'point'` target from a live ECI position at time `t`; `frame-controls.ts`'s "固定を解除" action is its only caller.

Screen projection itself is **not** in `camera/`: `physics/projection.ts` holds the pure math (`Viewpoint`/`projectToNdc`/`ndcToScreen`/`Projected`) and each camera exposes a bound `ProjectFn` (`(worldPos: Vec3) => Projected`) that consumers receive as an argument rather than importing.
