- `src/game/map-pickable.ts` — `MapPickable`/`MapPickKind` (`'body' | 'ship' | 'player' | 'apsis' | 'relnode' | 'ammo' | 'empty-space' | 'eqnode' | 'base'`) and `pickNearest<T extends { readonly pos: Vec3 }>`: the common shape for anything on the map (or in combat) that a click can hit, and the shared "project every candidate, take the screen-nearest one within a pixel-radius" search. The type parameter only requires a world position, not the full `MapPickable` shape, so a caller with its own payload (e.g. `Targeter.pickTargetAt`'s `{ pos, target }`) can pass it straight through without faking the rest of `MapPickable`. `'body'` is `FocusMarkers`' celestial labels, `'ship'` is every living `Enemy`, `'player'` is every living self-flown ship in `entities.players` — split from `'ship'` because a self-flown ship's menu offers activate/follow-plan/delete on top of what an enemy's does — `'relnode'` is `NavTarget`'s relative AN/DN icons, and `'apsis'` is `PlanDisplay`'s periapsis/apoapsis icons — each kind drives a different `MapContextActions.itemsFor` item set. Used by `map-pickables.ts`'s `MapPickables` (which builds the frame's candidate set) and `map-context-actions.ts`'s `MapContextActions` (the map's whole right-click path, and — via `handleCombatRightClick` — the combat-view one too) and `Targeter.pickTargetAt` (combat targets only, `[T]`-key selection), so target-selection and map-focus/property-window picking share one implementation instead of several near-identical screen-distance loops.
  A second `DynamicTrajectory` — `predictedTrajectory` — is the entity's own future trajectory, driven incrementally by `game/simulation/`'s `Predictor` (below) rather than recomputed from scratch each frame: `stepPredicted(attractors, simTime, dt, horizon)` lazily creates it from `actualTrajectory.state` if absent, then advances its tip by exactly the caller-supplied `dt` (one RK4 step) against the caller-supplied `attractors` and returns whether it stepped. Sizing `dt`, looking up `attractors` for that tip, and deciding `horizon` are all `Predictor`'s job, not this method's — the same split as `Simulator` deciding `subDt` before calling `stepActual`. It refuses the step (returns `false`) once `predictedTrajectory.state.t` already reaches `simTime + horizon` — so the tip always ends up at or past the horizon rather than short of it, which is what lets `at(t)` answer exactly `simTime + horizon` directly or by interpolation instead of needing a clamped final step (clamping would make the prediction's resolution silently depend on whether the tip happened to have caught up to the horizon yet). It permanently stops (sets `truncated`, cleared only when a fresh `predictedTrajectory` is created) if the integrated position sinks into any body's surface (`attractor.ts`'s `hitCelestialBody`, margin `REENTRY_ALT`) or goes non-finite — without that, a re-entering or NaN-corrupted entity would burn its budget forever without ever reaching the horizon. `discardPredictionIfDiverged(simTime, attractors)` is the cheap per-frame correctness check, returning whether it discarded: if `predictedTrajectory.at(simTime)` is absent or has drifted from `actualTrajectory.state.r` by more than the tolerance, it calls `invalidatePrediction()` (drops `predictedTrajectory` — a zero-cost operation; the list simply regrows through the normal budget next frame, which is why discards never spike frame time). The tolerance is **not** a flat `PREDICT_RESET_DIST`: `divergenceTolerance` widens it to whatever `at()`'s own cubic-Hermite interpolation error is at the list's current sample spacing (`PREDICT_SAMPLE_ERROR` scaled by the fourth power of how much that spacing has coarsened past `TRAJECTORY_SAMPLES_PER_REV`). Without that, a long display duration decimates the list far enough that a *correct* prediction reads as drifted — at the 28-day preset the interpolation alone is tens of km against a 500 m threshold — and every entity would discard and restart its list every single frame, so the prediction could never finish growing. **The spacing it scales by is the list's own recorded `DynamicTrajectory.sampleInterval`, not one re-derived from the current display duration**: the samples already in the list were decimated at whatever interval was in force when they were pushed, and nothing invalidates a list when the duration changes — deriving the threshold from the current setting instead would, on a switch from a long preset to a short one, shrink the threshold to metres against a list that is legitimately tens of km coarse and discard a correct list every frame forever. The `coarsening ** 4` term is capped against the local orbit scale (the semi-major axis implied by `localOrbitPeriod` at that position) rather than the raw distance to the strongest attractor, since out in interplanetary space that attractor is the star and a distance-based cap is effectively no cap at all. `predictsFuture` (`false` by default; `Ship`/`Ammo` set it `true`) only decides *whether* an entity predicts at all — how far ahead is `horizon`, an argument threaded down from `DisplayWindowManager.durationSec(referencePeriod)` every frame, not a per-class constant, since the prediction horizon now tracks whatever span the future-ghost slider can reach. The payoff is `displayState(t)`: `t <= actualTrajectory.state.t ? actualTrajectory.at(t) : (predictedTrajectory?.at(t) ?? null)` — a two-way branch (past vs. future, nothing else) that `sync` (below) and every marker-position lookup route through, so "what does this entity look like at the currently-displayed time" has exactly one answer for meshes and markers alike.
- `src/physics/trajectory-features.ts` — pure functions that find orbital features from an already-integrated trajectory, rather than from the osculating `OrbitalElements` at some evaluation epoch. It exists because osculating elements oscillate with the epoch under J2 — tens of km of periapsis/apoapsis wobble per revolution at LEO — so an analytically-placed icon jumps every time the evaluation epoch moves, e.g. when a zero-Δv maneuver node is added. `apsisCrossing(center, prev, next)` finds a periapsis/apoapsis directly on the physical condition that defines one — the radial velocity relative to `center` (`dot(r - center.r, v - center.v)`) changing sign — checked on a single pair of *consecutive* integration steps rather than scanned out of a decimated sample array; a negative-to-positive crossing is a periapsis, positive-to-negative is an apoapsis, refined with a fixed 20-iteration golden-section search over `kinematic-state.ts`'s `hermiteInterpolate` between the two steps — the same interpolation `DynamicTrajectory.at`/`game/trajectory-line.ts` already use, not a second one. Working on the raw step pair rather than a coarse sample array is what makes a genuine collision course (radial velocity stays negative all the way to impact, never crossing zero) correctly report no periapsis at all, instead of a coarse sample array spuriously reading an earlier, non-final close approach as the periapsis. `findEquatorCrossings(samples, center, pole)` finds the ascending/descending sign change of distance-from-center projected onto `pole` by scanning an already-integrated sample series (a bracketing triple by sign pattern), refined by a fixed 20-iteration bisection — a different feature with a different consumer (`game/marker/equator-node-marker-pair.ts`), which has only a decimated sample series to work from rather than a live step pair. Both iteration counts are constants rather than convergence-driven, since a variable iteration count would let the result wobble frame to frame by itself. Pure functions, no game state.
- `src/physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts` — `DynamicTrajectory`: THREE/game-independent (`physics/`, alongside `StateQueue`/`Ephemeris` as "pure but stateful"), holding **one** `KinematicState` as `state` plus a decimated `StateQueue` of samples strictly older than it (`history`). `step(dt, bodies, bcInv, thrust, sampleInterval, keepDuration)` advances via `dynamics.ts`'s `stepDynamics` against the caller-supplied gravity-source array (`bodies` — the caller's own `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(t)` result; which timestamp to sample it at, e.g. the step's midpoint, is the caller's policy, not this class's) — then — only if `keepDuration > 0` and the about-to-be-overwritten `state` is at least `sampleInterval` seconds past the newest recorded sample — pushes that pre-step state into `history` and caps the window with `history.cleanup(keepDuration, 2)` (this is where the display-resolution decimation happens; `keepDuration = 0` never touches `history` at all, so casings/debris/ammo cost nothing). `prevState` (needed every step by `simulation/contact.ts`'s swept-sphere TOI test and `targeter.ts`'s board crossings, even for entities with `keepDuration = 0`) is tracked in its own field, separate from the throttled `history` — it's simply "the state one `step`/`reset` ago". `reset(state)` is the discontinuous-overwrite counterpart to `step`: it discards any `history` samples at-or-after the new state's time (`StateQueue.discardFrom`) to keep the "history is older than state" invariant, and still advances `prevState`. `at(t)` answers any time within the retained span — `t === state.t` returns `state` itself, newer than `history.newest` but older than `state` interpolates between the two via `kinematic-state.ts`'s `hermiteInterpolate`, and anything older delegates to `history.at(t)` — so callers never need to reconcile the queue and the live state themselves. `samplesOldestFirst()` flattens the retained span into the oldest-first `readonly KinematicState[]` that `game/trajectory-line.ts` wants, joining `history` and `state` in the one place that owns the invariant between them, and memoizes the result — repeated calls between a `step`/`reset` pair return the same array reference, which is what lets `TrajectoryLine.syncGeometry`'s reference-equality re-bake guard actually skip work; `step`/`reset` both invalidate the memo since both change what the array would contain. This class holds no orbital-elements memo of its own — a state's osculating elements around a chosen body are `game-entity/game-entity.ts`'s `GameEntity.orbitalElementsAround`, since which body to center on is a per-caller decision this class has no stake in. The class is deliberately direction-agnostic — the same step/at/record operations back a **past** span (`GameEntity.actualTrajectory`), a **future** one (`GameEntity.predictedTrajectory`, whose `history` spans "now → prediction horizon"), and a **planned** one (`plan/plan-arc.ts`'s segment). There is no second implementation of "advance a timed state while recording decimated samples" anywhere in the codebase, and adding one would be a mistake.
- `ship.ts` — `Ship` (abstract; adds name/hp on top of the base `GameEntity.radius` (the constructor's own `radius` argument, `PLAYER_HULL_RADIUS`/`ENEMY_RADIUS` — used for both bullet-hit and rigid-contact geometry, with no separate ship-specific hit radius), `bcInv = SHIP_BCINV`, `baseHistoryDuration = SHIP_HISTORY_DURATION` — the per-class floor on how much past it keeps, raised on demand by `requestHistoryDuration` — and `predictsFuture = true`). Base for `Player`/`Enemy`. Also holds the two HP marker glyphs `markerItem` picks between: `hpMarkerSvg()` (combat view — a triangle outline with per-edge HP notches, meaningful only unrotated) and `headingHpMarkerSvg()` (map view — a triangle whose outline stays fixed while an inner fill rises from the base by HP ratio, so rotating it to face the heading direction never garbles the reading). **It also owns the parts model, and parts are the single source of truth for both health and performance.** `initDefaultParts()` splits the ship's nominal `maxHp` across a fixed `DEFAULT_PART_HP_RATIO` table (summing to 1) and then re-derives `hp`/`maxHp` from the parts it actually built, so the two can never disagree by a rounding step; radiators and solar panels come in **two parts each** because the ship physically has a left and a right panel (`radiatorParts`/`solarParts` return them in `RadiatorSide` order — `'up'` first). All damage funnels through `applyDamageToParts(amount, part?)`, which applies the best armor part's `damageReduction` and then either hits the caller-named part (when the hit location identifies one, e.g. a radiator panel) or a random surviving part; `updateOverallHp()` then sets `hp` to the sum of every part's remaining HP, overridden to 0 the moment the hull or the cockpit is gone. Performance is read the same way — `totalThrust`/`totalTorque`/`totalFuel`/`totalCoolingRate`/`totalPowerGeneration`/`totalFireRate`/`averageMuzzleVelocity` are what the throttle, thermal, power and gun systems actually consume, so swapping a part in the dock changes behaviour with no further wiring. The default thruster's `thrust` is defined as `PLAYER_MASS × max(THROTTLE_LEVELS)` precisely so a stock ship's real acceleration equals the number the HUD prints. `Bullet` carries the firing weapon's `damage`, so what a hit takes off is decided by the shooter's parts rather than a global constant. **`Part.weight` is still unread** — `mass` remains a per-class field (`Player` 1000 kg, `Enemy` 10000 kg), so a heavier part costs nothing yet; wiring it needs a decision on how an enemy's mass relates to its parts.

  The node-vs-context-menu split: in map mode `game.ts` calls `MapContextActions.handleMapRightClick`/`handleLeftClick`/`handleDoubleClick` before `PlanEditor.handleMapPointer`, so a right-click that lands on a marker (celestial label/ship/apsis/AN-DN icon) is claimed by `MapContextActions` first; only a right-click that misses every marker reaches the editor's own node hit test, and whatever that also misses falls through to `MapContextActions.handleEmptySpaceRightClick` last — the priority is expressed purely as call order, and the gizmos never reference each other.

`src/game/plan/` — maneuver planning and its future-trajectory *display*, independent of whether the map is open. "When and in what frame to look" (display duration, the future-ghost slider, the coordinate frame the future is drawn in) is a separate, `game/`-root concern (`DisplayWindowManager` below) from "what a plan looks like when you look" (this folder):
- `src/game/docking.ts` — `Docking`: the base rendezvous/dock/launch cross-cut (proximity detection spans `Base`+every `Player`, the dock/launch transition spans `EntityManager`+`CameraSystem`+`ActivePlayerController`+the `BaseView` UI — no single one of those owns it, so it gets its own module, the same shape as `MapContextActions`/`ViewManager`). **It holds no `Game` reference** — every collaborator arrives as its own reference (`ActivePlayerController`, `Stage`, `CameraSystem`, `ViewManager`, `EntityManager`, `MapContextActions`), and the one thing with nowhere else to come from — the pause state, which is `Game`'s own by the `/refactor-fixed` exception — arrives as two closures (`pauseGame`/`resumeGame`) instead of the whole object. **That is a deliberate, interim exception to this project's "inject references, not closures" rule**, and the reason is the imbalance: the two calls needed are tiny against everything `Game` exposes, and this module is still growing, so handing it `Game` would hand it reach it has no stated use for. Recording the exception here and in the constructor's own comment — rather than in `/refactor-fixed` — is itself the statement that it is a stopgap, not a settled boundary. `Game.resume()` is a plain assignment, so `leaveDock` calls it unconditionally rather than reading an injected `isPaused` first. Owns `BaseView` (`hud/base-view.ts`) and wires its `onLaunchShip`/`onClose` callbacks. `selectBase(base)` marks a base as the one `canEnterDock()` (and thus the `ViewBadge`'s view selector) will accept without itself changing the current view — `MapContextActions.handleLeftClick`'s base case and the property window's own "ドックビューを開く" item both funnel through it; `activate(base)` is `selectBase` plus an immediate `viewManager.setView('dock')`, kept as the one call that actually opens the dock. `checkProximity()` (called unconditionally from `Game.update` once per frame) returns immediately while `viewManager.current === 'dock'` — its own rule, not a caller-side guarantee — and otherwise walks `entities.bases` × `entities.players` and docks a ship once it's within `DOCK_CAPTURE_DIST` at under `DOCK_CAPTURE_REL_V` relative speed. Docking pushes a `DockedShipEntry` onto `base.baseState.dockedShips` and calls the new `EntityManager.parkPlayer` (splices out of `players` without disposing — `removePlayer`'s `dispose()` would destroy the mesh/VFX permanently, which a ship that will fly again can't afford); **`alive` is never touched by any of `dock`/`buildShip`/`launch`** — a docked, freshly-built, or freshly-restored-from-save ship stays `alive` throughout, and "docked" is defined purely as "outside `entities.players`", the same way a `Base`'s `dockedShips` entry needs no separate `alive`-mirroring field. Because a parked ship's `syncPlayer` no longer runs at all (it's not in `players`), `Docking` sets `ship.obj.visible = false` once at dock time as the one discontinuous mesh edit the transition needs (mirroring the scene-mutating lifecycle calls `EntityManager.removePlayer`/`dispose` already make from inside `Game.update`'s own entity-cleanup passes). The same "no more per-frame updates" gap used to leave the shared engine/RCS loop sounds stuck on if the docked ship was the active one and was thrusting/rotating at capture — `PlayerThrottle.updateThrustState`/`updateTorque` are the only things that ever silence `WorldSfx.setThrust`/`setRcs`, and those stop being called for a parked ship the instant it leaves `entities.players` (a differently-active ship would eventually re-silence them next frame, but the last-ship-docked case had nothing left to do it). `dock()` now calls `worldSfx.setThrust(false)`/`setRcs(false)` itself whenever `wasActive`, unconditionally, so this can't depend on what (if anything) becomes active next; if docking leaves `activePlayers.current === null` (no other living ship to hand off to), it also calls `viewManager.setView('map')` — a docked-away combat view would otherwise have nothing left to show. Launching reverses it: a fresh `KinematicState` near the base, `entities.addPlayer`, `activePlayers.set` — no explicit `obj.visible = true` needed, since the ship is back in `entities.players` before this frame's `sync` runs and `syncPlayer` recomputes visibility on its own. `buildShip` (wired to `BaseView.onBuildShip`) is the same "docked but not flying" state reached from the opposite direction: it constructs a brand-new `Player` at the base's own state, immediately sets `obj.visible = false`, and pushes a `DockedShipEntry` — no rendezvous needed because the ship never entered `entities.players` in the first place. `BaseView` collects the price and calls back only once payment is settled, so `Docking` never has to know about money.
- `src/game/map-pickables.ts` — `MapPickables`: the frame's single candidate set of `MapPickable`s and the `MapVisibilityPolicy` they're judged against — "what may be picked", split from "what happens once it is" (`map-context-actions.ts`'s `MapContextActions`, below). `refresh(displayWindow)` returns immediately unless `cameraSystem.overviewMode` holds — `Game.updateMapPresentation` calls it unconditionally every frame and leaves the map-view check to `refresh` itself — and otherwise re-derives `FocusMarkers`' celestial-label positions and `NavTarget`'s AN/DN (both are part of the set, so the pairing is kept inside rather than imposed on callers) and rebuilds `pickables`: `FocusMarkers`' celestial labels + every living self-flown ship (`'player'`), enemy (`'ship'`), ammo pickup and base, each at its `displayState(displayTime)` so a picked object sits at the same future-ghost position as its own mesh, + `navTarget.mapPickables()` + `PlanDisplay.apsisMarkers` + every entity's own `equatorNodes?.mapPickables()` (a loop over `entities.all()`, since each object owns its own EqAN/EqDN pair — see `marker/equator-node-marker-pair.ts` below). **The candidate set stays one list** — splitting it per kind guarantees a consumer that only reads one slice, which is how "the menu opens but focus jumps to Earth" bugs happen; `CameraSystem.update` (focus resolution) and `MapContextActions.handleRightClick` (menu) both read this same `pickables` getter. `refresh` is also **the one place the frame's `MapVisibilityPolicy` is built**, exposed as the `visibilityPolicy` getter (`null` until the first `refresh`): settling it in the update phase and letting `Game.sync` read it back keeps "what may be picked" and "what is drawn" one judgment, rather than two structurally identical constructions that could be handed different focus ids or camera positions. Each candidate's `inFocusedSystem` is resolved for the non-`'body'` kinds only — a celestial body's row stands for a system rather than sitting in one, so nothing reads the value for it, and deriving it costs a `strongestAttractor` plus an ancestor walk per candidate. `lastSimTime` is the `simTime` the frame's `refresh` ran against, read back by `MapContextActions` so a time-dependent row (e.g. a passage countdown) is derived against the same instant the candidate set itself was. `perfCounts()` is the map-mode/candidate-count/label-count slice `Game.perfCounts()` merges in. Constructed by `Game` right after `PlanEditor` (`this.editor`), taking `Game`/`EntityManager`/`Ephemeris`/`NavTarget`/`CameraSystem`/`PlanEditor` as plain references — holding none of `MapContextActions`'s own state (`windows`/`ObjectListPanel`/`ContextMenu`) is what lets a caller that only needs the candidate set (`OverviewCamera.update`, `FrameControls.sync`) take a `MapPickables` reference alone.

- `src/game/map-context-actions.ts` — `MapContextActions`: everything that happens once a `MapPickable` is picked — the left/right/double-click hit test, the per-kind context menu and property-window item set, the property-window lifecycle, and the dispatch of the chosen action to its owner; the candidate set itself is `MapPickables` (above), taken as a constructor argument and read back through its `pickables`/`visibilityPolicy`/`lastSimTime` getters rather than rebuilt here. `handleMapRightClick(input, simTime)` picks via `map-pickable.ts`'s `pickNearest` over `this.pickables.pickables` and opens a `hud/property-window.ts` `PropertyWindow<MenuAction>` (below) built from `itemsFor`. Each open window's `entry.target` is refreshed every `sync()` from the current candidate set, and its `onSelect` reads through `entry.target` rather than the `target` captured at open time — a time-dependent action (warp/add-node) must run against the object's current position/crossing time, not a frame-old snapshot; `'empty-space'` is the one kind with nothing to show a property window about, so `handleEmptySpaceRightClick` keeps opening the plain `ContextMenu<MapPickable>` instead — via the shared private `openEmptySpaceMenu`, which is also what a combat-view right click that hits nothing falls through to (below), so the two views never carry two separate empty-space menu implementations. `handleCombatRightClick(input, targets, project, simTime)` is `handleMapRightClick`'s combat-view counterpart (the same object opens the same window regardless of view) — it resolves the click through `Targeter.pickTargetAt` rather than `this.pickables.pickables` (which only `MapPickables.refresh` — map-view-only — populates) and, on a hit, opens the identical `openPropertyWindow` after converting the `CombatTarget` to a `MapPickable` (`combatTargetPickable`, kind `'player'`/`'ship'` by `instanceof Player`); a miss falls through to `openEmptySpaceMenu`. The one thing that differs by view is item/row content, not window type: `combatTargetLockItems(entity)` (private) prepends `targetPrimary`/`targetSecondary` items to the `'ship'`/`'player'` handlers' `itemsFor` — reading/writing `game.targeter.target`/`.secondaryTarget` via `Targeter.setPrimaryTarget`/`.setSecondaryTarget` (`runTargetLock`) — and returns none of them while `cameraSystem.overviewMode` holds, since target-lock has no meaning on the map. `itemsFor`: `'body'`/`'ship'`/`'player'` all add the shared `navTargetItems` — set/unset nav-target, shown only when `NavTarget.canTarget` finds a resolvable orbital plane for that id, so an item is never offered and then silently refused; `'apsis'`/`'relnode'`/`'eqnode'` all add both "warp here" (`SimSpeedManager.startAutoWarpTo`) and "add node here" (`PlanEditor.addNodeAt`), routed through the shared `runApsisRelnode`; `'player'` additionally offers, at the top of its item list, a single 軌道計画の実行 toggle (shown only while `activeStage.executesPlans`, cycling OFF→瞬間移動→自動操縦→OFF on each click, showing the current mode in its own label — `PlanExecutionMode` above) followed by activate/deactivate (`game.activePlayers.set`/`setOrNull`, one label swapping for the other by whether this ship is already active) and delete, the last simply omitted for the active ship so the action is unreachable rather than rejected. `'player'`/`'ship'`/`'ammo'`/`'base'` also offer "duplicate" (`duplicateItems()`), gated — like `'empty-space'`'s "place object" item and `runDuplicate`'s dispatch — on `activeStage.authoring` being non-`null` (`stages/stage.ts`'s `ObjectAuthoring`, the placement/duplication entry point a stage that supports authoring returns for itself), so the item is never shown where there's nowhere to route it, matching the "don't offer what would be refused" rule the whole menu follows. Choosing it (`runDuplicate` → `duplicateSourceFor`, which maps `MapPickable.kind` to the matching `EntityManager` lookup and that entity's live `state`) hands the state to `activeStage.authoring.openShipPlacerForDuplicate`, which converts it to a preset `ElementsForm` via `creative/duplicate-form.ts`'s `elementsFormFromState` and opens `ShipPlacerPanel` pre-filled with it — a body/`'apsis'`/`'relnode'` target has no matching `duplicateSourceFor` case (nothing to duplicate), which is exactly why those kinds don't get the item in the first place. If the state can't convert (hyperbolic/parabolic) or the converted form's reference body fails `placement-validation.ts`'s `validateBaseReferenceFields` (a base whose current orbit isn't moon-centered), `openShipPlacerForDuplicate` doesn't pass the mismatched form through at all — it hints why and opens with only the object type carried over (`ShipPlacerPreset`'s `{kind: 'objectType'}`), since applying the values while silently rejecting just the reference body would leave the panel showing a different orbit than the one displayed. `handleRightClick` runs before `PlanEditor.handleMapPointer`, so a right-click on a marker is claimed here first; `PlanEditor.handleMapPointer` only sees the misses, consuming the ones that hit a node, so priority is expressed purely as call order and the two gizmos never reference each other. A right-click that hits neither falls through to `handleEmptySpaceRightClick`, which opens the `'empty-space'` menu whatever stage is running — only its "place object" item is gated on `activeStage.authoring`. `MapContextActions` owns a permanent `ObjectListPanel` (`hud/object-list-panel.ts`, docked top-right via `hudRail(root, 'right')`) shown for as long as `overviewMode` holds, with a `buildCollapseToggle` fold on its own heading (the list is always up and takes a wide strip of the right edge, so it needs to be foldable without being closable), listing the current `pickables`, grouped into per-kind accordion sections (天体/自艦/敵/弾薬/基地; `apsis`/`relnode`/`eqnode`/`empty-space` are markers, not objects, and are left out), each section's header toggling its own body open/closed — within 天体, a body's satellites and own Lagrange points nest under that body's row as a collapsible child list rather than a flat indent, and a satellite's own Lagrange points nest one level deeper still under the satellite's own row, mirroring the physical hierarchy (`ObjectListPanel`'s recursive `RowNode`, built from a `parentOf: ReadonlyMap<id, id>` `MapContextActions.sync` derives from `FocusMarkers.labels` — a Lagrange label's parent is `object-groups.ts`'s `lagrangeParentId`, an ordinary body's is `primaryOf`; a body with neither a parent nor children stays a flat row). Every such child list starts collapsed (`RowNode.expanded` defaults `false`) since the set nested under any one body is large and rarely needed at a glance, unlike the five top-level sections which start expanded. A row answers three separate gestures: a single click selects it (`onSelect`), a double click moves the camera focus there (`onFocus` → `game.frameControls.setFocus({kind:'object', id})`) — the same single-click-selects / double-click-commits split the map itself uses, so no single click moves the view out from under you — and a right-click (`onSelectRight`) opens the same `openPropertyWindow` a right-click on the object itself would — so the window is just another view onto the one candidate set, not a second source of what's on the map. `sync(simTime, attractors, player)` (called from `Game.sync`, alongside the other map-mode panel syncs, reading `cameraSystem.overviewMode` itself rather than taking it as an argument) is the only place `pickables` reaches the DOM — and it must not do that by rewriting `innerHTML`: `ObjectListPanel.sync` keeps one persistent row element per item id (added/removed by diffing against the previous frame's id set) precisely because a full rebuild would drop each row's click listener the instant a click lands mid-frame, the same reason `stage.ts`'s radiator buttons stay off the `StageStatusPanel`'s rebuilt-every-frame center column. **Which rows a section holds, in what order, and under which parent is settled per section as ids alone (`SectionOrder`) and rebuilt only when it can actually differ** — when the inputs that decide it change (the candidate ids, their display names, kinds and parents, whether each passes the filter, and the filter/sort selection, all compared against the previous frame's record), or when the order being held no longer satisfies the sort under this frame's values. That second condition is what the distance sort needs: nothing about the candidate set changes as ships drift, yet the correct order does, so a pure input comparison would freeze a stale order — checking the held order against the comparator instead costs one pass and rebuilds exactly on the frames the sort genuinely flips. Row values (distance, detail) and the per-section header counts are written every frame regardless, since those change continuously and cost nothing to push.

`MapContextActions` also owns the property-window lifecycle: `windows: Map<string, WindowEntry>` (`WindowEntry = {win: PropertyWindow<MenuAction>, target: MapPickable}`) keyed by `` `${kind}:${id}` `` (a `MapPickable.id` alone isn't unique across kinds) so an object holds at most one window. **The "at most one non-clipped window" exclusivity itself moved to `hud/overlay-manager.ts`'s `OverlayManager`** (施策5): `openPropertyWindow` passes a shared `PROPERTY_WINDOW_TEMP_GROUP` string as `PropertyWindow`'s `tempWindowGroup` constructor argument, and `PropertyWindow` itself declares/reconfigures its own `exclusiveGroup` membership (only while unclipped) on every clip toggle — `MapContextActions` no longer tracks a `tempWindowKey` field or wires `onOutsideClick`/`onClipChange` callbacks at all. `openPropertyWindow(clientX, clientY, target, simTime)` — called from `handleRightClick` (map view) and `handleCombatRightClick` (combat view, below) for every kind but `'empty-space'` — moves and `bringToFront()`s an existing window for that key instead of opening a second one; otherwise it builds one via `buildContent` (`itemsFor`'s `type: 'header'` item becomes the window's title/subtitle; the action items themselves come from `windowItems`, not a one-time snapshot — see below) with `rows: []` (filled by the next `sync`, so there's no separate row-building path at open time) and wires `onSelect`/`onClose`. `onSelect` calls `this.handlers[kind].run`, then closes the window unless it's clipped — except `'delete'`, which always closes it since the target is gone either way. `close()` (the same method `ViewManager` already called to fold the object-list panel on leaving the map view) now also closes every open window. `sync(overviewMode, simTime, bodies, player)` decides whether to close a window via `isTargetGone(target)` — for `'player'`/`'ship'`/`'ammo'`/`'base'` this asks `EntityManager`'s own finders (a `'player'` by whether `findPlayer(id)` still resolves at all, since a lost self-flown ship leaves `entities.players` outright; the other three by the found entity's `alive`) rather than the frame's `pickables`, since a living ship can legitimately be absent from `pickables` for a frame when its `displayState(displayTime)` comes back `null` (future-ghost slider past its prediction horizon, or time-warp outrunning prediction growth) — closing on that would be wrong, since the entity hasn't died. The remaining kinds (celestial body/apsis/AN-DN icons) have no entity of their own, so they fall back to "still in this frame's `pickables`". A window that survives has its stored `target` refreshed from the current `pickables` entry when one exists (kept as-is otherwise, since every kind's row/item derivation reads the live entity's own state rather than `target.pos`) before `syncRows`/`syncItems` re-derive that kind's `PropertyRow[]`/`PropertyWindowItem[]` and push them through — row derivation is display-only and reuses `hud/orbit-info.ts`'s `orbitInfo`/`relativeInfo` for every kind that shows orbital elements, never rederiving them, and action-item derivation (`windowItems`, shared by `buildContent` at open time and `sync` every frame after) re-runs `itemsFor` so a clipped window's items track state that changes after it opened (e.g. which ship is the active one). A row is omitted outright (not shown as `—`) wherever it depends on a live `player` that doesn't exist, e.g. on CREATIVE before any ship is placed. `buildContent` also passes `renameHandlerFor(target)` as `onRename` — non-`undefined` only for `'player'`/`'base'` (the two kinds with a mutable display name — `Player`/`Base``s own `name`), and re-resolved through `target.id` inside the closure (not the `target` object itself) so a rename always lands on the live entity regardless of how many frames have passed since the window opened.
- `src/game/hud/base-view.ts` — `BaseView`: the full-screen docked-ship UI (格納艦/部品/ショップ tabs), entered as a view via `ViewManager.setView('dock')` once a base is active. Its own `#base-view` CSS is injected once via a module-local `ensureStyle()` (the same pattern `context-menu.ts`/`property-window.ts` already use), not `hud/hud-root.ts`'s STYLE. Under `MQ_COMPACT` the header wraps (title+close on the first line, the tab bar flowing to a full-width second line) and the 部品 tab's two-column layout (搭載部品/倉庫) collapses to one column; both part-row grids' fixed `120px`/`60px` columns are `minmax()`-based (`minmax(80px,120px)`/`minmax(40px,60px)`, narrower still under compact) so a column shrinks rather than overflowing on a narrow phone. Holds `currentBase`/`currentShip`/`currentTab` and rewrites its body's `innerHTML` per tab on every `open()`/tab switch/action (a one-shot modal, not a per-frame HUD panel, so the differential-DOM-update rule `ObjectListPanel`/`StageStatusPanel` follow for per-frame panels doesn't apply here — nothing here runs outside a user click). `SHOP_CATALOG` is its static part price list — **its numbers must stay in the same units and the same order of magnitude as `Ship.initDefaultParts`**, which is why the thruster entries derive their thrust/torque from `PLAYER_MASS`/`THROTTLE_LEVELS`/`MAX_ANG_ACCEL` rather than spelling out literals: a catalog value that drifts from the default scale turns a swap into a silent cliff (the thruster once read `thrust: 100` against a default of 100,000). Buying calls `game-entity/parts.ts`'s `createPart` and pushes into `base.baseState.inventory`; the 部品 tab shows 搭載部品 and 倉庫 side by side so the same-`type` comparison a swap needs is visible at once, and the 倉庫 column renders even with no docked ship because the inventory belongs to the *base*. Swapping `splice`s in place so `player.parts` keeps its array identity (the `Ship` performance getters read that array), then calls `Ship.refreshFromParts()` — the one place `maxHp`/`hp` are re-derived after a part changes. Repairing a part writes the ship's own `Part.hp` directly (docked `parts` is the live `player.parts` array, not a copy) and then re-derives the `DockedShipEntry`'s `hp`/`maxHp` totals through `Ship.refreshFromParts()`, since those are a display-only snapshot the ship-list tab reads without walking every part — routing through the ship is what keeps the "hull or cockpit gone ⇒ hp 0" rule from being bypassed. RCS tanks can also be refuelled here, which is the only way `RcsTankPart.fuel` is ever restored. The 格納艦 tab's 新造 button is `BaseView`'s own affordability gate (`NEW_SHIP_COST`, priced off `SHOP_CATALOG`'s cheapest full loadout) — it debits `base.baseState.money` itself and only then calls `onBuildShip`, so `Docking` (which owns the actual `Player` construction) never touches currency.
- `src/game/nav-target.ts` — `NavTarget`: the map's *navigation* target — any `MapPickable`, not just an `Enemy`. It is deliberately a different class from `Targeter`'s combat target: the two differ both in what they can point at (`Enemy` only vs. any `MapPickable`) and in what the designation is *for* (gunnery vs. maneuver planning), so neither one's constraints — e.g. `Enemy`-specific fire-assist logic — get to narrow the other's target range. They may happen to point at the same enemy; that is a coincidence, not a shared id. Holds only a target id (`toggleTarget`/`id`); `update(player, entities, ephemeris, displayWindow)` re-derives the relative AN/DN (where the player's orbit crosses the target's orbital plane) from scratch each frame — `resolvePlaneNormal` reads the target's own orbit normal for a ship or base (`orbitalElementsOf(strongestAttractor(...)).hHat`, found by name in `entities.enemies` first, then `entities.players`, then `entities.bases` — a base has a real orbit via `GameEntity` like any other entity, so it resolves the same way), the lunar orbit normal for the moon (`Ephemeris.orbitNormalAt('moon', …)`), or the relevant rotating frame's ẑ for a Lagrange point (`orbitFrameRotationAt('moon'|'earth', …)`) — and stores nothing if the target's plane can't be resolved or is ~coincident with the player's own. It also drives the target's own EqAN/EqDN pair (`marker/equator-node-marker-pair.ts`) off the private `resolveEntity(id, entities)` that `resolvePlaneNormal` shares, before the player's own elements are consulted at all — those icons describe the target's orbit, not the relation between the two. `nodeAnomalies` returns the node true anomalies in the player's own center-relative basis, so `anPos`/`dnPos` are un-baked back to absolute ECI via `frameOfAttractor(playerCenter)` + `toInertialState` before being stored — `positionOnOrbit` alone returns a center-relative position, which only happens to read as ECI while the player orbits Earth (center at the ECI origin). `canTarget(id, entities, ephemeris, t)` runs the same `resolvePlaneNormal` just to answer whether a plane resolves at all, which is what lets `MapContextActions.itemsFor` show the "set as nav-target" item on any `MapPickable.kind` whose id can actually work — including the moon and every Lagrange point, not just ships. `passTimeOf('nav-an'|'nav-dn')` answers the AN/DN crossing time (`tofBetween` from the player's current true anomaly), which `MapContextActions` feeds to `SimSpeedManager.startAutoWarpTo`/`PlanEditor.addNodeAt`. `mapPickables()`/`sync(project, overviewMode, cameraPos)` expose/draw the △/▽ icons themselves — `MapPickables.refresh()` folds them into the shared candidate set alongside `FocusMarkers`' labels and every living ship. `sync` hides an icon `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body when `overviewMode`, off the `Attractor[]` `update` cached at `simTime`. Setting the nav-target itself is otherwise map-only (`MapContextActions`'s right-click), with one exception: `handleCombatBaseClick(entities, input, project, overviewMode)` is `NavTarget`'s own combat-view right-click handler, holding a private `ContextMenu<Base, MenuAction>` (offering only `MenuCommon.navTarget`/`cancel`, since a base isn't a weapons target). It takes `overviewMode` as a plain argument and self-gates (`NavTarget` holds no `CameraSystem` reference, so it can't read the view itself) rather than trusting the caller to have already checked it. `Game.handlePointerInput` calls it *before* `MapContextActions.handleCombatRightClick` — its callback returns `false` on a miss (`Input.takeRightClicks`' fall-through convention), so a click that doesn't land on a base is left for `MapContextActions` to try against enemies/self-flown ships, exactly as if this handler weren't there. This is why the split stays clean: `Targeter` never learns about bases, and `NavTarget` never learns about enemies.
- `src/game/view-manager.ts` — `ViewManager`: **the source of truth for which of the three views is showing** (`ViewId` = `'combat' | 'map' | 'dock'`), and the sole place a transition happens (`setView`). **`setView` calls the private `applyChrome` unconditionally, even when `next` already equals `current`** — so every call, transition or not, leaves the guarantee "the camera/plan/future-display flags and HUD chrome match the current view" holding. The constructor takes `ActivePlayerController` and `TouchControls | null` (both constructed just before it — see `game.ts` above) alongside an optional trailing `requestedView?: WorldViewId` and resolves `worldView` through the same `canEnter` rule `setView` itself uses — `this.worldView = this.canEnter(requested ?? 'combat') ? requested ?? 'combat' : 'map'` — so a request the controller can't satisfy (`'combat'` with no ship yet to fly) falls back to `'map'` at construction exactly as it would on a live `setView('combat')` call, rather than trusting the caller to have already made that judgment. It calls `applyChrome()` itself right after settling `worldView`, so a launch that resolves to `'map'` starts with chrome already in sync rather than needing a first transition through `setView` to earn it. It co-switches the camera-side flag (`cameraSystem.overviewMode`), the plan-side editing flag (`editor.editMode`), and the future-display gate (`displayTimeManager.forceCurrent`) so they move together while staying logically independent. Combat and map are the two views that draw the 3D world, held as the private `worldView: WorldViewId` field; **dock is layered on top of whichever of those you came from** — a separate private `isDockOpen` flag tracks only whether it's open, leaving `worldView` untouched underneath, and `current` derives from the pair (`isDockOpen ? 'dock' : worldView`) rather than being stored itself. `applyChrome` drives the camera/plan flags off `worldView` rather than `current`. That split is what keeps map→dock from running `editor.onMapClosed()` (which prunes zero-Δv nodes) as a side effect of merely opening a dock. **`isDockOpen` stays `ViewManager`'s own source of truth for the dock's open/closed state; `hud/overlay-manager.ts`'s `OverlayManager` is only ever notified, never the other way around**: `setView` compares `isDockOpen` before and after the transition (`wasDockOpen`) and, only on the frame it actually changed, calls the private `syncDockOverlay`, which opens/closes a small `dockOverlayHandle` adapter (a constructor-built field — `contains` forwards to `docking?.baseView.element.contains`, `close` just calls `this.leaveDock()`) under overlay id `'dock-view'`. `leaveDock()` is a second, narrower exit besides `setView('combat' | 'map')`: it closes just the dock (`docking?.leaveDock()` — `Docking`'s own teardown, `baseView.close()` plus a resume if paused — then `isDockOpen = false` → `overlayManager.close('dock-view')` → `applyChrome()`) and drops straight back to whichever `worldView` was underneath, with no `canEnter` check to pass ("leave the dock" is never itself refused the way entering a view can be). `Docking` no longer takes `[ESC]` itself — `OverlayManager.closeTopmostOnEscape()` closes whichever registered overlay is topmost, and for the dock that means calling `dockOverlayHandle.close()` (i.e. `leaveDock()`); `BaseView.onClose` (the ✕ button) calls `leaveDock()` the same way. `handleInput(input)` takes `[M]` (ignored entirely while in dock) and, when leaving map for combat, refuses with `hud.hint('操作できる艦がいません')` whenever `canEnter('combat')` is false instead of silently no-op'ing; leaving combat for map is never refused. `selectableViews()` is what `ViewBadge` lists, filtering out both the current view and any view that can't be entered — the private `canEnter(view)` answers `'dock'` via `Docking.canEnterDock()` and `'combat'` via `this.activePlayers.current !== null` (every other view is always enterable). `ViewManager` knows nothing about `Stage` or the win/lose `phase` — a decided stage's result screen is a full-screen overlay sitting in the `system` layer above every view (combat/map/dock alike), so nothing here needs to force a view change when the game ends. `Docking` registers itself after construction (`setDocking`, the same late-injection shape `MapContextActions.setDocking` uses) because it's built later; `TouchControls` (`Game`'s own private `touchControls: TouchControls | null` field, built before `ViewManager` in `Game`'s constructor) is instead passed straight into `ViewManager`'s constructor, since nothing forces the two to be built in the other order. Rendering/viewpoint code checks `overviewMode`; input/behavior code checks `editMode`. This sits at the `game/` root because everything else about a view belongs to either the camera, the plan, or the dock concern. `applyChrome` also reparents a panel built statically elsewhere between combat and map placement — `hud/hud-root.ts`'s `syncNavballPlacement` (navball → `#hud-rail-left`), a no-op when the element is already at the target parent. It touches no collapse state: which docks and which panels the player folded away inside map view is a choice of theirs, not a property of the view, so it survives a round trip through combat view intact. `serializeView()` exposes `worldView` — the one piece of view state a snapshot saves, since dock is never itself a saved view (a docked ship is snapshotted as a `DockedShipEntry`, not a live view to return to); it re-enters through the constructor's `requestedView` argument on the next launch (`Game` passes `initialSave?.camera?.view`, resolved through `canEnter` as above), not through a method called on a live instance.
- `src/game/hud/frame-controls.ts` — `FrameControls`: the map's **座標系** panel, a cross-cut over two owners the same shape as `MapContextActions`/`ViewManager` — `OverviewCamera` (camera focus/rotation) and `DisplayWindowManager.frame` (the planned polyline's — and every other future display's — origin/rotation) each keep their own state, and this class only writes into them from one panel so the two don't need separate UIs. Two labelled sections, one per coordinate system, each split into the same center × rotation pair so the two read alike: `カメラ(視点)` (`基準にする天体` `AnchorZone` → this class's own `setFocus`, `視点を一緒に回す` `RotationZone` → `overviewCamera.setCameraRotation`) and `軌道計画(描画基準)` (`基準にする天体`/`線を一緒に回す`, both writing `displayWindow.frame` via `ephemeris.frameOf(center, rotatingWith)` and keeping whichever half the other zone chose). A `カメラの基準に追随` `ToggleSwitch` under the plan section decides whether a focus move also drags the plan's center along, and a one-line summary under both states what is currently selected (`カメラ: 地球・慣性系 / 計画: 地球・月回転系`) — the panel's four controls answer four separate questions, so what they add up to has to be readable without recomposing them by eye. **`setFocus(target: FocusTarget)` is the sole entry point for moving the camera's focus, and every focus-changing UI in the game routes through it** — `Game.frameControls` is `public readonly`, and `MapContextActions`'s object-list row double-click/property-window "move focus" item call `frameControls.setFocus(...)` on the reference they hold rather than reaching `overviewCamera.setFocusTarget` directly; `PlanEditor` holds this same instance as its own constructor argument (built right after `CameraSystem`, ahead of `PlanEditor` — see `game.ts` above) and calls `this.frameControls.setFocus(...)` directly for its node-focus menu action, rather than being handed a focus callback. It forwards to `overviewCamera.setFocusTarget` unconditionally, then — only while the `カメラの基準に追随` toggle is on, and only when `target.kind === 'object'` and `target.id` is a body registered in `ephemeris.registry` — also re-centers `displayWindow.frame` on that same body, keeping `rotatingWith` as-is; an unregistered id (a self-flown ship, an enemy, a base, an ammo pickup) or a `'point'` focus leaves `frame` untouched, since `Ephemeris`'s frame resolution has no ECI origin to fall back to for a body it doesn't carry. The toggle exists because moving the focus is a *viewing* action while the plan frame decides the drawn line's shape: with it off, the two stay independent and the line never changes shape as a side effect of looking somewhere else. The coupling is one-way either way — re-centering the plan section never moves the camera focus back. Only the camera section's center zone offers "固定を解除" (`releaseLabel` — see `AnchorZone` below): releasing bakes the camera's current ECI position into a `camera/focus-target.ts` `FocusTarget` fixed in the star-centered inertial frame (`ephemeris.frameOf(starId, null)`, or `ephemeris.inertialFrame` with no star), via `focus-target.ts`'s `focusPoint`, using `overviewCamera.resolvedFocus` and the simTime `sync` last saw (`lastTime` — `onSelect` fires from a DOM event outside the frame flow, so it can't take simTime as an argument). The plan section's center zone never offers a release: a drawn polyline is always baked into *some* coordinate system, so "fix to nothing" has no meaning there — fixing to solar-system space at all is what selecting the star itself in the pulldown already means. `Game` constructs it once and calls `sync(pickables, cameraPos, attractors, simTime, visible)` from `Game.sync`; it has no `update` of its own, since every zone's choices are re-derived fresh each `sync` from the current pickables/attractors rather than advanced over time.

  **Every way of editing Δv funnels through one method, `applyDv(axis, sign, amount)`**, which is the sole place `plan.applyNodeDv` is called from user input — so a drag, a key hold, a button hold, and a latched drag can't each compute their own rounding or sign convention, and a zero `amount` is a no-op (skipping the call, rather than letting `Plan.applyNodeDv`'s unconditional downstream-discard fire on a no-op edit). The three input paths differ only in how they arrive at `amount`: `applyAxisDrag` (short arm drags, below the latch threshold) scales pixel delta by the existing per-drag rate; `applyHeldDv` (WASDQE keys or a `dvButtons` press, checked together via `||` in `updateEditing`) accumulates a per-direction hold time in `dvHoldTime` (indexed `axis*2 + (sign<0?1:0)`) and looks it up through `rampedDvRate` — an exponential ramp from `DV_RATE_MIN` to `DV_RATE_MAX` over `DV_RATE_RAMP_SEC`, so a tap stays fine while holding sweeps up to coarse; a released direction resets its hold time to 0, so ramping always restarts from the beginning. The arm drag itself only free-drags (via `applyAxisDrag`) up to `NodeGizmo`'s own `DV_DRAG_LATCH_PX` — beyond that `NodeGizmo` stops reporting deltas and instead exposes `latch: AxisLatchState | null` (the axis/sign and the pixel excess past the threshold), which `updateEditing` reads every frame and feeds through `applyDv` at a rate proportional to the excess (capped at `DV_RATE_MAX`) — so a large, held drag keeps adding Δv even while the pointer stops moving, without the user needing to keep dragging further.
- `plan-display.ts` — `PlanDisplay`: the plan's trajectory display. It keeps **no copy of the `PlanData` it was updated with** — the only thing `sync` needs to know about it afterwards is how many nodes the drawn polyline has, and `PlanPath.nodeCount` (set by that same `update` call) already answers it, so a second field mirroring the count would only be a value that could go stale against the line actually drawn. What it does hold is the ⬢ `plannedPlayer` ghost marker showing where the plan puts the ship at `displayTime` (present or future alike, so it reads as "is the ship actually flying the plan" the instant the map closes — its label is `計画位置 高度 …` at or before `simTime`, `T+…h…m 高度 …` beyond it, altitude measured from whichever body pulls hardest at that position via `attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor`, not fixed to Earth), the ◇ Pe/Ap periapsis/apoapsis icons, the ルーラー calendar-tick marks along the polyline (below), so it disappears once `displayTime` runs past the trailing arc's one-revolution end. The apsis icons describe **the orbit the ship is on after its last planned burn**, found directly on the actually-drawn integration — not the analytic ellipse — because osculating `OrbitalElements` oscillate with the evaluation epoch under J2 (tens of km of periapsis/apoapsis wobble per revolution at LEO), which would otherwise make the icons visibly jump the instant a zero-Δv node changed which epoch they were evaluated at. `apsisIconsOf` reads `path.finalSegment()`'s `periapsis`/`apoapsis` (`PlanPath`'s exposure of the trailing `PlanArc`'s `periapsisPoint()`/`apoapsisPoint()` — see `plan-arc.ts`/`plan-path.ts` above for how those are found by the radial-velocity sign crossing on the live integration step pair, not by scanning the drawn sample array) directly rather than deriving them itself, so a collision-course segment's `periapsis === null` simply shows no periapsis icon rather than a spurious one. The center both points are measured against is `FinalSegment.apsisCenter` — **the same body the crossing was detected against**, not one re-resolved at the extremum's own time: the icon's altitude reading has to be taken against the body the physics that found it used, or a segment whose strongest attractor changes partway through would print a distance measured from a body the detection never considered. Only that body's *position* is re-evaluated at the extremum's time (`ephemeris.positionOf(center.id, point.t)`), which is the one thing that genuinely moves. The circular guard compares `(apDist - peDist) / (apDist + peDist)` from those two precise center-distances against `APSIS_MIN_ECC`, hiding both icons below it since the apsis direction is then unreliable; a lone point (e.g. a hyperbolic orbit's periapsis with no apoapsis) is shown ungated, since the guard only makes sense once both are available to compare. `apsisTimeOf(id)` reads the found point's own `t` directly (no re-derivation), so `PlanEditor.addNodeAt` always places a node at a time that actually exists on the drawn line, and `apsisMarkers` exposes the currently-shown icons as `MapPickable`s (`kind: 'apsis'`) for that same right-click to hit. In the map view, `syncApsisMarkers` also hides an icon `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body from the camera's current position (`sync`'s `overviewMode`/`cameraPos` arguments) — combat view never gates on this, since a body can't occlude anything from the chase camera's close-in vantage the way it can on the map. The ruler-style calendar ticks are split the same way: `tickIconsOf(mode, simTime)` (update, its two arguments taken from the frame's `DisplayWindow.tickLabelMode`/`simTime` and used only for the label text) reads `PlanPath.timeRange()` and `hud/calendar-ticks.ts`'s `calendarBoundaries` to build the candidate set, tagging each with the coarsest calendar rank it satisfies (an ordinary midnight is a day tick, a month-start midnight is a month tick) rather than deciding the whole displayed span's unit at once — on an eccentric orbit the drawn samples bunch near periapsis and spread near apoapsis, so the screen-space density (and thus which unit reads as legible) varies along a single polyline, and a caller can't pick one interval for the whole thing without either flooding the dense end or leaving the sparse end blank. `syncTickMarkers` (sync) does the actual thinning, walking ranks coarsest-first and keeping a candidate only when it's at least `PLAN_TICK_MIN_PX` from the nearest already-kept tick on either side — a plain per-rank pass would let a fine-rank tick crowd out its own coarse-rank neighbor, so ties are broken toward keeping the coarser marks first. A tick's radius is drawn relative to whichever rank is actually the finest surviving one this frame (`PLAN_TICK_RADIUS_PX`, indexed by that offset rather than by the absolute rank), so the average tick size stays constant as the unit switches along the line and only the switch point itself reads as a size step; its label only shows past the wider `PLAN_TICK_LABEL_MIN_PX` threshold, since a legible tick spacing is still too dense for a label at every one. Each tick is drawn as an inline SVG dot (`tickSvg`, `mk-plantick`) — a dot carries no orientation, so it reads the same when the drawn orbit is viewed from any angle, unlike a line segment aligned to the trajectory's tangent. This is a distinct concern from `hud/tick-scale.ts`'s PREDICT-slider ticks: that module places ticks at even fractions of an *elapsed*-time span with a `T+` label, which has no notion of a UTC calendar boundary at all.
- `plan-path.ts` — `PlanPath`: splits the plan's corners into segments (anchor → node₁ → … → nodeₙ, plus a trailing one), and owns two parallel index-keyed pools — a `PlanArc` per segment (the segment's own integration result) and a pooled `game/trajectory-line.ts` `TrajectoryLine` (grey before the first burn, white after it, orange beyond). The two pools are deliberately not the same lifetime: an arc is discarded and replaced the moment its segment's inputs change enough that it can no longer answer for the new region (see `plan-arc.ts` below), while a line is never discarded — only hidden — once built, since the color a line was constructed with (`arcColor(idx)`) has to stay tied to its screen position (pre-burn/post-burn/trailing) rather than to whichever segment happens to occupy that index this frame; `lineAt(i)` lazily grows the line pool but never shrinks it. `PlanPath` is also the **single place display-frame transforms and screen picking are done** (`toDisplay`/`toDisplayDir`/`projectPoint`/`nearestSample`) so the drawn polyline and click hit-testing can't drift apart. The two transforms are split by what kind of quantity is moved, mirroring `physics/frame.ts`'s own point/direction split: `toDisplay` is affine (a sample position), `toDisplayDir` is rotation-only (an orbital basis vector, e.g. the Δv gizmo's axes) — putting a direction through the point path would offset it by the display frame's own origin the moment that frame is centered on a moving body. `update(planData, ephemeris, frame, currentTime)` (`planData` being `plan.ts`'s `PlanData`, whose anchor is always settled — a caller that has already resolved "is there a plan to draw" passes the one value through instead of destructuring it) rebuilds the segments; for each index it asks the pooled `PlanArc` (if one exists yet) whether `represents(state0, end, sourceRevision, apsisCenterId, tracksLiveAnchor)` holds for this frame's segment — if not, it builds a fresh `PlanArc` (whose constructor integrates `state0` → `end` synchronously, the expensive path) and replaces the pooled entry outright; if so, it calls the existing arc's `setEnd(end)` instead, which extends the integration forward from its current tip or merely narrows the range it answers for, never re-deriving what's already been integrated. `sync(fo, project)` reads each `PlanArc.trajectory`/`.end` and pushes them through that index's own pooled line's `setDash`/`syncGeometry`/`syncTransform`/`sync`. The display context is split along the same line — the frame and un-bake time are settled in `update` (screen picking from a DOM pointer event has to work between frames), the `ProjectFn` arrives in `sync`; the `ProjectFn` is deliberately *not* captured at construction, since `CameraSystem.activeCameraProjection` returns a snapshot of whichever camera is active *now* (capturing it once would pin picking to the chase camera's startup viewpoint). `finalSegment()` returns the trailing segment as one `FinalSegment` value (`null` only before `update()` has ever run — a trailing segment always exists once it has, since `buildSegments` always appends one past the last node): `state0` (its start state), `samples` (that segment's already-integrated `PlanArc.samples`, the existing array by reference, not a copy — re-exposing it under a new frame-local array would defeat `game/trajectory-line.ts`'s reference-identity re-bake guard the moment a consumer draws it), `periapsis`/`apoapsis` (the same arc's `periapsisPoint()`/`apoapsisPoint()`, each still `null` on its own when the segment is truncated before reaching that extremum), and `apsisCenter` (the body those two were measured against, so a consumer can state a distance without re-resolving a center that might disagree with the detection's own). `state0`/`samples` are what `PlanEditor` feeds the operated ship's own `marker/equator-node-marker-pair.ts` `EquatorNodeMarkerPair` (see that module below), so it doesn't have to know how segments are split; `periapsis`/`apoapsis` are what `PlanDisplay`'s apsis icons read directly rather than deriving from `samples` themselves — the two are computed by the trailing arc's own integration against the `apsisCenter` its `Segment` carries — chosen once in `buildSegments` by `strongestAttractor` at that segment's own start state and time, rather than at the currently-displayed time, so the detected apsis stays tied to the segment's own physics regardless of where the future-ghost slider sits. That choice and the segment's own length are the same question asked of the same state, so `buildSegments` draws the body window once and answers both from it. Every other segment carries `apsisCenter: null`, since only the trailing segment is ever queried for Pe/Ap; carrying the body on the segment (and forwarding it through `FinalSegment`) is what lets `PlanDisplay` state a distance against the very body the detection used. Owned by `PlanDisplay`; `PlanEditor` shares the same instance (`planDisplay.path`) for its own screen picking. `sync` caches its caller-supplied camera position — `nearestSample` fires off a DOM pointer event, outside the frame flow — and drops any sample `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body before ranking candidates, so a node can't be placed on a stretch of the line that isn't actually visible. **How far ahead the plan is drawn is the PREDICT panel's call**: every segment ends at the next node's `t`, and the trailing one runs `plan.ts`'s `segmentDurationFrom(state0, attractors, displayDuration)` from its own start state — the constructor takes a `DisplayDurationSource` (`plan.ts`'s narrow `{durationSec(referencePeriod): number}` interface, threaded from `Game`'s concrete `DisplayWindowManager` through `PlanEditor`/`PlanDisplay` — see `display-window-manager.ts` above) precisely so this length tracks the PREDICT panel's selection instead of a fixed span, with no import of `display-window-manager.ts` itself. The default `'orbit'` preset reproduces one orbital period around whichever body pulls hardest at that state (`attractor.ts`'s `orbitalElementsOf`'s `OrbitalElements.period` — not `localOrbitPeriod`'s circular approximation, which would return the short period of the periapsis radius for a node freshly placed on an eccentric orbit); picking a longer preset draws further, and `Plan.nodeTimeRange` (the range a node may be dragged across) grows the same way since it calls the same `segmentDurationFrom`. Beyond one revolution the polyline overlaps itself, so a click can no longer be disambiguated by screen distance alone — see `nearestSample` below. `nearestSample`'s optional `TimeRange` is how a caller restricts picking to part of the polyline; `PlanEditor` passes `Plan.nodeTimeRange` there so a node drag can only ever land on a legal time. Its tie-break is two-stage: first the screen-nearest candidate decides *which arc wins* — arc 0 (pre-burn) always has smaller `t` than the post-burn arcs, so comparing `t` across arcs at the point where two arcs visually cross would silently pick the wrong one — then, restricted to that arc's candidates within `NEAREST_SAMPLE_TIE_PX` of the screen-nearest distance, it picks whichever is closest to the caller-supplied `referenceT` (ties broken toward the earlier `t`). Both call sites in `plan-editor.ts` route through this one rule rather than branching: new-node placement (`handleMapClick`/`handleNodeRightClick`) passes `referenceT = -Infinity`, which — since every candidate is then equally "infinitely far" — degenerates to the earliest-`t` tie-break and reproduces "the first time the line reaches here"; a node drag (`dragNodeToNearestSample`) passes the node's own current time, so a click near where a later revolution's polyline overlaps an earlier one keeps the node on the revolution it was actually dragged from instead of snapping back to revolution 0. Every line is drawn at the same `PLAN_ARC_OPACITY`, so the only thing distinguishing the pre-burn segment's line from the post-burn ones is `SEGMENT_COLORS`. `sync` also converts the dash pattern from screen pixels to world metres before handing it to each line (`TrajectoryLine.setDash`): it takes the arc's middle sample, puts it through its own `toDisplay`, and asks the caller-supplied `ScaleFn` for the metres-per-pixel there, so the dashes keep the same on-screen spacing across the map's four orders of magnitude of zoom.
- `plan-attractors.ts` — `PlanAttractors`: **計画軌道の積分が時刻ごとに引く重力源と衝突体、そしてその保持**。 `PlanEditor` が1つだけ持ち、フレームを跨いで生き続ける。`at(t)` は解析天体の窓(`Ephemeris.attractorsAt(t)`)を**1回だけ**引き、衝突体(`mu = 0` の表示天体を含む全天体 + `displayState(t)` が解ける生存 `collides` entity。計画の起点になっている艦は除外集合で外す)と重力源(そのうち `mu !== 0` のもの + 動的重力源)を**同じ窓から**組む — 同じ時刻の重力源を別の窓として引き直すと、同じ天体の位置を二度計算することになる。求めた `{gravity, collision, collisionById, classified}` は時刻をキーに数スロットだけ保持する: 1ステップが引くのは開始・中点・終了の3時刻で、ある歩の終了は次の歩の開始と一致するので、その一致ぶんが当たる。**保持を捨てる判断はこのクラスの中だけで完結する** — `resolve(...)` はこのフレームの入力(除外集合・`Plan.revision`・`planEnd`・`simTime`)を受け取って `planSourceRevision` を組み直し、値が動いたときにだけ保持を捨てる。呼び出し側は入力を渡すだけで、何をいつ捨てるかを知らない。

  **`revision` is what tells `PlanArc` that a cached integration is stale**, and it is a *semantic* generation value: `planSourceRevision(entities, excludedEntityIds, planRevision, planEnd, simTime)` folds exactly the inputs that can change what `at()` returns — `Plan.revision` (the plan's own edit counter), the excluded-id set (the operated ship changing), and, per entity, that entity's `id` and its prediction tip. `Ephemeris`'s own phase offsets are fixed at construction and never move under a running `Game` (loading a snapshot discards the running `Game` and constructs a fresh one — with its own `Ephemeris` — rather than moving bodies under the current one), so there is no "save load moved every body" term left to fold in. **Only the entities that can answer for a future time appear in `at()`'s output at all** — `displayState(t)` reads `predictedTrajectory` past the entity's own real state, so a `predictsFuture === false` entity (a bullet, a casing, a fragment) never enters either set and is never folded; the ones that do fold their `id`, which is what makes an entity joining or leaving the set (an asteroid placed or removed, a ship added, an enemy destroyed) change the revision. `displayState(t)` is `null` past an entity's prediction horizon, so a growing prediction makes that body *appear* in both sets at times it was absent from; the raw tip would therefore change every frame and defeat the cache, so each entity contributes only **whether its prediction reaches `planEnd`** — a value that holds still for the whole of the growth and changes exactly once, at the moment coverage is achieved (monotone, so it cannot oscillate; a truncated prediction never reaches it and stays put; a discarded and regrown one moves covered→short→covered and re-integrates correctly). An entity with no prediction at all contributes a third value distinct from both. The deliberate cost is that a body growing into the plan's span is not reflected in the drawn arc until its prediction covers the span outright — the final shape is right, the intermediate frames are slightly stale. A quantized tip instead makes the growth itself visible to the cache, and at a 28-day display duration coverage takes many frames to reach, so every one of those frames re-integrates the whole plan. The fold is a 32-bit `Math.imul` mix with `| 0` on every input, so the result is always a finite integer — a `NaN` revision would compare unequal to itself and re-integrate forever, silently. `planEnd` is the trailing edge of the polyline drawn on the *previous* frame (`PlanEditor`'s own private `lastPlanEnd` → `PlanPath.timeRange()`), since `PlanEditor.update` resolves the provider before the same call's `planDisplay.update` decides this frame's segments; an unresolved `planEnd` (nothing drawn yet) yields a value that differs on every call, so the first frame always integrates.


- `plan-arc.ts` — `PlanArc`: **the integration result for one segment of the plan — it owns no drawing** (the folded-line mesh is `plan-path.ts`'s own pooled `TrajectoryLine`, see above). `state0`/`sourceRevision`/`apsisCenterId` are frozen at construction, and the constructor itself seeds a `physics/dynamic-trajectory.ts` `DynamicTrajectory` at `state0` and integrates synchronously up to the requested `end` (the private `integrateTo`) — so a freshly-built `PlanArc` is immediately readable with no separate call needed, and the plan reuses the same "advance a timed state while recording decimated samples" machinery every entity uses rather than a second implementation of it. Its step size is `localOrbitPeriod(r, attractors) / PLAN_ARC_STEPS_PER_REV` (=100, `attractor.ts`'s `localOrbitPeriod` against that step's own `attractorsNear` result — see `simulation/attractors.ts` above) off the current radius, so fixing the step count per revolution keeps accuracy uniform regardless of altitude, eccentricity, or which body the segment is currently orbiting; **the cumulative step count across the arc's whole life — construction plus every later `setEnd`-driven continuation, tracked in the private `steps` field — is capped at `PLAN_ARC_MAX_STEPS`** (20000) as a safety bound — a segment with a deep or fast periapsis pass shrinks that local-radius-derived step size and could otherwise take an unbounded number of steps to cover its full duration — and hitting the cap sets `truncated`, after which every later `integrateTo` call is a no-op, truncating the segment the same way reentry does. With `PLAN_ARC_STEPS_PER_REV` fixed, this cap converts directly into a maximum revolution count per segment (≈200) regardless of which PREDICT duration is selected — see `DEVELOP/SPEC.md` for the measured truncation distances. A lower step-size floor to force a long segment to finish drawing regardless was rejected: it lets RK4 diverge into a fabricated trajectory instead of an accurate but truncated one. **The gravity sources and collision bodies are resolved per step, by the `plan-attractors.ts` `PlanAttractors` `PlanEditor` owns** — `integrateTo` asks it at the step's start, midpoint and end, and that provider answers each distinct time by drawing `Ephemeris.attractorsAt(t)` once and building both the collision set and the gravity subset from that single window, holding the derived `{gravity, collision, collisionById, classified}` under its own time key. Body positions therefore stay per-step accurate — a segment near the Moon integrates against the Moon where it actually is, and `findImpact` still interpolates each body between the step's own two endpoints — while the repeated work (a step's end time is the next step's start time) costs nothing. Resolving the gravity window separately from the collision window, as a second `Ephemeris` call at the same `t`, is what made a long segment unaffordable: it evaluated every gravity-bearing body twice per time, and a 20,000-step segment paid that twice for every one of its steps. Decimation is `DynamicTrajectory`'s own `sampleInterval` (segment length / `PLAN_ARC_MAX_SAMPLES`) and `keepDuration` (the segment length integrated so far, which grows across continuation the same way `end` does), the polyline's points are the `samples` getter — `samplesOldestFirst()` clipped to the arc's current `end` and memoized against `(that array's own reference, end)`, so a read that needs no clipping keeps returning the exact same array `plan-path.ts`'s pooled `TrajectoryLine.syncGeometry` reads by reference (see `game/trajectory-line.ts` below for why that identity matters) — and `at(t)` is the queue's Hermite interpolation, itself refusing anything past `end`. `impactPoint()`/`periapsisPoint()`/`apoapsisPoint()` are the state at which the integration reached a body's surface / the first periapsis found / the first apoapsis found, each answering `null` once its own state's time falls past the arc's current `end` — a segment narrowed by `setEnd` must not go on reporting an extremum that now lies beyond what it answers for, even though the underlying integration that found it is still there; `impactPoint()`'s state is what `plan-display.ts` turns into the `✕` marker, and the arc itself simply ends there. The step that first lands inside the body is backsolved to the exact surface crossing via `physics/sphere-contact.ts`'s `sweptSphereToi` between that step's start and end position — a body's radius can be tens of km, and a single step near a fast, steep approach can otherwise land well past the surface, which would print an altitude reading deep in the negative rather than ~0. The constructor's `apsisCenter: Attractor | null` argument (`PlanPath` passes a body only for the trailing segment, `null` for every other) is what the arc's periapsis/apoapsis are measured against: right alongside the existing surface-impact check, each step pair (`trajectory.prevState`/`.state`) is handed to `physics/trajectory-features.ts`'s `apsisCrossing(apsisCenter, prev, next)`, and the first periapsis and first apoapsis found (independently — a segment can find one without the other) are kept as `periapsisState`/`apoapsisState` for the arc's whole life — a `setEnd` continuation never resets them, since only a wholesale replacement (a fresh `PlanArc`) starts that search over. Checking on the live step pair rather than the decimated `samples` is what makes a genuine collision-course segment (radial velocity never crosses zero on the way to impact) correctly report `periapsisPoint() === null` — there is no periapsis on that course. `apsisCenter === null` skips the check outright, so the segments nobody queries for Pe/Ap pay nothing for it. Drag is on at `SHIP_BCINV` — the planner only ever plans the player's own orbit, so it integrates with the player's own ballistic coefficient — and integration stops when the state sinks into any body's surface (`attractor.ts`'s `hitCelestialBody`, margin `REENTRY_ALT`) or goes non-finite, in which case `endState()` (`= at(this.end)`) is `null`. **`represents(state0, end, sourceRevision, apsisCenterId, tracksLiveAnchor)` is what `plan-path.ts`'s `PlanPath.update` asks each frame to decide whether this instance can go on answering for a segment, rather than being replaced outright**: `sourceRevision`/`apsisCenterId` must match exactly (a change in what the provider will answer, or in which body Pe/Ap are measured against, invalidates the arc exactly as an edit to the segment itself does), and the coarsest decimation floor the already-integrated samples were recorded under (the private `decimation`, the largest `segment length / PLAN_ARC_MAX_SAMPLES` any `integrateTo` call has used) must not exceed the newly-requested floor by more than `PLAN_ARC_MAX_SAMPLE_COARSENING` (=8) — otherwise narrowing a segment's `end` after a long display duration would leave only a handful of samples in the shortened range and turn the folded-line's click candidates from continuous into sparse points. **The comparison is floor against floor, never the samples' achieved interval against a floor**: the achieved interval is also bounded below by the step size (`PLAN_ARC_STEPS_PER_REV`, ≈ one revolution / 100), which a rebuild reproduces identically, so measuring it against a floor derived from `PLAN_ARC_MAX_SAMPLES` (=2000) makes the test unsatisfiable at the `'orbit'` preset — where the requested floor is one revolution / 2000 — and rebuilds the whole segment every frame over a coarseness no rebuild can reduce. Below those two gates, an unchanged `state0` (reference equality) always represents, whatever `end` moved to; a changed `state0` only can while `tracksLiveAnchor` (`PlanPath` passes `true` only for the sole segment while the plan has no nodes — the only case where `state0` follows the live ship every frame), where identity comparison alone is useless (`state0` is a fresh object every frame) and even the segment length isn't exactly repeatable under the `'orbit'` preset — the ship's osculating period drifts continuously under J2/drag — so the anchor's time drift is compared against the current sample interval (segment length / `PLAN_ARC_MAX_SAMPLES`): below it the drawn polyline wouldn't visibly change, so the existing integration is kept (extended by `setEnd`, not rebuilt) with `state0` staying frozen at whatever it was originally built from rather than re-seeding at the newer, barely-different state; a real duration-preset change is orders of magnitude larger than that threshold and is always caught immediately. That threshold-based tolerance assumes the anchor is the *same* orbit merely ticking forward, which doesn't hold across a discontinuous re-anchor (`ActivePlayerController.set` to a different ship, a dock launch, a collision's `state` overwrite) — the module-private `anchorJumped(prev, next)` is what catches those first, and it compares the *states*, not their times: a swap routinely lands at a later `t` than the last recompute, so time ordering is no proxy at all. Continuous propagation over `dt` can move a state by at most its own speed times `dt`, so a position difference beyond `ANCHOR_JUMP_SPEED_MARGIN` (=2) times that reachable distance (plus `ANCHOR_JUMP_MIN_DIST` = 1 m of slack at `dt` ≈ 0) cannot be explained by propagation, and forces `represents` to answer false whatever the thresholds say.
