When `represents` answers false (or no arc exists yet at that index), `PlanPath.update` builds a fresh `PlanArc(state0, end, provider, apsisCenter)` — the constructor's synchronous integration, the expensive path — and drops the old instance outright; when it answers true, `PlanPath.update` instead calls the existing instance's `setEnd(end)`, which moves the terminus it answers for (`_end`, exposed as the `end` getter) and only continues integrating (via `integrateTo`) when the already-integrated tip still falls short of the new `end` by at least a sample interval — a narrowing `end` costs nothing beyond that comparison, since the underlying `DynamicTrajectory` and its `steps`/`truncated`/impact/apsis state are kept exactly as they were, with `samples`/`at`/`endState`/`impactPoint`/`periapsisPoint`/`apoapsisPoint` all clipping their answers to the current `end` on read rather than the integration itself being cut back. A reusable per-segment unit — knows nothing about `Plan` — so `PlanPath` composes several of these, and their matching pooled lines, rather than predicting the whole multi-node polyline in one shot.
- `node-gizmo.ts` — `NodeGizmo`: the maneuver-node DOM pointer-events layer — node handles + Δv axis arms + the node context menu (warp/delete). A plan concern; uses the shared `hud/context-menu.ts`. An axis-arm drag accumulates its total screen-space projection (`totalProj`) from pointerdown; once `|totalProj|` exceeds `DV_DRAG_LATCH_PX` the drag latches — `onAxisDrag` stops firing per-move deltas and the public `latch: AxisLatchState | null` field instead exposes the axis/sign and the pixel excess past the threshold, for `PlanEditor.updateEditing` to read and integrate every frame at its own rate (see `plan-editor.ts` above). `latch` is cleared and the latched flag reset on pointerup/pointercancel.
- `plan-gizmo-3d.ts` — `PlanGizmo3D`: `NodeGizmo`'s 3D counterpart — six world-space arrow meshes (PRO/RET blue, NRM/ANM green, OUT/IN red), one `THREE.Group` at `renderOrder = 999` so the gizmo always draws on top of the scene it's pointing into. It holds no pointer/drag logic of its own and never references `NodeGizmo`; the two stay decoupled the same way the DOM/3D split works everywhere else in `plan/` — `PlanEditor.syncGizmo` is what reads `NodeGizmo`'s `latch`/`activeAxis` state each frame and translates it into this class's own `setStretch(axis, sign, factor)` call, so a drag on the 2D arm and the matching 3D arrow's stretch are two independent pushes from one shared frame of truth rather than one gizmo driving the other. `setPositionAndRotation(pos, pro, nrm, radOut, scale)` places the whole group at the selected node and aligns its local axes (X=RAD, Y=PRO, Z=NRM) to the orbital basis at that node, with `scale` supplied by the caller from the current camera distance so the gizmo holds a constant apparent size regardless of zoom. `setVisible`/`setPositionAndRotation`/`setStretch` are plain setters pushing already-computed values into mesh transforms — sync-shaped by what they do, even though (unlike most of this codebase) they aren't named with the `sync` verb.

- `plan.ts` — `Plan`: the node list plus the `anchor` (the plan's starting state). **Both are plain `KinematicState`s: a node *is* its frozen post-burn absolute state, not a relative Δv** — the burn time is the state's own `t`, and Δv is the derived value (node `v` − arrival velocity). The node list is a single `KinematicState[]`, always kept in execution-time order by construction: `addNode(postState, from)` finds the insertion point (the count of existing nodes whose `t` is earlier), truncates from there, then pushes — so the added node always lands at the end and its returned index is stable. **The anchor and the node list are one value, `{anchor, nodes} | null`, and `null` is exactly "no nodes"** — a plan with no node has no start of its own, since that start is the live ship's own state, so `Plan` refuses to hold it. **That `null` never surfaces as an anchor a caller has to substitute for**: every way of reading the start takes the state to borrow when nothing is frozen (`anchorOr(fallback)`, the single statement of the rule, which `displayData(shipState)`/`addNode`/`nodeTimeRange` all route through), so no caller ever writes `?? ship.state` itself — `displayData` returns the folded-line material `PlanData`, whose `anchor` is therefore never `null`, and `nodeTimeRange` likewise always answers a range. The one method that does report the absence is `frozenData(): PlanData | null`, whose `null` means "there is no plan to persist" — the save path's own question, not a rule to re-implement. `addNode`'s `from` is what freezes the anchor, and only when there is no node yet; from then on prediction is a pure function of anchor + nodes and never reads `player` state. **`consumeNodesUpTo(t, actualState)` is the one way a node is ever consumed** (`PlanGuide.update` at `simTime - C.NODE_EXPIRE_GRACE`, `PlanExecutor.finish` at cutoff, `CreativeStage.applySimulationEvents` for an `'instant'` ship): it removes every node whose execution time is at or before `t`, returns how many it removed, and — **whenever any node is still queued behind it** — **re-anchors the plan on `actualState`** — where the ship actually ended up, never the node's ideal value. With nothing left queued the anchor goes with the nodes, since there is no longer a plan for it to start. A powered burn does not achieve its planned Δv exactly, and folding that residual away would make the divergence between plan and reality unreadable on screen; `'instant'` execution passes the consumed node itself, since a teleport has no error to keep. The remaining plan is still drawn from a valid start and the already-flown segment of `PlanPath` disappears on its own. `addNode` refuses a state at or before the anchor's own time (returning `-1` rather than inserting), since a node there would break the "a node is never earlier than the state it follows" invariant at the very first node — `PlanEditor` turns that `-1` into a hint instead of a silent no-op. Editing a node discards downstream nodes (`removeNode` trims from `idx`, and `replaceNode` — which `applyNodeDv` delegates to — trims everything past it), which is what keeps the module's one invariant true: **a node is never placed later than its predecessor's drawn segment reaches** (node *i*−1, or the anchor for node 0). `nodeTimeRange(idx, from, ephemeris, displayDuration)` is the single statement of that rule and `replaceNode`'s precondition — with it holding, node times are already ordered, so `replaceNode` neither sorts nor moves the node's index. `applyNodeDv` discards downstream nodes unconditionally, with no zero-Δv guard — a caller that wants to skip a no-op edit (`PlanEditor.applyDv`) checks for that itself before calling, rather than `Plan` special-casing a magnitude it has no reason to inspect. The module-level `orbitPeriodOf(state, attractors)` (`attractor.ts`'s `orbitalElementsOf`'s `OrbitalElements.period`, around whichever body pulls hardest at `state` — never a plan-level setting — falling back to `APERIODIC_ARC_DURATION` when no finite period exists, e.g. hyperbolic orbits) feeds `segmentDurationFrom(state0, attractors, displayDuration)` — the shared definition of "how long a segment starting at this state may run," which `nodeTimeRange` and `plan-path.ts`'s `buildSegments` both call rather than each deriving it separately, so the legal node range and the drawn polyline can never disagree. `displayDuration` is typed as the narrow `DisplayDurationSource` interface (`{ durationSec(referencePeriod): number }`) rather than the concrete `DisplayWindowManager`, so this module — and the no-DOM `tsconfig.test.json` that compiles it directly for `test:physics` — never has to resolve `DisplayWindowManager`'s DOM-heavy dependency chain. No prediction cache lives here. `revision` is the plan's own edit counter — bumped by every call that actually changes the anchor or the node list (`addNode`/`removeNode`/`replaceNode`/`consumeNodesUpTo`/`clear`, each only on the path where it really did change something, and `applyNodeDv` only through the `replaceNode` it delegates to), and it is held outside the `{anchor, nodes}` value so that emptying and refilling the plan still leaves it monotonically increasing (a counter that reset would collide as a cache key); a nodeless plan's start following the live ship is not an edit and bumps nothing — the drift/length thresholds in `plan-arc.ts` are what judge that case. **Each `Player` owns its own `Plan`** (`readonly plan = new Plan()`), which is what lets a multi-ship stage like CREATIVE keep each ship's plan independent of the others.
- `plan-executor.ts`/`plan-executor-math.ts` — `PlanExecutor`: drives a `'powered'` ship's maneuver node through a state machine (idle → slew → armed → burn → trim → idle), owned one-per-`Player` (`readonly planExecutor: PlanExecutor`) the same way `plan: Plan` is. It receives the ship/HUD/sim-speed it acts on as `PlanExecutorShip`/`PlanExecutorHud`/`PlanExecutorSimSpeed` — narrow structural types covering only the members it actually reads/writes, **not** `Player`/`Hud`/`SimSpeedManager` themselves (the same reason `plan.ts` has `DisplayDurationSource` instead of importing `DisplayWindowManager`): `Player` drags in `three/webgpu` and DOM types transitively, and importing it — even `import type` — would still force the whole reachable graph through `tsc -p tsconfig.test.json`'s DOM-less, `three`-less compiler options, breaking `test:physics`'s ability to compile this file at all. `Player` itself is passed at every real call site without a cast, since it's a structural superset of `PlanExecutorShip`. `PlanExecutionMode` (`'off' | 'instant' | 'powered'`) is defined here, not on `Player`, for the same reason — `player.ts` re-exports it (`export type { PlanExecutionMode }`) rather than defining its own copy. **Node identity is tracked by reference (`targetNode: KinematicState | null`), never by `node.t`**: `Plan.applyNodeDv`/`replaceNode` replace a node with a new `KinematicState` at the *same* `t` (Δv is applied to the existing node, not appended as a new one), so comparing `t` alone would read a mid-burn Δv edit as "still the same node" and let the burn keep going in its old, now-stale direction. Every entry point (`update`/`applyIgnitionAndCutoff`/`nextEventTime`) re-checks `node !== this.targetNode` — a stale reference stops `applyIgnitionAndCutoff`/`nextEventTime` outright (they no-op until `update` has re-synced `targetNode` to the new node next frame), and `update` itself routes an identity change through the same `reset()`/`clearState()` every other "stop what's in flight" path uses, so `ship.thrust`/`ship.torque` can never survive across a node swap into the new node's (unrelated) burn.

  **Attitude is held inertial-fixed, not chased**: while idle/slew/armed, the target forward is `norm(node.v − ship.state.v)`, re-derived every `update()` call so it tracks the moving target right up to ignition; at ignition it's frozen into `burnDirWorld` (and a matching `burnUpWorld`) and never recomputed for the rest of the burn — a real high-thrust/short-duration burn is closer to an impulsive shot than a continuously-corrected one. The up reference defaults to the ship's own radial-out `state.r`, but `plan-executor-math.ts`'s `burnUpReference` falls back to the orbit normal (`kinematic-state.ts`'s `orbitAxes(state).nrm`) whenever the burn direction is itself ~radial — `r` and the orbit normal are orthogonal by construction, so a burn direction can never be singular against both at once, which is what keeps a pure radial-in/out node from deadlocking `qFromForwardUp` (angle stuck at 180°, torque stuck at zero, `slew` never exits) the way using `r` alone would. `updateBurnOutput` (the `burn`/`trim` half of `update`) keeps torquing toward the *frozen* `burnDirWorld`/`burnUpWorld` for the whole burn — attitude authority is never silently dropped mid-burn — separately from whether thrust is currently flowing.

  **A ship is not considered "close enough" to a node just because its current velocity happens to be near the node's target velocity** — on a periodic orbit, that coincidence recurs every revolution, not only right before the actual burn. `update()` gates the whole idle/slew/armed/finish path behind an approach window computed off the *current* Δv and attitude error, `C.NODE_APPROACH_LEAD + burnDurationFor(dvMag, accel) + turnTimeFor(errRad, C.MAX_ANG_ACCEL)` (`plan-executor-math.ts`): outside it, `update()` zeroes `ship.torque` and returns without evaluating alignment or the "Δv already ~0" finish condition at all, so a return visit to the same phase a revolution early can never be mistaken for the node's own approach. `turnTimeFor` is a bang-bang-control time estimate (`2√(θ/α)`) for the current alignment error — without it, the window only ever covered `NODE_APPROACH_LEAD` (10s) plus the burn itself, so a small-Δv node that happens to need close to a full reversal of attitude wouldn't leave `slew` enough real time to finish turning before the computed ignition instant, arriving late and burning asymmetrically around the node time. `C.NODE_APPROACH_LEAD` itself is left untouched (it's shared with `plan/plan-guide.ts`'s combat-view approach hint) — the turn-time term is `PlanExecutor`'s own addition on top.

  `applyIgnitionAndCutoff` opens with an `!ship.alive` check that calls `reset` (the same teardown a `planExecution !== 'powered'` ship gets in `update`) and returns — a ship destroyed mid-burn is no longer simulated (`GameEntity.stepSim` self-gates on `alive`), so nothing this class writes reaches the integrator either way, but without the check the invariant "burn state only exists while genuinely burning" would be false for the rest of that frame's substeps.

  `update(ship, simDt, simTime, simSpeed)` (the *simulation* clock — `CreativeStage.update` derives `simDt = dt × simSpeed.simSpeed` itself, the same way `EntityManager.updatePlayers` does, because the fuel this call consumes has to be proportional to the span the resulting thrust is integrated over; called for *every* `entities.players` entry, not just the active one, since `Player.behave` — which would otherwise drive this — only runs for the operated ship) is also the sole place that keeps `ship.thrust` correct across an entire frame for the *active* ship: `Player.behave` runs before `CreativeStage.update` each frame and unconditionally overwrites `this.thrust` (to `null`, absent manual input) via `PlayerThrottle.updateThrustState` — if only `applyIgnitionAndCutoff` (below) wrote `ship.thrust`, that write would land mid-`Simulator.advance`, survive only until the *next* frame's `behave()` call nulls it again, and the ship would integrate with `thrust = null` for the first substep of every single frame. `update()` (and `updateBurnOutput` within it) re-asserts `ship.thrust` unconditionally every frame after `behave()` has run and before `Simulator.advance` — gated behind `simSpeed.canShipAct`, which every entry point (`update`/`applyIgnitionAndCutoff`/`nextEventTime`) takes as an argument of its own rather than caching — a cached copy would let two entry points reached at different moments of the same frame disagree about whether the ship may burn at all — since at high time-warp a substep can be tens of seconds long and an ungated single-substep 400 m/s² burn would deliver an absurd, uncontrolled Δv in one step. **`update`'s gate sits at the very top, ahead of the node and phase checks**, so a closed gate writes nothing at all — not even the `slew`/`armed` alignment torque, which is what would otherwise reach the integrator during a warp: this class writes `thrust`/`torque` from outside `Player.behave`, so it is the one module besides `EntityManager.updatePlayers` that has to answer "may this ship be commanded right now" for itself, and it doing so is what makes a post-hoc fold over every ship after the fact unnecessary. While burning, `update` also re-derives the output stage (full thrust, or `THROTTLE_LEVELS[0]` "trim" once the projected residual drops under `C.PLAN_EXECUTOR_TRIM_DV`) and the fuel-limited `pendingAccel`. `applyIgnitionAndCutoff(ship, simTime, simSpeed)` — called every `Stage.applySimulationEvents` boundary (`Simulator`'s existing per-substep event contract — see `simulator.ts`/`stage.ts` below) — is the simTime-precise half and **also** writes `ship.thrust`/`ship.torque` at the instant it ignites (armed → burn, freezing `burnDirWorld`/`burnUpWorld`), so the remaining substeps left in the *current* frame (after the event boundary but before the next `update()` call) see thrust immediately rather than waiting a frame: it only actually ignites once `simTime` reaches the closed-form `ignitionTimeFor(node.t, dv, accel)` (placed symmetrically around the node's own time so the burn is centered on it), and only cuts off (`finish`: `ship.thrust = null`, `ship.torque = v3()`, **`ship.plan.consumeNodesUpTo(node.t, ship.state)`** — re-anchoring the plan on the *actual* (imperfect) post-burn state rather than the node's ideal one, so the residual stays visible in the rest of the plan's display — then a one-shot `hud.hint` reporting that residual Δv) once `burnCutoffProjection(node.v, ship.state.v, burnDirWorld) <= 0` — the *signed projection* of the remaining Δv onto the frozen burn direction, not its raw magnitude, since a magnitude threshold would oscillate right at the crossing (the projection instead falls monotonically through zero and never comes back). `nextEventTime(ship, simTime, simSpeed)` mirrors the same two closed-form/live-projection computations (returning `null` outright while the gate is closed, since neither ignition nor cutoff can actually happen then) so `Simulator.advance`'s substep sizing lands exactly on the predicted ignition or cutoff instant regardless of frame rate — `applyIgnitionAndCutoff` is still safe to call every substep besides (both its ignition and cutoff checks are live-state comparisons, not "was this the scheduled instant" checks), so an off-target substep boundary from an unrelated event just no-ops here. A gate that closes mid-burn **aborts the burn** rather than pausing it: `burnDirWorld` is an inertially frozen direction, an approximation that only holds over the seconds between ignition and cutoff, and after a time-warped coast neither the ship's position nor its velocity is the one it was frozen for. Every exit path (`finish`/fuel-exhausted/mode-off/node-swap/gate-closed, plus `stopIfActive`'s no-op-if-idle wrapper) funnels through the private `clearState`, the one place `ship.thrust`/`ship.torque` are actually zeroed — so no path can leave a stale command behind. `plan-executor-math.ts` holds the closed-form/projection/up-reference functions with no `Player`/THREE dependency, which is what lets them (and `plan-executor.ts` itself) compile under `tsconfig.test.json` for `test:physics` the same way `plan.ts` does.
- `plan-guide.ts` — `PlanGuide`: the combat-view execution guide for the nearest node, split across the two frame phases. `update(player, simTime, editMode, attractors)` (`Game.update`) drops overdue nodes via `player.plan.consumeNodesUpTo(simTime - C.NODE_EXPIRE_GRACE, player.state)`, then — once the nearest remaining node enters its execution window (`simTime >= node.t - C.NODE_APPROACH_LEAD`) — notifies approach and, once the player's and the node's strongest attractor agree and the module-level `orbitalElementsClose` also holds between the player's own elements (`player.orbitalElementsAround(strongestAttractor(player.state.r, attractors))`) and the node's target elements (`orbitalElementsOf(node, strongestAttractor(node.r, attractors))`), achievement — a node placed near a different attractor than the one the player currently orbits can never read as achieved, since comparing elements taken around different centers is meaningless; node consumption is purely time-based (`consumeNodesUpTo`), so the center check and `orbitalElementsClose` only gate the achievement hint. Each hint fires once per node: private `approachNotified`/`achievedNotified` hold the last-notified node and are compared by **identity**, since editing a node always replaces it with a new `KinematicState`. `sync(player, simTime, editMode, project)` (`Game.sync`) only places the ◈NODE/⬡BURN markers for that same nearest node; the planned polyline itself is `PlanDisplay`'s, drawn in combat view too. Taking `player: Player | null` directly rather than a separate `Plan` argument means the plan is read off `player.plan`, and `player === null` (no ship flown) simply notifies/draws nothing, so no caller needs to guard the call itself. `PlanGuide` takes only `Hud`/`UiSfx`/`MarkerManager` — auto-warp cancellation near the node is `SimSpeedManager`'s own concern.
- `plan-editor.ts` — `PlanEditor`: **`plan` is a getter forwarding to `activePlayers.current?.plan`** (`Plan` is owned per-`Player`, not by `PlanEditor` — see `player.ts` above), so editing always targets whichever ship is currently active, and **every internal read of the plan goes through that same getter** — the private sibling `ship` getter (`activePlayers.current`) is reached only where the ship's own state or attitude is what's wanted (a new node's `from`, `nodeTimeRange`'s fallback, `fineAttitude`, the equatorial-node markers, the ship-swap check), never as a route to `plan`. `PlanEditor` holds the shared `ActivePlayerController` by reference (a constructor argument, right after `markerManager`) rather than a `Player` of its own — the DOM pointer-event handlers read `activePlayers.current` fresh on every access too. `update()` compares `activePlayers.current` against the previous frame's ship (private `lastSeenShip`) and, on a change, closes the now-stale open menu (`closeMenu()`) — the selected node needs no such explicit clearing, since it resolves by identity against `plan.nodes` every read (below) and a plan swap alone drops it to "none" on its own. `plan` is therefore `Plan | null` — a ship owns its own plan, so with no ship (e.g. CREATIVE before any ship is placed) there is no plan to edit, and no stand-in empty `Plan` is kept in its place: `handleMapPointer` self-gates on `!editMode || activePlayers.current === null` rather than trusting `Game.handlePointerInput` (which calls it unconditionally, alongside every other pointer receiver) to have already checked either, and `update`/`sync` both read the private `displayedPlan` getter (`PlanData`, below) rather than assuming one exists. `PlanEditor` otherwise owns the `editMode` flag, the node selection (held as the selected `KinematicState` itself, exposed as a `selectedNodeIdx` get/set pair that resolves it against `plan.nodes` by identity every read — a node is immutable and every edit replaces it, so an index would silently come to mean a different node whenever the list shifted, while a reference either still resolves or reports the selection as gone, covering deletion, downstream truncation and consumption with one rule), the `NodeGizmo`, a single `panel: PlanPanel` (the 軌道計画 HUD panel's DOM, including the 6 `HoldButton`s (`dvButtons`) — see `plan-panel.ts` below), and `planDisplay` (below) — editing and future-display are both "what this plan is/looks like", so one owner. The private `dvEditActive` (`editMode && selectedNodeIdx !== null`) is what `updateEditing` gates itself on, and nothing outside this class reads it: while it holds, `updateEditing` claims the six Δv keys through `Input.takeHeld`, so WASDQE reach Δv editing instead of real thrust because `Player` sees them as un-pressed — not because a flag told it to stand aside. `handleInput(input, dt)` takes `K.deleteNode` (→ `clearPlanByKey`) and, only while `!editMode` (the same stand-aside rule the Δv keys follow), `K.autoWarpToNode` (→ `simSpeedManager.toggleAutoWarpToFirstNode(plan?.firstNode(), simTime)`) ahead of `updateEditing`. Running in the input phase (from `handleInput`, ahead of `Game.advanceSimulation`) is what makes the Δv-key claim land before `Player.behave` reads the same keys; it also means the ramp and the plan can be edited while paused, which is what the input phase is for. While `dvEditActive` is false the method resets every direction's hold time to 0 and returns, so the rate ramp always restarts from the beginning when a node is selected. `update(displayWindow)` (`Game.update`, before `MapPickables.refresh` so the apsis icons it emits are this frame's) hands this frame's inputs to the `plan-attractors.ts` `PlanAttractors` it owns (`resolve(...)`: the operated ship's id as the sole excluded id, `plan.revision`, the private `lastPlanEnd`, `displayWindow.simTime`) first — that call is what drops the held sources if and only if the folded revision moved — then forwards `displayedPlan` — the private getter that folds "is there a ship" and "is there anything to show" into one `PlanData | null` (`null` when there is no ship, or when the plan holds no node outside `editMode`; otherwise `plan.displayData(ship.state)`, whose anchor is the frozen one once the plan holds a node and the live ship's own state before that) — and that same `PlanAttractors` straight into `planDisplay.update`, and `sync(cameraSystem, simTime, fo)` (`mapDist` read off `cameraSystem.overviewCamera.dist`) drives `planDisplay.sync(fo, project, scale, overviewMode, cameraPos, camera)`, each pulled off `cameraSystem`, whenever `displayedPlan !== null` — the polyline is drawn in combat view too, since that's where the plan is actually flown; separate calls to `syncGizmo(plan, ...)`/`syncPanel(ship, ...)` — the gizmo needs only the plan, the panel needs the ship (its altitude reference falls back to `plan.anchorOr(ship.state)`) and reads `ship.plan` itself rather than taking both — run only while `ship !== null && editMode`, keeping the node gizmo and the 軌道計画 panel restricted to editing; `planDisplay.hide()` covers the remaining case (not editing, no nodes, or no ship). `displayWindow` arrives as an argument from `game.ts`, resolved via `DisplayWindowManager` — `PlanEditor` never reads "when and in what frame to look" itself. `updateEditing(input, dt)` only accumulates Δv; the panel's DOM writes are `syncPanel`'s. **A node can be created two ways**: `handleMapClick` (left-click on the drawn polyline) calls `plan.addNode` directly on the clicked `planDisplay.path.nearestSample`, while `addNodeAt(t)` turns a *time* into a node by first resolving `planDisplay.path.sampleAt(t)` — hinting instead of doing nothing if the plan doesn't reach that time — and is what `Game`'s "add node here" context-menu action (against a periapsis/apoapsis or relative-AN/DN icon) calls. `handleMapClick` otherwise selects the nearest existing node on a hit, else clears the selection — a click that lands on neither node nor polyline always deselects. Drag-to-retime / node context menu / `[X]` delete (`clearPlanByKey`) all read `planDisplay.path`'s screen picking (`projectPoint`/`nearestSample`) rather than a per-frame argument, since the node drag / context-menu handlers fire on DOM pointer events, outside the frame flow. A node drag picks only among the samples inside `Plan.nodeTimeRange(idx, ship.state, …)`, so the pointer snaps to the nearest *legal* point instead of freezing at the limit. Derived-only values are never stored: per-node Δv is `node.v` minus the arrival velocity, and the arrival states come from `planDisplay.path.arrivalStates()` — the same integration that drew the line, so the panel's Δv and the drawn trajectory can't disagree. `onMapClosed` hides the panel and the gizmo, prunes the trailing run of near-zero-Δv nodes, then clears the selection.

`src/game/stages/`:
- `stage.ts` — `Stage`: the per-stage instance contract (`init`/`update`/`checkWin`/`onWin`/`hudSubStatus`; `isUnlocked` is a static method instead — see below), **the win/lose `phase` and the result screen's content** (private `_phase`/`_result`, exposed as `phase`/`isPlaying`/`result` — `Game` only reads them; `protected decide(phase, result)` is the sole writer of both, so a phase can never end up decided without a `StageResult` to show for it — `onWin`/`recordPlayerLost` build that `StageResult` themselves, and *showing* it, and deciding what happens next, is `Launcher`'s job, not `Stage`'s), the default kill-count win condition, the shared `addPlayer`/`addEnemy`/`behaveAllEnemies` helpers, and the shared `recordEnemyDeath`/`recordPlayerLost` result bookkeeping — `recordPlayerLost` opens with `if (!this.isPlaying) return;`, the same guard `recordEnemyDeath` already had, so a loss that lands after the stage has already resolved (e.g. the player's own ship decaying into reentry the instant the last enemy dies) can't overwrite a `'won'` phase with `'lost'`. Its constructor takes `saved: StageSaveData | undefined` ahead of `StageDeps` — the named tuple `[hud, worldSfx, uiSfx, scene, entities, unlockManager, fx, markerManager, ephemeris, simulator, activePlayers]` — and assigns each argument straight onto its matching protected `_hud`/`_worldSfx`/`_uiSfx`/`_scene`/`_entities`/`_unlockManager`/`_fx`/`_markerManager`/`_ephemeris`/`_simulator`/`_activePlayers` field, since `CreativeStage` (and others) need several of them beyond construction time. **Both per-frame entry points take `player: Player | null`** — a stage runs every frame and the presence of a flown ship is an argument, not a reason to skip the call, which is what lets `CreativeStage` keep its placement preview alive during the very state it exists for (before its own first ship is placed). The campaign stages open their `update` with `if (!this.isPlaying || !player) return;`; the base `sync` passes the ship straight through to `StageStatusPanel.sync`, which folds itself away when there is none. Its per-frame `sync(player, fo, cameraSystem, displayTime, visibilityPolicy)` — `fo` is carried for subclasses that draw their own world-space geometry, currently `CreativeStage`'s preview ellipse — the `StageStatusPanel` it owns (`stage-utils/stage-status-panel.ts`: a three-column layout — left (`hudSubStatus()` text + kills, plus a persistent widget slot any concrete `Stage` can drop a DOM element into via `Stage.addStatusPanelWidget` → `StageStatusPanel.appendLeftWidget` — kept out of the text's own `innerHTML` rebuild so a widget's listeners survive), center (装甲 / エンジン出力 / 温度, with the shutdown ceiling `MAX_HULL_TEMP` shown alongside it, / 電力 in `fmtEnergy` joules-scale units, not a percentage), right (the two radiator deploy buttons) — shown only for stages whose `hudSubStatus()` returns a string; it takes the `Player` reference directly rather than a list of individual readouts. **`sync(player: Player | null, message, kills)` is its only entry point, and this class is `#hud-stagestatus`'s only writer** — `null` folds the panel away *and* drops the held ship, which is what keeps the persistent radiator/solar buttons from firing at a ship that has already been lost; the caller still decides the two conditions the panel can't see (`hudSubStatus() === null`, and map view, where the panel is never shown — the PREDICT bar occupies that same bottom-center strip) and passes `null` for them. The radiator buttons *are* the deploy control (click toggles `RadiatorSystem.toggle`) — each shows the side's key mapping (`ラジエーター左`/`右`, mapped 9/0 — `RadiatorSide.up`/`down` is +X/-X, i.e. port/starboard), deploy state, and `wearOf` as a percentage in its label, plus a `fill` bar behind the label whose *width* shrinks with wear (so the button visibly loses "meat" as the panel takes hits) and whose color turns hot past half wear, else `ACCENT` while deployed. The radiator buttons and both non-center columns are built once as persistent DOM and updated by direct style/`innerHTML` writes on their own containers, unlike the center column which rebuilds its own `innerHTML` wholesale — the split matters because a persistent radiator button keeps its click listener across frames, which a from-scratch rebuild would drop). Game-owned resources arrive as constructor arguments (`StageDeps`, not per-frame ctx) rather than through a later injection step, since every one of them already exists by the time `Game`'s constructor reaches `new stageClass(initialSave?.stage, ...)`; the `Simulator` among them is what lets `CreativeStage` read `simTime` at ship-placement confirm time (a DOM event outside the frame flow) without keeping a copy of it. Each concrete stage owns its own runtime state as instance fields, but the title-screen metadata is **static** instead: `selectLabel`/`selectSub`/`selectLockedSub`/`selectKeys`/`selectGroup`/`hiddenFromSelect` and the `isUnlocked(clearCounts)` method all live on the class itself, declared on the `StageClass` interface alongside `id`/`createEphemeris` — the title screen (`stage-select.ts`) and `UnlockManager` both need them before any instance exists. `Stage`'s own static async `createEphemeris(phaseOffsets)` is the default every stage inherits: it resolves the launch's ephemeris profile (`ephemeris-profile.ts`'s `profileAt(SIM_EPOCH_JD_TDB)`), fetches and verifies its precise pack (`ephemeris-catalog.ts`'s `loadAbsoluteEphemeris`), and returns `new Ephemeris(undefined, undefined, SIM_EPOCH_ET, phaseOffsets, pack, SIM_EPOCH_JD_TDB)` — `main.ts` awaits it before `Game` exists (see `main.ts` above), so `Game`'s constructor receives an already-built `Ephemeris` rather than the registry/pack pieces to build one itself; `StageDebugAltSystem` overrides it to build its own invented registry synchronously instead (see below). `Stage` supplies static defaults for `selectLockedSub`/`hiddenFromSelect`/`selectGroup`/`isUnlocked`, but deliberately leaves `selectLabel`/`selectSub`/`selectKeys` undeclared — assigning a concrete class into `stage-dictionary.ts`'s `STAGE_CLASSES: readonly StageClass[]` (below) then fails to compile if one of those three is missing, the same exhaustiveness role an `abstract` instance member would play. `selectGroup` names the tab a stage is listed under and nothing else reads it, so no behavior ever branches on one of them. **A new game's whole starting world is the stage's own `init(entities)`, including its ship** — `protected` and deliberately **not** abstract, returning `void` (it mutates the world, so it hands nothing back; a stage that wants to print its enemy count in the briefing reads `scoreCounter.totalEnemiesSpawned`, which `addEnemy` already counts). The base places nothing, so the least-restricted stage simply doesn't override it. A campaign stage's override opens by placing its ship through `protected addPlayer(init?: PlayerInit): Player` — `new Player(...)` → `entities.addPlayer` → `activePlayers.claimIfNone`, the exact path `CreativeStage.placeObject` also takes — passing `{ammo}` only where the load-out differs from a stock ship (stage0's empty magazines, the debug stages' 20), and `{state}` where the default Earth LEO is meaningless (`StageDebugAltSystem`'s invented registry). `init` is never called directly — `protected begin()` is the sole caller: it returns immediately once the stage was restored from a save (the private `restored` flag, set from whether the constructor's `saved` argument was `undefined`), otherwise calls `init(this._entities)` and then `hud.toast(briefingHtml(), ...)`. **Every concrete stage's constructor calls `super(saved, ...deps)` and ends by calling `this.begin()` itself** — `begin()` cannot run from `Stage`'s own constructor, since a subclass's field initializers and constructor body only run after `super()` returns, and both `init` and `briefingHtml` are subclass-provided; calling it from the base constructor would reach them before the subclass has finished building itself. Three instance capability flags separate the general 0..n-ship form every stage actually runs on from a campaign stage's specialization of it (exactly one ship, lost on destruction) — a lost ship is reclaimed from `players`/the active-player slot the same way, and just as promptly, on every stage (`ActivePlayerController.reclaimDead()`, unconditional — see `active-player-controller.ts` above), so no capability flag is needed for that part: `freeProcurement` (default `false`; `CreativeStage` `true` — `Docking` reads it and passes it straight through to `DockView.open` as its own `freeProcurement` argument, so every dock purchase/repair/refuel there shows and behaves as free), `executesPlans` (default `false`; `CreativeStage` `true` — whether this stage drives `PlanExecutor`/the `'instant'` teleport, which is what `MapContextActions` gates the 軌道計画の実行 item on) and `authoring: ObjectAuthoring | null` (default `null`; `CreativeStage` returns itself — the `openShipPlacer`/`openShipPlacerForDuplicate` pair `MapContextActions`'s place/duplicate items route through, so a stage without a placement panel simply never shows them).
- `stage-dictionary.ts` — the module's whole surface is two exports: `STAGE_CLASSES: readonly StageClass[]` (the title screen's ordering — no `Stage` instance is ever constructed to read metadata, since every launch-time and title-screen static already lives on the class itself) and `findStageClass(id: string | null): StageClass | null` — the single id→class lookup, `null` for an id nothing registers — the `?stage=` param path's own type guard, and what `main.ts`'s `resumableStageClass`, `unlock-manager.ts`, and `hud/save-browser.ts` each resolve an id through. `CreativeStage` sits at the **tail of `STAGE_CLASSES`**, alongside every campaign/debug stage — it is picked by `StageId` (`'creative'`) like any other class and shown on the title screen's クリエイティブモード tab (`Stage.selectGroup`, above) exactly like any other non-`hiddenFromSelect` entry. `StageClass` (the static side every stage class carries — `id`/`createEphemeris`/the title-screen statics/`isUnlocked` plus its constructor signature) lives in `stage.ts`, not here — this module only holds the list and the lookup. The launch-time ephemeris is a static **async factory** rather than declarative data: `Stage.createEphemeris(phaseOffsets)` is the default every stage inherits (build the real solar system's precise pack), and `stage-debug-alt-system.ts`'s `StageDebugAltSystem` is the only override, returning its own invented registry synchronously instead. `main.ts` — not `Game` — is what calls `stageClass.createEphemeris(...)`, before `Game` exists, so a stage that says nothing still gets exactly today's defaults with no branch inside `Game` at all. **How many ships a launch starts with is not a static declaration at all** — every stage begins with an empty world and places whatever it wants in its own `init`, so a campaign stage's single ship and CREATIVE's none are the same mechanism used differently rather than two values of a count nobody could raise above 1. `Stage.stageClass` (a getter reading `this.constructor`) is how a live instance hands back its own class with no id round-trip, and `Stage.id` is just `this.stageClass.id`. `Game`'s constructor is the only place a stage is actually built, and it does so in a single expression with no branch on which stage it is: `new stageClass(initialSave?.stage, this._hud, this._worldSfx, this._uiSfx, this._scene, this.entities, this.unlockManager, this.entities.effects, this.markerManager, this.ephemeris, this.simulator, this.activePlayers)` — `StageDeps` (`stage.ts`, above) already carries `activePlayers`, which `CreativeStage.placeObject` reads as `this._activePlayers.claimIfNone(ship)` after placing a ship, claiming it as the operated one only while none is. The base `init` places nothing and returns 0, so `CreativeStage` (which starts with no ship) simply doesn't override it, and every concrete stage's own trailing `this.begin()` call (see `stage.ts`, above) still fires a fresh CREATIVE launch's briefing toast exactly like a campaign stage's. `Game` itself holds no `instanceof CreativeStage` check anywhere.
- `stage0.ts` / `stage00.ts` / `stage1.ts` / `stage2.ts` / `stage-debug.ts` — the concrete stages: training scoreattack (time-limited), infinite survival (`Stage00`'s own `stage-utils/wave-attack.ts` `WaveAttack` instance drives the wave-spawning — see that file above), the two LEO/Molniya campaign stages, and the debug stage. `StageDebug` places a single enemy group via `wave-attack.ts`'s own `generateWave` (called directly, bypassing `WaveAttack`'s phase machine, from a private `waveCount` field seeded at 2 and again from its own 敵集団をスポーン button), overrides `checkWin()` to always return `false` so testing is never interrupted by a result screen (which also keeps it out of `UnlockManager`'s clear counts), and drops a `ToggleSwitch` for `Enemy.fireEnabled` into the status window's left column via `Stage.addStatusPanelWidget` rather than owning a floating panel of its own. It sets `hiddenFromSelect`, so `stage-select.ts` skips it in the ステージモード tab and reaches it through a small corner link instead.
- `stage-debug-alt-system.ts` — the same `hiddenFromSelect` debug-only shape as `stage-debug.ts`, but its `static async createEphemeris(phaseOffsets)` override returns `new Ephemeris(ALT_REGISTRY, PRIMARY_ID, 0, phaseOffsets)` synchronously (no pack fetch, unlike `Stage`'s own default) — a small invented registry/origin instead of `SOLAR_SYSTEM`/`'earth'` — and `init` overwrites the default LEO initial state (meaningless against an invented `mu`/`radius`) with an orbit around that registry's own body. Exists to demonstrate, and to keep exercising, that the GUI (celestial names, coordinate-system choices, camera focus, lighting/SRP) degrades safely when the active registry has no Earth, no Sun, or nothing `CELESTIAL_BODIES` has a hand-authored view for — the real solar system stays intact in ECI throughout, simply too far away to matter.
- `stage-debug-load.ts` — the same `hiddenFromSelect` debug-only shape as `stage-debug.ts`, existing to reproduce heavy load on demand rather than only when it happens to occur: `init` scatters `DEBUG_LOAD_ASTEROID_COUNT` gravity-bearing `Asteroid`s and `DEBUG_LOAD_DEBRIS_COUNT` non-gravitating `DebrisPiece`s around the player, both placed by a `physics/random.ts` `mulberry32(DEBUG_LOAD_RNG_SEED)` stream so the same layout (and thus the same load) reproduces run to run. **The asteroid field is placed at the real main belt's number density** (`DEBUG_LOAD_ASTEROID_DENSITY` = 3e-29 /m³, ~1.9 million bodies over 1 km across between 2.1 and 3.3 AU): the rocks are 1 km class (`DEBUG_LOAD_ASTEROID_MASS`/`_RADIUS` = 1e12 kg / 500 m), and `DEBUG_LOAD_ASTEROID_MAX_DIST` is *derived* from the count and that density rather than being a chosen length, so the field can't drift into a configuration no star system could hold — 300 bodies at that density fill a sphere of radius 1.3e10 m, i.e. a mean separation of 3.2e6 km. The `DEBUG_LOAD_DEBRIS_COUNT` fragments keep their own much tighter `DEBUG_LOAD_DEBRIS_MAX_DIST` (250 km), since a breakup cloud really is local. Both are sampled uniformly *by volume*. `checkWin()` is always `false`, same as `StageDebug`, so a play session can be held at that load indefinitely.
- `creative-stage.ts` — `CreativeStage extends Stage`: a stage like any other, specializing the general 0..n-ship form the opposite way a campaign stage does — it starts with none and lets the player add/remove them freely — through the same declarations every stage exposes: it simply doesn't override `init`, so the empty world every launch starts from is the world it keeps until the placement panel adds to it, plus instance `freeProcurement = true`/`executesPlans = true`/`authoring = this` (every dock price reads and behaves as free, ships fly their own plans, and objects may be placed/duplicated — see `stage.ts` above; a destroyed ship's slot frees the `MAX_PLACED_SHIPS` cap it sat against the same way it does on every stage, via `ActivePlayerController.reclaimDead()`). `checkWin()` is always `false`, same as `StageDebug`, and `recordPlayerLost` is overridden to just `hud.hint(reason)` instead of the inherited result-screen/`UnlockManager` path, so no result screen or clear-count bookkeeping ever fires either way, win or lose. `hudSubStatus()` returns `''` rather than the base class's `null` — `Stage.syncStatusPanel` only hides `StageStatusPanel` on `null`, so this is what makes the combat-view HP/thrust/temp/power gauges appear on this stage too, with no stage-specific message line (an empty string still shows the kill counter/gauges, just without the leading text a real stage like `stage0.ts`'s countdown would put there). `Stage` is already the one thing `Game` holds exactly one of (win/lose `phase`, per-frame `update`, status panel, `Logistics`), so riding the existing single `activeStage` path costs nothing, whereas a `Game`-level `mode` field would put a branch through the whole of `Game.update`/`sync` — `Game` never learns which `Stage` implementation is running. Its constructor builds the `ShipPlacerPanel` (below) — opened only through `openShipPlacer`/`openShipPlacerForDuplicate` (`MapContextActions`'s 配置/複製 items, themselves offered only in map view — see `map-context-actions.ts` above), not driven by `sync` — right after `super(saved, ...deps)`, then a `WaveAttack` (`stage-utils/wave-attack.ts` above, shared with `Stage00`) seeded from `saved?.waveAttack`, a `waveAttackEnabled` flag (`saved?.waveAttackEnabled ?? false`), and finally its own **設定** panel (`buildCreativeOptionsPanel`, `#hud-creative-options`, docked to the map's right side via `hud/hud-root.ts`'s `hudRail`) before the trailing `this.begin()` call — the `markerManager`/`ephemeris` it needs (building a ship's meshes/markers, and un-baking a moon-centered orbit into ECI) come from `Stage`'s own `_markerManager`/`_ephemeris` fields (see `stage.ts` above). The 設定 panel holds two `ToggleSwitch`s — 補給の自動投入 (`logistics.resupplyEnabled`) and 敵の波状攻撃 (`waveAttackEnabled`) — and, unlike `ShipPlacerPanel`, is shown only while `cameraSystem.overviewMode`, read directly off the `cameraSystem` argument `sync(player, fo, cameraSystem, displayTime, visibilityPolicy)` already takes rather than a separate boolean parameter. `waveAttackEnabled` gates only whether `update` calls `waveAttack.update` to spawn a *new* wave — every already-placed enemy still gets its own AI turn each frame through the unconditional `behaveAllEnemies` call regardless of the toggle, so turning wave-attack off stops the spawner without freezing enemies already on screen. `serialize()` returns a `CreativeStageSaveData` extending the base `StageSaveData` with `waveAttackEnabled` and `waveAttack.serialize()`. **The placement preview is split across the two phases like everything else**: `update` re-derives `preview` (the form's current orbit as `OrbitalElements` + the ship's would-be position, `null` while the panel is closed, in lagrange mode, or when the numbers don't parse) and `sync` pushes it into the white preview ellipse and the ▷ PREVIEW marker. Re-deriving it every frame rather than on a form-change callback is what keeps it honest — the placement is defined at the current `simTime`, which keeps advancing (under warp, fast) while the panel is open, so the panel needs no `onChange` notification at all and the preview can never show an orbit for a moment that has passed. `placeObject` turns a confirmed `ShipPlacerForm` into an `KinematicState` (`buildElementsState` via `elements.ts`'s `stateFromOrbitalElements`/`semiMajorFromPeriod`, or `buildLagrangeState` via `physics/halo.ts`) and dispatches on `form.objectType` (`player`/`enemy`/`ammo`/`base`) — a ship placement (refusing past `MAX_PLACED_SHIPS`) constructs a plain `Player` and calls `entities.addPlayer` directly, the same two calls `Game`'s constructor makes for a campaign stage's ship, so placing a ship here and spawning the flown one on a campaign stage are exactly the same operation; a base placement constructs `game-entity/base.ts`'s `Base` and calls `entities.addBase`. Each branch's fallback name (used when the panel's name field is left blank) is type-specific (`Player-N`/`Enemy-N`/`Ammo-N`/`Base-N`) — the panel itself never invents a name, so the fallback always matches what was actually placed. A placed `Base`'s mesh (`render/ships.ts`'s `buildBaseModel`, tens of meters across) reads as a speck next to LEO distances, which is why every `Base` builds its own `marker/entity-marker.ts` `EntityMarker` in its constructor — the base stays findable at any zoom without this stage knowing anything about it. `update` also drives every `entities.players`' `PlanExecutor.update(ship, dt, simTime, simSpeed)` regardless of which ship is active, and stores the `SimSpeedManager` reference it was handed — `nextSimulationEventTime`/`applySimulationEvents` run at substep boundaries with no arguments of their own, yet must ask the same live thrust gate — `Player.behave` only runs for the operated ship, so a non-active `'powered'` ship's attitude alignment/output-stage selection has nowhere else to happen. Node consumption itself is `nextSimulationEventTime`/`applySimulationEvents`' job (`Stage`'s existing per-substep event-boundary contract, see `stage.ts`/`simulator.ts` below): for an `'instant'` ship, the last node at or before `simTime` (already a post-burn absolute state, so no finite-burn simulation is needed) becomes the ship's new `state` directly (teleport) and is handed to `plan.consumeNodesUpTo(simTime, reached)` as the achieved state; for a `'powered'` ship, `PlanExecutor.applyIgnitionAndCutoff(ship, simTime, simSpeed)` does the same node consumption through its own `finish()` (see `plan/plan-executor.ts` below) — the state is *not* overwritten, so the achieved Δv's error against the plan stays visible. `Player.behave` (AI-free — a placed ship neither fires nor thrusts on its own) runs only for the active ship (`Game.update`'s single `this.player.behave(...)`), so an inactive placed ship costs only integration/attitude/render sync, never belt/thermal/radiator/power.
- `src/game/creative/ship-placer-panel.ts` — `ShipPlacerPanel`/`ShipPlacerForm`: the マップモード-only 艦艇配置 panel `CreativeStage` owns. Two `placementMode`s — `'elements'` (a reference body, an exclusive `sizeMode` tab over 近地点+遠地点/半長軸+離心率/周期+離心率, and 傾斜角/Ω/ω/ν) and `'lagrange'` (系・点・halo/lissajous、面内/面外振幅) — sit in separate DOM groups the panel shows/hides by `placementMode`, so the confirm handler only ever needs one branch's worth of fields. The panel does no physics itself: it collects the raw form values into `ShipPlacerForm` and hands them to `onConfirm` (plus `isOpen`, which `CreativeStage` polls each frame to decide whether to derive a preview — there is no change notification), leaving `stateFromOrbitalElements`/`semiMajorFromPeriod`/`haloState`/`lissajousState` (all in `physics/`) to turn them into an `KinematicState` — same "form owns input, caller owns conversion" split as `plan-editor.ts`'s Δv panel. Both the reference-body list and the Lagrange-system list are built from the same `ORBITING_IDS` — every orbiting body in the active registry (a star is excluded because a ship cannot orbit the thing the ellipse is centered on). `LAGRANGE_DEFAULT_AMPLITUDE_KM: Partial<Record<OrbitingId, {ax, az}>>` only has hand-picked defaults for a few systems; any other system falls back to deriving its default amplitude from that system's own primary-secondary distance times a ratio, so a newly registered gravity source needs no entry here to work.
- `src/game/creative/duplicate-form.ts` — `elementsFormFromState(state, bodies)`: the inverse of `buildElementsState` (above) — takes a live entity's current `KinematicState` and derives the `ElementsForm` that would reproduce its orbit, for the "duplicate" menu item. It's pure inversion of existing geometry, not new orbital math: the reference body is `attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor` (falling back to Earth if that's a star, since the panel's reference-body control never offers one), the osculating elements are `attractor.ts`'s `orbitalElementsOf`, the apsis altitudes are `elements.ts`'s `apsisAltitudes`, and the true anomaly is `trueAnomalyAt` against the center-relative state (`frame.ts`'s `toFrameState`). The one piece with no existing counterpart is recovering Ω/ω from the elements' `hHat`/`pHat` (`raanArgpFromBasis`), since nothing else in the codebase needed to invert `elements.ts`'s `orbitPlaneBasis`; an equatorial orbit's undefined node falls back to Ω=0 by convention. Returns `null` for a hyperbolic/parabolic state (`e >= 1`), since neither an apoapsis altitude nor a meaningful reference body selection exists there — the caller opens the panel as a plain new placement instead. The duplicated true anomaly is **not offset** — the panel opens with the duplicate sitting exactly on top of the original, letting the person placing it re-aim ν (and everything else) before confirming, rather than guessing an offset that would just need to be edited away for the common case of wanting a near-identical clone.
- `stage-utils/logistics.ts` — `Logistics`: ammo-pickup spawn/pickup/despawn logic, shared by all stages via `Stage`'s own constructor, which builds one `Logistics` instance per stage and exposes it as the protected `logistics` field. It builds each `Ammo` with the `MarkerManager` that pickup needs for its own `marker/entity-marker.ts` `EntityMarker`, and holds no marker of its own. Automatic resupply is gated two ways, both checked in `updateLogistics(simTime, player, simSpeed, respawnOnDespawn)`: the public `resupplyEnabled` flag (CREATIVE's own 設定 panel toggle — see `creative-stage.ts` above) and `SimSpeedManager.canResupplyAmmo`. `simSpeed` arrives as a per-frame argument rather than a constructor reference because every caller is a `Stage.update` that already holds it, the same shape `Stage.behaveAllEnemies` uses. Absorption and despawn are deliberately *not* gated — those settle pickups that already exist, a separate question from whether new ones appear — and while resupply is blocked the interval timer is left un-advanced, so re-enabling it resupplies on the next frame instead of carrying the blocked stretch forward as dead time. The stage-scripted one-shot `spawnForPlayer` calls (stage0/stage00/stage-debug's wave and opening drops) bypass both gates: they are scripted events, not periodic resupply, and their wave machines count on the spawn actually happening.
- `stage-utils/score-counter.ts` — `ScoreCounter`: pure shot/hit/kill/loss tallies; result side effects (notifications, win/lose) live on `Stage`.
- `stage-utils/score-attack-timer.ts` — `ScoreAttackTimer`: stage0's countdown.
- `stage-utils/wave-attack.ts` — `WaveAttack`: the 波状攻撃 phase machine (`WaveState` = `waiting_for_ammo` → `spawning_enemies` → `active_combat`) plus the wave-formation generator, factored out so `Stage00` and `CreativeStage` can each own their own instance (constructed from the `hud`/`worldSfx`/`fx`/`scene`/`ephemeris` slice of `StageDeps` it needs, plus an optional trailing `WaveAttackSaveData` — `waveState`/`spawnTimer`/`waveCount` — to resume from). `spawnWave(player: Player, addEnemy, forcedPattern?)` calls the module's own `generateWave(player.state, waveNumber, ephemeris, hud, worldSfx, fx, scene, forcedPattern?)` to pick the wave's ship count/formation/flyby-approach trajectory and hands each built `Enemy` to the caller-supplied `addEnemy` rather than touching `EntityManager` itself, so the same instance works whether the caller wraps it in `Stage.addEnemy`'s `scoreCounter.recordSpawnEnemy()` bookkeeping or calls `entities.addEnemy` bare. `update(dt, player, enemies, simTime, activeStage, addEnemy)` advances the phase machine one frame: `waiting_for_ammo` waits for the player to hold any rounds before toasting and moving to `spawning_enemies`; `spawning_enemies` counts down `STAGE00_SPAWN_DELAY` before the first `spawnWave` and moving to `active_combat`; `active_combat` despawns out-of-range enemies (beyond `STAGE00_MAX_RANGE`, via each `Enemy.despawn`), counts how many distinct `waveId` groups are still alive, and spawns the next wave only once both the concurrent-group cap and the per-wave-count cap (`resolveWaveSpawnLimits`, which loosens as `waveCount` climbs) allow it. `waveCount` is exposed read-only for `Stage00.hudSubStatus()`'s `第N波` readout. `generateWave` is exported on its own too — `stage-debug.ts`'s `StageDebug` calls it directly, bypassing the phase machine entirely, for its initial enemy group and its 敵集団をスポーン button, keeping its own `waveCount` field independent of any `WaveAttack` instance.
- `spawner/enemy-generator.ts` — builds individual `Enemy`s from orbital-element/phasing parameters (phased/coelliptic/crossing/elliptic/Molniya presets).
- `spawner/enemy-spawner.ts` — builds enemy *groups* by calling `enemy-generator.ts` repeatedly; only `generateCluster` (stage0's training cluster) remains here — the wave-attack phase machine and its wave-formation generation live in `stage-utils/wave-attack.ts` (above), shared by `Stage00`/`CreativeStage` rather than kept here alongside the single-shot cluster generator.

- `src/game/sim-speed-manager.ts` — `SimSpeedManager`: the sim-speed stage (`SIM_SPEED_LEVELS`, ×1 to ×131072; shown to the player and referenced in SFX as "warp"), the auto-warp-to-node behavior (`toggleAutoWarpToFirstNode`/`startAutoWarpTo`/`cancelAutoWarp`/`update` — `handleInput` itself only takes `[,]`/`[.]`; `toggleAutoWarpToFirstNode` is called by `PlanEditor.handleInput` for `[N]`, since a maneuver node is `PlanEditor`'s own data), and the **`can*` predicates** (`canShipAct`/`canResolvePhysicalCollisions`/`canResupplyAmmo`) — callers ask these instead of comparing `simSpeed` against `MAX_PHYS_SIM_SPEED` themselves. `canShipAct` answers whether a self-flown ship's own commands (thrust, firing, RCS command torque, autopilot burns) and an enemy's own firing are live at this warp level, and has three readers: `EntityManager.updatePlayers` withholds `input` from every ship while it reads false — **a ship you cannot operate is one state, whether that's because it stopped being the operated ship or because the warp level is past the physical-interaction band**, so both reach `Player.behave` as the same absent `input` and fold through the same path, rather than a warp-specific partial gate that would leave a thrust latch or a held trigger alive across the warp; `Enemy.behave` checks it before firing; and `plan/plan-executor.ts`'s `PlanExecutor`, which writes `thrust`/`torque` from outside `behave`, gates its own three entry points on it directly. Since the gate silently withholds input rather than being reported at the key press, `shift` is what tells the player: raising the level past the band appends the reason to its own `時間加速 ×N` hint, stated once when the capability is actually lost rather than on every blocked keystroke. `canResupplyAmmo` (`Logistics`' gate) is the one predicate with a *different* threshold — `simSpeed === 1`, real time only — because a resupply pickup exists to be flown to and collected, and letting time run while spawning keeps happening would only pile up pickups nobody has approached to collect. `estimatedRealSecondsToWarpEnd(simTime)` answers "how many real seconds until the current auto-warp stops" for the HUD: it can't just divide the remaining sim time by the current speed, since `update`'s own step-selection rule (the largest level at or below `remaining / AUTOWARP_MARGIN`) means the level keeps dropping as the remaining time shrinks — high-multiplier stretches are short and low-multiplier ones are long, so the estimate replays that same step-down rule and sums the real time spent at each level. Returns `null` when no auto-warp is active.
- `src/game/theme.ts` — **the sole definition source for every UI design token**: color (`ACCENT`/`ACCENT_SOFT`/`ACCENT_SECONDARY`/`DANGER` plus the layered `SURFACE_WEAK`/`SURFACE`/`SURFACE_OPAQUE`/`EDGE`/`TEXT_STRONG`/`TEXT`/`TEXT_MUTED`/`TEXT_DIM`, the accent/danger/neutral fill-and-edge ramps `ACCENT_FILL_WEAK`→`ACCENT_FILL_STRONG`/`ACCENT_EDGE_SOFT`/`ACCENT_EDGE`/`DANGER_FILL`/`FILL_1`→`FILL_4`, and `SHADE_1`/`SCRIM`/`BAR_BG`/the glow-mix ratios `GLOW_STRONG`/`GLOW_WEAK`), type scale (`FONT_XXS`→`FONT_3XL`, `FONT_FAMILY`), marker glyph scale (`GLYPH_BASE`/`GLYPH_2_3`/`GLYPH_1_3`/`GLYPH_POI`/`GLYPH_BORESIGHT` — independent of the type scale, since a glyph's apparent size is tuned to its own shape, not to legible text height), and layout constants (`RADIUS_S`→`RADIUS_PILL`, `SPACE_1`→`SPACE_6`, `TRANSITION_FAST`/`TRANSITION_SLOW`, `HIT_TARGET_MIN`). It takes no import of its own, because the UI's look is a declaration in its own right rather than something derived from any other module's needs. **The boundary against `const.ts`'s color section is**: `theme.ts` is the UI's look (HUD panels/windows/buttons/touch controls), `const.ts`'s "色管理" section is the game world's (markers, VFX, orbit lines, hull colors); the two never read each other. `AXIS_PROGRADE`/`AXIS_NORMAL`/`AXIS_RADIAL` are the one color triad that crosses the DOM/3D boundary — `plan/plan-editor.ts`'s Δv panel and `plan/plan-gizmo-3d.ts`'s 3D arrows draw the same three axes and must agree, so both read them off `theme.ts`; a color that only ever appears in the 3D scene (e.g. `plan/plan-path.ts`'s `SEGMENT_COLORS`, anything under `render/`) stays a local constant instead, since nothing forces it to match a DOM value. `injectThemeVariables()` (called once by `hud/hud-root.ts`'s `buildHudDom`, right before it injects the HUD's own `<style>`) writes every token onto `:root` as a kebab-case custom property (`--accent`, `--font-s`, `--space-3`, ...) rather than `#hud`'s own root, because `input/touch.ts`'s virtual pad sits outside `#hud` (directly under `body`, so it isn't covered by `#hud`'s own DOM subtree) and both need to read the same tokens. `SAFE_AREA_TOP`/`_RIGHT`/`_BOTTOM`/`_LEFT` seed `--safe-t`/`--safe-r`/`--safe-b`/`--safe-l` with the static `env(safe-area-inset-*, 0px)` expression at that same injection; `hud/viewport.ts`'s `startViewportTracking` (called once, also from `buildHudDom`) keeps those four live afterward — widened, never narrowed, against `visualViewport`'s own transient inset — and additionally writes `--vvh` (the visual viewport's own height), a token this file defines no constant for since it has no static default to seed. Every HUD/touch/popup CSS string is written in `var(--…)` rather than a literal color/size/radius — a hand-written `#rrggbb` or `rgba()` inside `#hud`'s CSS is a token that got missed, not a legitimate one-off. Code that isn't CSS (an inline `style` write, or a color handed to THREE) reads the same value off `theme.ts`'s TS exports directly rather than through a CSS variable.
- `src/game/vfx/effects-system.ts` / `vfx/flash-effect-manager.ts` — the flash/debris spawn API (`EffectsSystem`, the sole place `Player`/`Enemy`/`PlayerFire`/`Stage` reach to spawn VFX or add debris) and the flashes themselves (`FlashEffectManager`), split across the two phases: `update(dt, simDt)` ages each flash, advects it by its source velocity and drops the expired ones; `sync(fo, camera)` only pushes the survivors' transforms into a shared `render/instanced-pool.ts` `InstancedPool` (`render/billboard.ts`'s `flashResources()` — one glow-textured plane geometry/material for every flash, `C.MAX_FLASHES` capacity, beyond which a flash simply isn't drawn). Because the ageing is on the update side, flashes freeze while the game is paused and keep animating after a win/lose (`Game.update`'s stage-decided path still calls `update`). Each `FlashEffect` holds only a plain `THREE.Object3D` (`transform`, no geometry/material of its own) that `sync` writes the camera-facing position/scale/rotation into before `push`ing it — since the material is additive-blended, per-instance opacity is folded into the pushed color (`baseColor × opacity`) rather than tracked separately, which is mathematically equivalent for that blend mode. `Billboard` (`render/billboard.ts`) itself remains for every other camera-facing glow (thrust plume, RCS puffs, reentry glow, sun, point-body) — those hold few, fixed-count instances, so a per-object mesh costs nothing there. `FlashEffectManager.dispose()` disposes its pool *and* the `flashResources()` geometry/material — that getter mints a fresh pair on every call rather than caching one at module scope, so this instance is their owner, unlike the bullet/casing pools whose pair is shared and must be left alone (its `map`, `glow-texture.ts`'s one cached `CanvasTexture`, is shared and is left alone here too); `EffectsSystem.dispose()` forwards to it.
- `src/game/nan-watchdog.ts` — `NanWatchdog`: シミュレーション状態に非有限値(NaN/Infinity)が入った**最初のフェーズ**を捕まえてコンソールと HUD へ報告する。**Game が所有し、`update` の各フェーズ境界で呼ぶ。** 一度検出したら以後は何もしない。存在理由は、NaN が静かに伝播したあと別の顔で表面化するため — 自機が汚染されると `FloatingOrigin` 経由で全メッシュが消えて 3D だけが暗転し、敵が汚染されると `Enemy.checkLoss` の `hitCelestialBody` 判定が false になって「再突入により喪失」と誤表示され、非有限な座標は空間グリッドのセル添字自体を壊す。伝播を止めるガードは `simulation/contact.ts` 側にあり、検出と報告はこちらの責務。
- `src/game/targeter.ts` — `Targeter`: the combat target — **`target`/`secondaryTarget` are the only state, both `CombatTarget | null` (`Enemy | Player`), and there is no auto-selection** (`aliveTarget`/`aliveSecondaryTarget` are the read side, hiding a dead individual rather than a live re-pick). `clearIfTargeting(entity: CombatTarget)` drops `target`/`secondaryTarget` if either currently points at `entity` — `active-player-controller.ts`'s `ActivePlayerController.remove` calls it (alongside `NavTarget.clearIfTargeting`) so a self-flown ship removed from `entities.players` can't linger as somebody's locked target. `Targeter` holds no `ContextMenu` of its own (施策5 §7-2: the same object opens the same `PropertyWindow` regardless of view) — it exposes `setPrimaryTarget(t)`/`setSecondaryTarget(t)` publicly, and `pickTargetAt(click, targets, project)` (the screen-nearest-within-`TARGET_LOCK_PICK_PX_SQ` search over `map-pickable.ts`'s shared `pickNearest`) for `map-context-actions.ts`'s `MapContextActions.handleCombatRightClick` to resolve a combat-view right click into a `CombatTarget`, which it then opens the corresponding property window for (kind `'ship'`/`'player'`) — that window's `targetPrimary`/`targetSecondary` items (`MapContextActions.combatTargetLockItems`, shown only in combat view) are what actually call `setPrimaryTarget`/`setSecondaryTarget`. A combat-view right click that hits neither an enemy/self-flown ship nor a base (`NavTarget`'s own base menu runs first) falls through to `MapContextActions`'s shared empty-space menu, the same one map mode's own empty-space right click opens. `handleTargetSelectKey(input, targets, project, overviewMode)` is the other, orthogonal way to set a target — the `[T]` key, still owned entirely by `Targeter`, still doing its own on-screen-nearest scan with no menu involved. It takes `overviewMode` as a plain argument and self-gates the same way `NavTarget.handleCombatBaseClick` does, since `Game.handlePointerInput` calls every pointer receiver unconditionally and leaves each one to decide whether this frame is its own. Setting one target clears it from the other role (no double duty). The secondary target is **display-only**: it gets its own cyan `secondaryOrbitLine` (`ACCENT_SECONDARY`, `theme.ts`) and enemy-list/marker highlighting, but the target-board bullet-pass marks (`updateBoardMarks`/`syncBoardMarkers`) and the ⇨/⇦ target-bearing direction markers are primary-only — `Enemy.markerItem(role, ...)` is what the marker set reads the primary/secondary/none distinction from. The relative AN/DN markers live on `NavTarget`, not here (see `nav-target.ts` above for why the two designations are separate classes). Orbit lines, board marks and the target-direction markers go out through one public `sync`. A separate `syncTargetMarkers(player, targets, displayTime, simTime, overviewMode, project, screenScale, visibilityPolicy)` is what builds the actual `GroupedMarkerItem` set for every living combat target (dropping one whose `displayState(displayTime)` is `null` — past its own prediction horizon — or that `visibilityPolicy` marks unpickable) and drives `markerManager.combatMarkers`/`.leadMarkers` with it — each item is tagged with a `MarkerRole` (`'none' | 'primary' | 'secondary'`, exported from this module) so the marker set reads the same primary/secondary distinction the orbit lines do; `Game.sync` calls it once per frame alongside the plain `sync` above, rather than assembling the marker set itself.
- `src/game/unlock-manager.ts` — `UnlockManager`: per-stage clear-count persistence (localStorage is the source of truth) and unlock-toast notification; each stage class's static `isUnlocked(clearCounts)`, read via `stage-dictionary.ts`'s `findStageClass`, stays a pure predicate over the counts.
- `src/game/save/` + `save-data.ts` — セーブは**セーブデータ(歴史線)⊃ ステージ履歴 ⊃ スナップショット**の三階層。**Every save-carrying class owns its own `serialize()`, and takes its saved counterpart as a constructor argument rather than through a separate restore step** — a composite owner just calls its children's `serialize()`s to assemble the result, and on the way back passes each child's saved slice straight into that child's own constructor as it builds it — `save-data.ts` (`GameSaveData` and its per-class fields, e.g. `PlayerSaveData`/`EnemySaveData`/`BaseSaveData`/`StageSaveData`, plus `CameraSaveData`/`ChaseCameraSaveData`/`OverviewCameraSaveData`/`FocusTargetSaveData` for the camera viewpoint, and the index-side `SnapshotMeta`/`StageHistoryMeta`/`SaveSlotMeta`/`SaveIndex`/`SlotExport` and the `SAVE_VERSION`/`SLOT_EXPORT_*` constants both sides of a boundary must agree on) is pure type definitions, never logic. `save/` splits the work by what each part is allowed to know:
  - `save-store.ts` — `SaveStore` (interface) / `LocalStorageSaveStore`: JSON persistence only, `Game`-free. The index lives at `tepui.saveIndex`, each snapshot body at `tepui.snapshot.<id>` — **split so the browsing UI can list snapshots without reading a single body** (a body carries every ship/enemy/base/part and runs tens of KB). Reads swallow their errors and return `null`; **writes deliberately let the exception through**, since the caller is the one that can free space and retry.
  - `save-slots.ts` — `SaveSlots`: the `SaveIndex` is its own field, and it is the sole thing that edits it (slot create/rename/delete/duplicate, stage histories, snapshot metas, pruning). Snapshot bodies are always `SaveStore`'s. **Retention hangs on one axis, `SnapshotMeta.pinned`** — unpinned autos are a per-history ring buffer (`AUTO_SNAPSHOT_LIMIT` = 12, oldest dropped), pinned ones are never pruned and instead refuse past `PINNED_SNAPSHOT_LIMIT` (30) rather than silently evicting, since clipping *is* the declaration that a snapshot must not disappear. `SnapshotKind` (由来: how it was taken) and `pinned` (保持: whether it survives pruning) are separate fields on purpose: promoting an auto snapshot to `kind: 'manual'` on clip would erase which trigger produced it. `addSnapshot` retries a quota-failed write while dropping its oldest unpinned neighbour, and `duplicateSlot` routes through `importSlot` so the "copy bodies, re-mint ids, roll back what was written on failure" path exists once — `SaveBrowser`'s 「ここから分岐」 is exactly `duplicateSlot` with a cut-off snapshot.
  - `snapshot-service.ts` — `SnapshotService`: the `Game` ⇄ `GameSaveData` bridge, and it never holds a `Game` reference itself — it only reads one it's handed (`capture`) or hands a plain `GameSaveData` back (`load`), so nothing here can mutate a running `Game`. `capture` walks `Game`'s own arrays (`entities.players`/`.enemies`/`.ammos`/`.bases`, `activeStage`) calling each entity's `serialize()`, also calls `game.viewManager.serializeView()`/`game.cameraSystem.serialize()` for the `camera` field, derives the list-facing `SnapshotMeta` through `hud/orbit-info.ts`'s `orbitInfo` (never re-deriving centre/altitude/speed itself) and hands both to `SaveSlots`. `load(snapshotId, expectedStageId)` is its read-only counterpart: it reads the snapshot body from `SaveStore` and validates it (body presence, `SAVE_VERSION`, `expectedStageId` against the snapshot's own `stageId`, and `isEphemerisContextCompatible` against `CURRENT_EPHEMERIS_CONTEXT` — a snapshot that explicitly records its ephemeris context must not be loaded under a different epoch/profile/pack, since that would make its absolute celestial state ambiguous), returning the `GameSaveData` on success or `null` on any failure — it never touches `SaveSlots.discardAfter` itself, since a load that turns into a running `Game` only happens through `Launcher.loadSnapshot` (`launcher.ts`, above) discarding the current run and constructing its replacement, and discarding the now-unreachable future is that caller's decision once it knows the load actually took. `GameSaveData.earthSpinPhase0` (optional, matching `bases`' own backward-compat pattern rather than a `SAVE_VERSION` bump) rides alongside `phaseOffsets` for the same reason — `EarthBody`'s initial spin phase is a per-game phase exactly like `Ephemeris`'s, just owned outside `Ephemeris` itself (Earth's spin isn't part of the analytic ephemeris model); `main.ts` is what resolves it (`initialSave?.earthSpinPhase0 ?? Math.random() * 2π`, the sole random draw for it) before `Game` exists, passing the result in as `Game`'s own `earthSpinPhase0` constructor argument, which `Game` forwards unchanged into `new EnvironmentScene(scene, ephemeris, earthSpinPhase0)` — whose own constructor calls `earth?.setSpinPhase0(earthSpinPhase0)` unconditionally (the `?.` is only about whether Earth exists in the active registry), so a snapshot with none simply gets a freshly-drawn value the same way a first launch does.
  - `autosave.ts` — `AutoSave`: **when** to capture, and nothing else. Currently the 直前枠 only (`AUTOSAVE_INTERVAL_REAL_SEC` = 60 real seconds, measured off `performance.now()` since it must not scale with time warp); the event/PNR triggers are `memos/mikanixonable/SAVE_SLOT_PLAN_2026-08-09.md` §6-3b/§6-3c.
  - `save-transfer.ts` — file I/O and the validation of foreign JSON (`exportSlotToFile`/`importSlotFromFile`/`pickAndImportSlot`). Separate from both of the above because neither the index nor the store should learn about `File`/`Blob`/`<a download>`. A `SlotExport` carries a `format` identifier so an unrelated JSON reads as "not a save file" rather than "corrupt save", validation narrows from `unknown` step by step, and a snapshot whose body is missing or whose `version` doesn't match is dropped from the metas rather than aborting the whole import. Import always lands as a **new** slot with freshly minted ids — re-reading the same file must not overwrite what is already there.
  - `legacy-save.ts` — one-shot adoption of the single-slot-era `tepui.save` key.
  **A `Game` never overwrites its own already-running state.** Its constructor takes an optional trailing `initialSave?: GameSaveData` and builds the whole world exactly once from it, in construction order — there is no public method that lets a caller push a `GameSaveData` into a live `Game` afterward, and no second pass that revisits an already-built subsystem. `new EntityManager(...)`'s constructor — given `initialSave`, or nothing at all on a fresh launch — runs first and reconstructs `players`/`enemies`/`ammos`/`bases` each through its own class's constructor (a `PlayerInit`/`EnemyInit`/`AmmoInit`/`BaseInit` discriminated union — either a fresh placement, or `{saved, simTime}`) — which ship is operated is not decided here; `ActivePlayerController` takes `initialSave?.activePlayerId` as its own constructor argument and resolves it against the just-rebuilt `entities.players` itself (id match, else the first ship, else `null` — there is no later `.set` call). HP restore splits by what the snapshot carries: a `Ship` subclass that saves its full `parts` array (`Player`) rebuilds HP in its own constructor by replacing `parts` outright and calling `refreshFromParts()`; one that doesn't (`Enemy` — only a scalar `health` is saved) instead builds default parts fresh and calls the protected `Ship.setOverallHp(savedHp)`, which prorates each part's HP to reproduce the saved total — both routes converge on `parts` as hp's one source of truth, per the `Ship` architecture note above. `Stage`'s constructor takes the matching `saved: StageSaveData | undefined` ahead of `StageDeps`, setting `scoreCounter`/`_phase` from it directly and building `Logistics` from `saved?.logistics` in that same call, since `hud`/`scene` already arrive as constructor arguments rather than through a later injection step. `GamePhase` values other than `'playing'` restore as plain constructor-assigned data (`this._phase = saved?.phase ?? 'playing'`) without replaying `showResultScreen`, which is why both `AutoSave` and `SnapshotControls`' `[F5]` clip refuse to write at all once `!activeStage.isPlaying` — a phase restored this way has no path back to `showResultScreen`, so loading one would resume play indefinitely past a decision with no result overlay ever shown. `ViewManager`'s constructor takes `initialSave?.camera?.view` as its requested initial view and resolves it through the same `canEnter` rule `setView` uses (a launch with zero ships falls back to the map, since combat view has no meaning without one to fly), then calls `applyChrome()` itself right after, so the camera/plan/future-display flags and HUD chrome are already in sync with the resumed view from the first frame — no separate re-sync step is needed for a snapshot that carries no `camera` field. `CameraSystem`'s constructor takes the matching `initialSave?.camera` and forwards each half into `CombatCameraSystem`/`OverviewCamera`'s own constructors — it takes no `player` argument at all, since `ChaseCamera` holds no ship reference of its own (see `chase-camera.ts` above) and instead reads whichever ship it's handed each frame; a snapshot with no `camera` field leaves both sub-cameras at their default viewpoints.

  **Loading a snapshot discards the running `Game` and constructs its replacement in the same page, not a page reload.** `hud/save-browser.ts`'s `SaveBrowser.onLoadSnapshot` (wired by `main.ts` to `Launcher.loadSnapshot`) hands the chosen snapshot id straight to `Launcher.loadSnapshot(snapshotId)`, which re-runs `startRun(this.launchedStage, snapshotId)` — its `initialSaveFor(stageClass, snapshotId)` resolves that id through `SnapshotService.load(id, stageId)` ahead of any fallback, and `startRun` passes whatever `GameSaveData` results as `new Game(...)`'s `initialSave` argument — so the game that ends up running was never mutated after construction, it was simply constructed with that state to begin with. `Launcher.initialSaveFor` calls `SaveSlots.discardAfter` itself once it knows a load actually took (an id was resolved and `load` returned non-`null`): everything unpinned later than the restored point is a future that did not happen, the same "editing invalidates downstream" rule `Plan`'s node edits follow.
- `src/game/hud/save-browser.ts` — `SaveBrowser`: the full-screen セーブデータ/スナップショット window, opened from the `[Esc]` menu's スナップショット item or `[F9]` (the latter via `SnapshotControls`, below). Its own `#save-browser` CSS is injected once via a module-local `ensureStyle()`, not `hud/hud-root.ts`'s STYLE.
