A **one-shot modal** like `DockView` (it rebuilds its own `innerHTML` on every action and has no per-frame `sync`, so the differential-DOM rule the per-frame panels follow doesn't apply), owned by `main.ts`, which constructs it with a `CurrentGameSource` (this file's own `{readonly current: Game | null}`, satisfied by `Launcher`) rather than a `Game` reference — `SaveBrowser` reads `gameSource.current` fresh on every use (`open()`/`close()` call `gameSource.current?.pause()`/`.resume()`, and `canCaptureNow()`/the snapshot pane's `loadable` check/`handleCaptureNow()` all treat a `null` current `Game` — no run in progress — the same as one that can't capture or load) instead of holding the `Game` of whichever run was active when it was constructed. `open()`/`close()` also register/deregister with `overlay-manager.ts`'s `OverlayManager` under id `'save-browser'` (`kind: 'modal'`, `exclusiveGroup: 'system-modal'`, same group as `PauseMenu`/the help panel), which is what lets ESC close the browser first when it's the topmost registered overlay — `Game.handleInput` dispatches ESC to whichever overlay is on top rather than `PauseMenu` opening on top of it. Left pane = slots (rename/duplicate/export/delete/play), right pane = that slot's snapshots **split into クリップ済み and 自動 sections**, so "これらは自然に消える" is visible before anything is clicked rather than after. Both panes always render (`rebuild()` builds them every time regardless of width); under `MQ_COMPACT` a `sb-mobile-tabs` `TabBar<'slots'|'snapshots'>` — hidden outside compact by CSS alone — switches which one carries `.sb-pane-mobile-active` (the other gets `display:none` via the same media query), so there is exactly one candidate set, not a second compact-only one built in parallel. Every name it prints goes through an escape helper first, since slot and snapshot names come from `prompt()` and from imported files alike. Loading is a double-click on the snapshot card itself (there is no separate load button), offered only while the viewed slot is the active one (a different history line's state cannot be restored into the running game) — an ineligible card carries no `sb-snap-loadable` class/cursor and its double-click reports the reason via `setStatus` instead of attempting a load; every card the browser does present as loadable already passed `SnapshotService.load`'s own eligibility checks by construction, so a load attempted through the UI cannot itself fail. A double-click on an eligible card calls `this.close()` and then the constructor-supplied `onLoadSnapshot(snapshotId)` — `main.ts` wires this to `Launcher.loadSnapshot` (see `launcher.ts` above) — rather than restoring in place, since loading a snapshot is expressed as discarding the running `Game` and constructing a fresh one with that state, not as mutating the running one. `handlePlaySlot`/`handleNewSlot` (the slot-switch actions `onSlotSwitched` fires for) close the browser the same way before calling back, so a run transition never fires with this modal still registered as open.
- `src/game/input/input.ts` / `input/touch.ts` — keyboard/mouse/pointer edge-trigger queue (`Input`: `update()` freezes one frame's snapshot of presses/clicks/double-clicks/right-clicks/mouse deltas; middle-drag pan and two-finger pinch are folded into the same `MouseDelta`). Double-clicks are the one edge queue sourced from a native browser event (`dblclick`, left button only) rather than this class's own pointerdown/up drag-threshold logic — double-click timing/movement judgment is left to the browser instead of reimplemented, and it queues independently of (and in addition to) the two single clicks `dblclick` implies, since `takeClicks` already consumes those through the ordinary click path. Edges are handed out **first-come-first-served**: `takeKey(code)`, `takeKeys(handler)`, `takeClicks(handler)`, `takeDoubleClicks(handler)`, `takeRightClicks(handler)` remove any event whose handler returns true, so a later module never sees it. `down(code)`/`mouse()` stay plain reads. The held-key set is dropped wholesale (`releaseAll`) on blur / `pagehide` / `visibilitychange`-to-hidden **and on the Command key's own `keyup`** — macOS withholds every other key's `keyup` while Command is held, so a key released during e.g. `Cmd+Shift+5` would otherwise stay held forever (thrust that never stops); which keys are still physically down is unknowable at that point, so all of them are released. This is what lets each module own its own keys — see `Game.handleInput` and the touch virtual-pad overlay (`TouchControls`, feeding the same `Input.setVirtualKey`; `syncModeButtons(rcsDamp, fineAttitude, progradeHold, isThrustLatched)` is its per-frame entry point — it owns the code↔button mapping, the caller passes only the current values and a `(key) => boolean` predicate the six translation buttons read to light their own `.on` state from `PlayerThrottle.isThrustLatched`). `tapKey(key)` is `setVirtualKey(key, true)` immediately followed by `setVirtualKey(key, false)` — the one way a UI button (not just the touch pad) synthesizes a single press edge; `hud/status-panel.ts`'s R/F/G/T alternate-operation buttons are its other caller. `onPointerKindChange: ((kind: PointerKind) => void) | null` (`PointerKind = 'touch' | 'mouse'`) fires whenever the most recent keydown/pointerdown/pointermove disagrees with the last-reported kind (deduped, so it also fires exactly once on the very first input of a session) — `setVirtualKey`/`tapKey` never report a kind themselves, since a HUD button can be pressed by either a touch or a mouse and reporting `'touch'` unconditionally would show the touch pad for a mouse-only player clicking an ordinary button. `Game` wires this to `TouchControls.setPointerKind` (below) only. **`onUserGesture: (() => void) | null` is a separate, narrower hook**, `Launcher.startRun` wires to `AudioEngine.unlock()` plus `Bgm.autoStart()` (the one-shot BGM autostart) — per run rather than once, since each run builds its own `Input`: it fires on keydown/pointerdown/`setVirtualKey`'s `down=true` edge but deliberately never on `pointermove` — unlocking WebAudio needs an event that actually carries user activation, and a bare pointer move would otherwise construct the `AudioContext` already `suspended`; `AudioEngine.unlock()` also resumes an already-`suspended` context on every call, since a browser may suspend one again after construction (e.g. on tab backgrounding). `onLongPressFeedback: ((point: PointerPoint | null) => void) | null` reports the long-press ring's show/hide moments — `point` to show it at that screen position (fired `TOUCH_LONG_PRESS_FEEDBACK_MS` into a touch hold), `null` to hide it (fired on the long-press's own resolution, on movement past the click threshold, on release, and from `releaseAll` when the gesture is interrupted) — `Game` wires it to a raw screen-space marker via `MarkerManager.set`/`.hide`, since `Input` itself holds no reference to the marker layer.

  `TouchControls` is constructed unconditionally by `Game` (regardless of device) but starts visually absent — `setPointerKind('touch')` is what first shows it (`.shown`, opacity 1, children pointer-events enabled), and once shown it stays shown; a later `'mouse'` kind only dims it to `.faded` (opacity 0.35, still tappable) rather than hiding it again, so a hybrid touch+mouse device keeps the pad reachable without it dominating the screen while a mouse is in use. It also toggles `document.body`'s `touch-ui-active` class in step with `.shown` — `hud/status-panel.ts`'s touch-only スロットル 1-4 `SegmentedControl` reads that same class (via CSS alone) rather than asking `TouchControls` a second time, per the rule that a display condition is computed once and shared. Both classes hold every listener they attach as a named instance field so `dispose()` can detach it again: `Input`'s twelve span `window`, `document` and **the canvas**, and the canvas belongs to `GameScene` — a handler left on it would go on receiving pointer and wheel events forever, alongside whichever `Input` came next. `TouchControls.dispose()` additionally removes its `#touch-ui` root and the `<style>` it injects, which — unlike the module-scope `ensureStyle()` blocks elsewhere in the HUD — is minted per instance.
- `src/physics/atmosphere.ts` — piecewise-exponential density model (Vallado table, 0–1000 km), plus the co-rotating atmosphere built on it: `airspeed(r, v)` (v minus `EARTH_OMEGA`×r about the Y/polar axis) and `dragAccel(r, v, bcInv)` (Cd·A/m per entity class), both folded into `dynamics.ts`'s acceleration composition; player hull temp uses Sutton–Graves stagnation heating + Stefan-Boltzmann radiative cooling (see `updateThermal` in `player/thermal.ts`).
- `src/game/hud/` — the DOM-overlay HUD, split by concern: `hud.ts` (`Hud` — just the shell: `root`/`layers`/`svgOverlay`/`overlayManager`/`helpPanel` for others to attach to or read, toast/hint/help, and the six always-on panel classes (`statusPanel`/`orbitPanel`/`targetPanel`/`contactsPanel`/`globalStatusBar`/`mapScaleBadge`, built from `hud-root.ts`'s `els` map — see `status-panel.ts` et al. below), each a public readonly field so `Game.sync` calls its `sync` directly), `overlay-layer.ts` (`buildOverlayLayers`/`bringToFront` — **the single definition of the HUD's stacking order, and the only module allowed to assign a `z-index`.** Eight fixed `<div>`s are appended to `#hud` once, back to front: `marker` (world-space markers + the SVG overlay) / `panel` (the always-on docked/status/navball/plan/creative panels) / `window` (`PropertyWindow`) / `popup` (`ContextMenu`/`ObjectPicker` popups) / `gate` (`#hud-overlay-shield`, the input-gating scrim — sits above those four so a gated modal blocks the game world/panels/windows/popups beneath it, but below `view`/`system` so a gating modal itself is never blocked by its own scrim, wherever it lives) / `view` (`BaseView`, the one full-screen view that must cover the panels/windows beneath it) / `notify` (toast/hint/the view badge) / `system` (help/end-screen/pause-menu/save-browser) — `z-index: 10` through `17` in that order. Every other DOM owner picks which layer it's a child of (via a constructor argument threaded down from `Hud.layers`, e.g. `panelRoot`/`popupRoot`) and never sets a `z-index` of its own; ordering *within* a layer is plain DOM append order, and `bringToFront(el)` — re-`appendChild`ing `el` to its own parent — is the sole way to move something to the front of its layer. A popup that opens from inside another popup (an `ObjectPicker` inside a `PropertyWindow`, a `ContextMenu` inside a placement panel) calls `bringToFront` on itself at open time, so "opened from X draws above X" falls out of the layer split alone, with no per-widget z-index bookkeeping that could get the ordering wrong), `overlay-manager.ts` (`OverlayManager` — the single registry every overlay (modal/popup/window) joins, and the sole source of the *logical* stacking order among them (the DOM order within a layer is still `overlay-layer.ts`'s). Registration declares `kind` (`'modal' | 'popup' | 'window'`), `closeOnEscape`, `closeOnOutsideClick`, `gatesInput`, and an optional `exclusiveGroup` (opening a member closes any other open member of the same group — `'system-modal'` holds `help`/`pause-menu`/`save-browser`; `map-context-actions.ts`'s unclipped `PropertyWindow`s share their own `'property-window-temp'` group so at most one stays unclipped at a time). It owns **no** `window`/`document` listener of its own for ESC or for item shortcuts: `Game.handleInput` takes `[Esc]` off `Input`'s edge queue and calls `closeTopmostOnEscape()`, which closes the highest still-open `closeOnEscape` entry, falling back to opening the pause menu only when nothing closed; the same call also runs `Input.takeKeys` over the frame's remaining pressed codes and hands each to `dispatchShortcut(code)`, `closeTopmostOnEscape`'s sibling — top-down through the stack, calling `handle.handleShortcut?.(code)` on each entry and stopping at the first one that returns `true` (an entry with no `handleShortcut`, or whose own items don't match, is transparent and falls through to the one below — this is what lets a clipped `PropertyWindow` sitting above an unclipped one in the stack still let the unclipped one's shortcuts reach it), returning `false` while `document.activeElement` is a text input so a keystroke typed into a `ValueInput` field can never double as a shortcut. It owns the **one** capture-phase `document` `pointerdown` listener for outside-click closing, iterating every `closeOnOutsideClick` entry and closing any whose `handle.contains(target)` is false — several independently-registered overlays can each close on the same click if it lands outside all of them. `gatesInput` (true only for full-screen modals — `help`/`save-browser`/`result`/`dock-view`; `pause-menu` leaves it false so background camera/map input still passes while paused) drives the `#hud-overlay-shield`'s `pointer-events`; **`isInputGated(): boolean`** is the same predicate exposed for a caller that needs to ask "is *some* gating overlay open" without naming one — `Game.handleMapPointerInput` is its sole caller, gating the map-edit/combat-targeting pointer dispatch generically rather than against one hardcoded id; **any** `kind: 'modal'` entry being open (`gatesInput` or not) drives the `body.hud-overlay-modal-open` class (hides `#touch-ui`) and fires `tepui-release-touch-inputs` — but only on that flag's own false→true transition, not on every registration change, which is what stops a second modal opening (or one of two closing) from spuriously re-releasing already-held touches. Every overlay-owning class (`ContextMenu`/`ObjectPicker`/`PropertyWindow`/`PauseMenu`/`SaveBrowser`/`ShipPlacerPanel`/`HelpPanel`, plus `ResultScreen`'s module-level singleton handle and `ViewManager`'s small `dockOverlayHandle` adapter for `'dock-view'`) implements `OverlayHandle` (`contains`/`close`, plus an optional `handleShortcut` on the three that carry item shortcuts — `ContextMenu`/`PropertyWindow`/`ShipPlacerPanel`) and calls `open`/`close`/`reconfigure` on itself — `OverlayManager` never reaches into a caller to decide how it closes. `BaseView`'s own open/closed state stays owned by `ViewManager` (`isDockOpen`); the adapter only notifies `OverlayManager` of transitions and forwards a manager-initiated close back to `ViewManager.leaveDock()` — the registration is one-way, never the reverse), `help-panel.ts` (`HelpPanel` — owns the `[H]`-toggled help panel's open/closed state, its `OverlayManager` registration (`kind: 'modal'`, `exclusiveGroup: 'system-modal'`), and its own `#hud-help` DOM (`buildHelpTableHtml()`, built once in the constructor) — the operation-reference table's touch-gesture rows (長押し=右クリック相当/二本指ドラッグ=パン/ピンチ=ズーム/ダブルタップ=フォーカス移動) sit first, ahead of the keyboard rows, so a touch player reaches every mapped gesture from the same table without a second one), `hud-root.ts` (`buildHudDom` — static DOM/style construction for the panels it builds directly (SHIP STATUS/ORBIT/TARGET/CONTACTS, the global status bar, chase-reset badge); the help panel and result screen build their own DOM instead (`HelpPanel`/`ResultScreen`, below) — `buildHudDom` only instantiates/appends them. Calls `buildOverlayLayers` once and is the one place that decides, per element it builds, which layer it becomes a child of, and calls `viewport.ts`'s `startViewportTracking()` once. Anything painted at HUD level must be given a layer, because markers are created at runtime and would otherwise land on top of everything by DOM order. `input/touch.ts`'s virtual pad sits below `#hud` entirely for the same reason — it drives the game world, so it must not cover a system window. `breakpoints.ts` is the sole definition of the responsive vocabulary the other three files (and `input/touch.ts`, `hud/base-view.ts`, `hud/save-browser.ts`, `hud/object-picker.ts`, `hud/property-window.ts`, `stage-select.ts`) all reference rather than re-deriving: three named width classes — `compact` (<700px, `MQ_COMPACT`), `medium` (700–1100px), `wide` (>1100px) — plus two orthogonal axes, `coarse` (`pointer:coarse`, `MQ_COARSE`) and `short` (<500px tall, `MQ_SHORT`, deliberately not paired with `orientation:landscape` — a short viewport gets the short-height treatment whichever way it's held). `MQ_MEDIUM_DOWN` (`(max-width:1100px), (pointer:coarse)`) is what most "shrink for mobile" rules key on — coarse forces the medium-or-narrower treatment even on a wide touch display — and `MQ_COARSE_SHORT` is the compound most relevant to a phone held sideways. `isCompactViewport()` re-evaluates `MQ_COMPACT` on each call (viewport width changes far more often over a session than pointer coarseness does, unlike `pointer-precision.ts`'s once-at-startup `isCoarsePointer`) and is read at the discrete moments a layout decision is made once — `hud-root.ts` picking a panel/rail's default collapse state, `object-picker.ts`/`property-window.ts` deciding whether to anchor near a trigger or open as a bottom sheet — never per frame. Its own `STYLE` is the concatenation of five place-specific files (`WIDGET_STYLE` is not one of them — it joins separately, only at injection time, below), each importing `breakpoints.ts`'s query strings rather than spelling out a threshold — `layout-tokens.ts` (`LAYOUT_TOKENS_STYLE`, the `--rail-w-left`/`--rail-w-right`/`--combat-panel-max-h` custom properties, defined once at `:root` and reassigned per breakpoint in self-contained `@media` blocks so a rail width or the combat panels' height cap is written in exactly one place per breakpoint; every consumer reads `var(--rail-w-left)` etc. rather than repeating the underlying `min(...)`/`calc(...)` expression), `skeleton-style.ts` (`SKELETON_STYLE`, concatenating `overlay-layer.ts`'s `OVERLAY_LAYER_STYLE` — the four置き場 that aren't a caller-built panel: `#hud` root/reset, the marker layer's z-index and glyph appearance, the shared `.panel`/`.panel-shell-*`/`.row` shell every panel builds on, the left/right rails (`.hud-rail`, width from the rail vars; `#hud .hud-map-root.active .hud-rail` alone gets `pointer-events:auto; touch-action:pan-y` so a player can grab the rail's empty space to scroll it, without an empty rail swallowing camera-drag input outside map mode) — `buildWorldRoot` mints one left/right rail pair per world root, so the combat root's own pair (populated by `buildInfoPanels` with SHIP STATUS/ORBIT/TARGET/CONTACTS) is a separate DOM instance from the map root's, each foldable independently through its own `rail-toggle` — and the screen-fixed badges (view badge/global status/map scale/chase-reset/hint/toast), `panel-content-style.ts` (`PANEL_CONTENT_STYLE`, every caller-specific panel's own content: the four info-panel rows, the object list, 軌道計画/表示設定/表示時刻/座標系/艦艇配置/ナビボール/ステージステータス/設定・ヘルプ・終了画面), `combat-view-style.ts` (`COMBAT_VIEW_STYLE`, `.hud-combat-root.active`-scoped — the SHIP STATUS/ORBIT/TARGET/CONTACTS panels' and the global-status-bar/chase-reset badge's Quiet Glass background/blur/radius, with every saturated color confined to the small areas marking the primary target, proximity to it, the secondary target, or danger; also the no-`backdrop-filter` fallback and the `MQ_MEDIUM_DOWN`/`MQ_COMPACT`/`MQ_COARSE`/`prefers-reduced-motion` responsive rules), and `map-view-style.ts` (`MAP_VIEW_STYLE`, `.hud-map-root.active`-scoped — hides the combat-only `#hud-target`, gives the always-on object-list/view-options panels a Quiet Glass and the decision-time PREDICT/frame-controls/plan panels a denser Focus Glass, and holds every map-only semantic color (the object list's selected/related-orbit rows, the view-options body-class on-state dot, PREDICT's future-vs-past accent split) plus its own `MQ_MEDIUM_DOWN`/`MQ_COMPACT` rules). `#base-view`/`#save-browser`'s own CSS lives in `base-view.ts`/`save-browser.ts` themselves, each injected once via its own module-local `ensureStyle()`, not in this file. `hud/panel-shell.ts`'s `PanelShell` (see below) is what `buildInfoPanels` wraps SHIP STATUS/ORBIT/TARGET/CONTACTS in, giving each an individual collapse toggle on top of the two-tier rail-collapse that already existed; a panel's programmatic show/hide (as opposed to the player's own collapse choice) is the `.hidden` class (`PanelShell.setHidden`/the four info panels' own `classList.toggle('hidden', …)`), never `style.display` — the `#hud .hud-rail > .panel[style*="display: none"]` inline-style-matching selector this replaced is gone. `hud/widgets/collapse-toggle.ts`'s `buildCollapseToggle(root, id, className, target, labels)`, re-exported here for `hud-root.ts`'s own callers, is the one implementation of "a button that toggles `target`'s `.collapsed` class and keeps its own glyph/`aria-expanded`/`title` in sync with it," parameterized by a `CollapseToggleLabels` (glyph/title pairs) so a caller only supplies which labels it wants — `buildRailToggle` builds the left/right map-panel rail toggles this way, and `predict-panel.ts`'s `PredictPanel` builds the PREDICT bar's own fold toggle and `hud/object-list-panel.ts`'s `ObjectListPanel` its own, both the same way. Nothing resets these: a collapsed panel stays collapsed until the player reopens it. `COLLAPSE_EXPANDED_GLYPH` (`▾`) / `COLLAPSE_COLLAPSED_GLYPH` (`▸`) are exported from here so every vertical fold in the HUD — rail toggles, the PREDICT bar, the object list, `PanelShell`'s own toggle, `PropertyWindow`'s row groups and its 詳細 toggle — points the same way for the same state; the map's own markers deliberately draw from a different glyph family (`marker/marker-glyphs.ts`) so a fold arrow can never be misread as a direction marker), `panel-shell.ts` (`PanelShell` — the common "見出し+`CollapseToggle`+本文" outer shell every individually-collapsible panel builds on: `el` (the `.panel.panel-shell` root, still carrying the plain `.panel` class so every existing `.panel`-scoped rule keeps matching), `titleEl` (the `<h3>`, exposed publicly so a caller can append embedded markup — CONTACTS' count badge, TARGET's `data-id`), and `body` (where the caller's own content goes; `.collapsed` folds it). Collapse state is per-panel-id and persists to `localStorage` under `tepui.panelCollapsed` via the exported `loadPanelCollapsed`/`savePanelCollapsed` (a stored value always wins; with none, the constructor's optional trailing `defaultCollapsed` argument decides the very first render), independent of the pre-existing rail-level `.hud-rail.collapsed`, whose hiding effect is scoped to `#hud.map-mode` (`#hud.map-mode .hud-rail.collapsed > .panel`) since the right rail also carries `TARGET`, a combat-view-permanent panel a map-session collapse must never be able to hide — the two nest (a whole rail can be folded, and a panel within it can independently be folded too), but share the *same* persisted store: `hud-root.ts`'s `buildRailToggle` reads/writes a rail's own `hud-rail-${side}` entry through the same two functions rather than inventing a second mechanism, so a rail's fold survives a reload exactly like a `PanelShell`'s does. compact 縦の既定表示(戦闘ビューの ORBIT/CONTACTS、マップビューの左右レール — PREDICT バー自体は畳まない)はどちらも `defaultCollapsed`/レールの既定値が `breakpoints.ts`'s `isCompactViewport()` を `buildHudDom()` 構築時に一度だけ読んで決まる — 初回起動が compact なら畳んだ状態で始まり、以後どちらの幅でも一度でも手で開閉すればその状態が永続する。 `setHidden(hidden)` is the *other* axis — a `.hidden` class for state the game decides (has a target, is in map mode, …), never confused with the player's own collapse choice), `viewport.ts` (`startViewportTracking`/`onViewportChange` — the sole subscriber of `visualViewport`'s `resize`/`scroll` events (falling back to `window` where `visualViewport` doesn't exist), called once from `buildHudDom`. Each event recomputes `--vvh` (the visual viewport's own height, distinct from `dvh` under an on-screen keyboard or pinch-zoom) and `--safe-*` (`max()`'d against the static `env(safe-area-inset-*)` `theme.ts` already injects, so a transient visual-viewport inset — keyboard, pinch-zoom pan — is honored without discarding the notch/home-indicator floor). `onViewportChange(listener)` is what every other module that used to hold its own `window` `resize` listener (`property-window.ts`'s reclamp, `context-menu.ts`'s reposition) subscribes to instead — `render/scene.ts`'s renderer resize is the one exception, kept as its own `window` listener since it's a renderer concern, not a layout one), `status-panel.ts` (`StatusPanel`), `orbit-panel.ts` (`OrbitPanel`), `target-panel.ts` (`TargetPanel`), `contacts-panel.ts` (`ContactsPanel`), `global-status-bar.ts` (`GlobalStatusBar`), `map-scale-badge.ts` (`MapScaleBadge`) — the six always-on info panels, one sync class per panel rather than one god object: each takes the `Game` instance directly (cherry-picking from the whole game state rather than a narrow ctx) and the shared `els: Map<string, HTMLElement>` `hud-root.ts`'s `buildHudDom` already built the panel's DOM into, and is **display-only** — it writes its own panel and touches no other module's state or DOM. Each self-throttles its own row-value refresh to a `SYNC_INTERVAL_MS` (100ms for `StatusPanel`/`OrbitPanel`/`TargetPanel`/`GlobalStatusBar`, 250ms for `ContactsPanel`), reading `performance.now()` against a private `nextSyncAt` rather than depending on a shared cadence, while the panel's own visibility (`.hidden`) is still checked every frame regardless — a panel that should disappear this frame must not wait out a stale throttle window. `StatusPanel` (`#hud-status`: RCS制動/並進出力/微調整/進行方向ホールド/視点のRCS追従/弾薬 — plus two placeholder rows `hud-root.ts` leaves for it, `status-actions`/`status-throttle-touch`, which its own constructor fills with `Button`s/a throttle `SegmentedControl` built against `Input.tapKey`, the same synthetic-press mechanism the touch pad uses: R/F/G/T (reload/prograde-reset/follow-attitude-toggle/target-select) are always shown, while the スロットル 1-4 control is shown only while `document.body` carries `touch-ui-active` — the class `input/touch.ts`'s `TouchControls.setPointerKind` already toggles, read here through CSS alone rather than a second "is touch active" query. `Input` isn't available at construction (`Hud` predates `Game`), so `setInput(input)` is a late-injection setter `Game`'s constructor calls once these buttons' `onClick`s can actually reach) and `OrbitPanel` (`#hud-orbit`: 自艦の基準天体/高度/速度/AP/PE/INC/PRD/動圧/機体温度, deriving each row via `orbit-info.ts`'s `orbitInfo` and only formatting/writing them) both hide outright with no ship and fold in map view — an object's orbital elements are read through its own `property-window.ts` window's `'軌道'` group there instead, not duplicated in these two. `TargetPanel` (`#hud-target`) shows only name/装甲/距離/接近速度/相対速度 for the locked combat target (`orbit-info.ts`'s `relativeInfo`) plus a trailing hint row pointing at the right-click property window — orbital elements and relative inclination are **not** shown here, so a target's full orbit reads from exactly one place (the property window), not two competing ones. `ContactsPanel` (`#hud-enemies`) lists living enemies distance-sorted, collapsing every enemy sharing a `waveId` (`game-entity/enemy.ts`'s `Enemy.waveId`, stage00's wave spawns only) into a single `第N波 ×count` row at that wave's nearest member's distance, so a large stage00 wave doesn't flood the list with one row per ship, and also owns the title's `count` badge (spawned/killed). `GlobalStatusBar` (`#hud-globalstatus`: MET/時間加速/NODE WARP) is independent of whether a ship exists and always syncs. `MapScaleBadge` (`#hud-map-scale`) is a thin DOM-writing wrapper around the pre-existing pure functions in `map-scale.ts` (`mapScaleFor`/`formatMapScaleDistance`, still DOM-free so `tests/physics/map-scale.test.ts` can compile it under the no-DOM `tsconfig.test.json`), deriving the ruler from the meters-per-pixel at the camera's current focus depth rather than screen center, so panning without zooming leaves the reading unchanged as long as the same body stays focused. `Hud` (`hud.ts`, above) owns all six as public readonly fields, constructed in its own constructor from the `els` map `buildHudDom` returns; `Game.sync` calls each one's `sync` directly (`hud.statusPanel.sync(this)`/`hud.orbitPanel.sync(this, attractors)`/`hud.targetPanel.sync(this, attractors)`/`hud.contactsPanel.sync(this)`/`hud.globalStatusBar.sync(this)`/`hud.mapScaleBadge.sync(this)`)), `orbit-info.ts` (`orbitInfo(entity, bodies)`/`relativeInfo(self, other, bodies)` — the DOM-free derivation of an entity's resolved center/altitude/speed/apsides/inclination/period, and of one entity's distance/closing-speed/relative-speed/relative-inclination against another; both route the center resolution through `attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor` and the shape through `elements.ts`'s `apsisAltitudes`, so `OrbitPanel`/`TargetPanel`/`MapContextActions`'s row builders and any other consumer needing the same numbers share one implementation rather than rederiving them), `widgets/` (`button.ts`/`toggle-switch.ts`/`segmented-control.ts`/`hold-button.ts`/`close-button.ts`/`value-input.ts`/`meter.ts`/`tab-bar.ts`/`collapse-toggle.ts`, one widget per file, plus `widget-base.ts`/`widget-style.ts`/`index.ts` — the HUD's one operable-DOM vocabulary; every pressable/toggleable/typeable element in the HUD is one of these, not a hand-rolled `<button>`/`<input>`. `Button` (単発クリック; `on`/`disabled` の表示は呼び出し側が `setOn`/`setEnabled` で与え、自分では反転しない — 点灯型トグルはこの `setOn` を外から呼ぶ形で表現し、別ウィジェットを持たない), `ToggleSwitch` (見出し+トラック/ノブ型の ON/OFF; `setOn` は表示のみで `onChange` は呼ばない), `SegmentedControl<T>` (3択以上の排他選択専用 — 2択の ON/OFF には使わない; `setItems` は内容が同一なら作り直さない、押しかけのボタンでクリックを落とさないため), `HoldButton` (押している間 `isHeld` が真になり、呼び出し側が毎フレーム読む), `CloseButton` (✕), `ValueInput` (数値/文字/検索入力の唯一の実装 — Enter・blur=確定、Escape=破棄; `escapeBehavior: 'revert'`(既定、確定値へ戻す)/`'clear'`(検索欄限定、空にする)を型で明示させる), `Meter` (HP/温度/電力バー; `setRatio(0..1)` は常に左から右へ満ち、`setDanger` が `--danger` 色へ切り替える), `TabBar<T>` (パネルの表示面を切り替えるタブ列), `buildCollapseToggle` (縦方向の開閉トグル)。状態語彙は `.on`(点灯)/`.pressed`(押下中、タッチと `:active` の差を吸収するため JS で付与)/`.disabled` に統一し、`widget-style.ts` の `WIDGET_STYLE`(`hud-root.ts` の `STYLE` へ連結して注入)がこの3状態の見た目を一箇所で定義する — 個別ウィジェットや呼び出し側で上書きしない。セレクタは `#hud` に閉じない(`.w-btn` であって `#hud .w-btn` ではない)— `#hud` の外(`stage-select.ts`・`input/touch.ts` の `#touch-ui`)でもウィジェットを組めるようにするためで、他ファイルの `#何か span.クラス名` 形の上書きは元から ID を持つぶん `.w-btn` より詳細度で確実に勝つ。`box-sizing: border-box` を要る要素(`.w-close`/`.w-toggle-track` — 固定寸法と border/padding を両方持つ)はここで自前に持ち、`#hud, #hud * { box-sizing: border-box; … }` の一括リセットに依存しない。`widget-base.ts` の `stopDragPropagation`(カメラドラッグへの伝播を止める)と `expandHitTarget`(`pointer:coarse` のときだけ要素自身の寸法で `--hit-target-min` まで広げる)は全対話要素が経由する共通の下地。`widgets/` (`Button`/`ToggleSwitch`/`SegmentedControl`/`HoldButton`/`CloseButton`/`ValueInput`/`Meter`/`TabBar`/`CollapseToggle`/`Slider`、1ウィジェット1ファイル、plus `widget-base.ts`/`widget-style.ts` — HUD 内のあらゆる押す/切り替える/択一/長押し/閉じる/文字数値検索入力/バー/タブ/折りたたみ/スライダーの唯一の実装。フォルダの外で組む生 `<button>`/`<input>` は、切り出し漏れのウィジェットと見なす。`SegmentedControl.setItems` は見出しを保ったまま選択肢だけを差し替える(内容が前回と同じなら何もしない — 毎フレーム同じ選択肢を渡す呼び出し側で、押しかけのボタンを消さないため)。姉妹の `TabBar` は値でなくパネルの表示面そのものを切り替える用途向けで、`role="tab"`/`aria-selected` を持つ。`HoldButton.isHeld` は毎フレーム呼び出し側が読む(`setInterval` に頼らない、他の継続入力と同じ毎フレーム参照の流儀)。`ValueInput` は文字/数値/検索入力の唯一の実装 — Enter・blur で確定、Escape は確定済みの値へ破棄(`escapeBehavior: 'clear'` は空にする、検索欄限定)、数値欄で非有限な確定は破棄扱い。コンストラクタ第3引数の任意 `onCancel` は「確定」と「破棄」を呼び出し側が区別できるようにするためのもの(`predict-panel.ts` の `DurationValueInput` がこれで内側の編集UIの開閉を判断する)。`Button`/`CloseButton`/`ToggleSwitch` はクリックで自分の `on` を反転しない — クリックは呼び出し側の `onClick` を呼ぶだけで、状態(既に持っているか、`setBodyClassToggles` のように毎フレーム鏡映しに保つ必要があるか)を持つ呼び出し側が `setOn` を呼び返す。ある画面の「on の意味」を決める場所は常に1箇所になる。`widget-style.ts` は hover・pressed(`:active` でなく JS が付ける `.pressed` クラス)・on・disabled の視覚規約を定義する唯一の場所 — ウィジェット側にも呼び出し側にも上書きは禁止で、レイアウト(位置・幅・余白)だけを別クラスで足す。`widget-base.ts` の `stopDragPropagation`/`expandHitTarget` は、ウィジェットのクリックがカメラドラッグを起動しないための pointerdown 停止と、`--hit-target-min` までのタップ領域拡張を、各ウィジェットが構築時に一度だけ適用する共通処理。Anything here that takes a click needs `pointer-events: auto` in `hud-root.ts`'s STYLE, since `#hud` itself is `pointer-events: none`, **`pointer-events` is an inherited property**, and a click that falls through reaches the canvas and fires the gun. The same reset (`#hud, #hud * { margin: 0; padding: 0 }`) outranks bare class selectors by ID specificity, so any rule that sets margin/padding/width on a HUD child must be scoped by an id — see the `#dock-view …` rules), `frame-labels.ts` (`celestialBodyName(id)` — the `AttractorId` → display-name lookup, reading `celestial-registry.ts`'s `CELESTIAL_BODIES` for a real solar-system body and falling back to the bare id for one a custom registry invented, shared by `OrbitPanel`/`TargetPanel`'s center-body rows, `camera/focus-markers.ts`'s celestial labels, `hud/object-picker.ts`'s rows and `creative/ship-placer-panel.ts`'s reference-body control, so no caller spells out "地球"/"月"/"太陽" itself), `object-picker.ts` (`ObjectPicker<T>` — the trigger button + popup used wherever the choices number in the dozens: a filter input, then the rest grouped and laid out as a multi-column grid (`.op-grid`, `repeat(auto-fill, minmax(140px, 1fr))`) so a hundred-item candidate set doesn't need a page-length scroll. `setGroups` skips the rebuild when the incoming groups are contentwise identical to what's already shown (label + each item's `[value, label]` pair, value by reference) — callers such as `FramePicker`/`ShipPlacerPanel` pass the same groups every frame, and an unconditional rebuild would fight an open popup's own filter/hover state. **`SegmentedControl` stays for anything with a handful of fixed choices** — it lays its items out in one flex row with no wrap/scroll/truncate handling, which is exactly why a registry-sized list needs a different widget rather than a wider row. Registers itself with `hud/overlay-manager.ts`'s `OverlayManager` on open/close (`kind: 'popup'`, `closeOnEscape: true`, `closeOnOutsideClick: true`) — the same registration `ContextMenu` makes, so both close through the manager's one capture-phase outside-pointerdown listener rather than a listener of their own; the popup is a child of the `popupRoot` its constructor is given — always the `overlay-layer.ts` popup layer — and calls `bringToFront` on itself when opened. Under `MQ_COMPACT` the popup is a bottom sheet (CSS `right:0; bottom:0; width:100%`) rather than anchored under the trigger — `open()` checks `isCompactViewport()` and skips the anchor-clamp math entirely there (clearing any `left`/`top` a prior non-compact open left behind, since an inline style would otherwise override the sheet's `right`/`bottom`)), `object-groups.ts` (`groupPickables(registry, items)` — the pure `MapPickable[]` → ジャンル別 `ObjectPickerGroup<string>[]` classifier `AnchorZone`'s object-picker builds on: `'body'` items split into ラグランジュ点(id が `-l1`〜`-l5` で終わるもの、`LAGRANGE_ID`/`lagrangeParentId` もここに置く)か `body-class.ts`'s `bodyClassOf` の5分類、`'player'`/`'ship'`/`'base'`/`'ammo'` はそのままジャンル、マーカー由来の kind(`apsis`/`relnode`/`eqnode`/`empty-space`)は出さない — 空のグループは返さない), `anchor-zone.ts` (`AnchorZone` — マップの座標系UIのうち「何に固定/追随するか」を選ばせる1ゾーン: 上段は登録天体・自艦・敵・基地・弾薬まで含む全候補から選ぶ `ObjectPicker`、下段は呼び出し側(`FrameControls`)が `celestial/body-visibility.ts`'s `systemMembersAt` で絞った天体列を `setNearby(members, pickables)` で受け取って並べるだけのクイック `SegmentedControl` — 押せてから拒否されることのないよう、`pickables` に無い id はクイックボタンに出さない。コンストラクタの `releaseLabel` が `null` なら「固定を解除」の選択肢自体を出さない(並進ゾーンの用途)。値は `string | null` — `null` が解除で、`onSelect` はどちらのゾーンでも同じ値を受け取る)、`rotation-zone.ts` (`RotationZone` — 同じUIのうち「何の公転に合わせて回すか」を選ばせるゾーン。`setNearby(members)` で受け取った天体列のうち `primaryOf` が非 `null` を返すもの(恒星以外)だけを `OrbitingId | null` の `SegmentedControl` として並べる — 恒星の除外はこのクラス自身の判断で、渡ってくる `members` 自体は恒星を含む。原点込みの座標系そのものは呼び出し側が `Ephemeris.frameOf` で組む — このクラスは回転対象の id だけを返す), `pause-menu.ts` (`PauseMenu` — the `[Esc]` pause menu, split into 一般 / 描画 faces by a `TabBar` (the 描画 face is `hud/graphics-panel.ts`'s `GraphicsPanel`, which writes `render/graphics-settings.ts`'s `GraphicsSettings` it holds by reference for its quality/toggle controls, plus a `SegmentedControl<DebugTargetId>` labelled デバッグ表示 — built from `render/pipeline/debug-target.ts`'s `DEBUG_TARGETS` — that writes a second, unrelated reference: a `DebugTargetHost` (`PauseMenu`'s own constructor parameter, typed against the narrow interface rather than the concrete `RenderPipeline` so `game/hud/` never imports `render/pipeline/`'s internals) whose `debugTarget` field picks which intermediate render target the whole screen shows instead of the normal frame; `GraphicsPanel.sync()` reads both `graphics.current` and `host.debugTarget` back, since the panel itself holds no state of either); a face is shown by toggling `.hidden`, so which tab is open stays a class, never a read of `style.display`. `main.ts` owns the instance and constructs it with that same `GraphicsSettings` plus the `RenderPipeline` it built right after `initScene` (structurally satisfying `DebugTargetHost`), and wires its `onOpenSnapshots` to close itself then `SaveBrowser.open()`, its `onOpenPerfWindow` to close itself then `PerfMeter.open()`, its `onBgmVolumeChange` to `Bgm.setVolume`, its `onQuitToTitle` to `launcher.returnToTitle()`, and its `onPauseMenuOpenChange` to `launcher.current?.pause()`/`.resume()`. It owns no `handleInput` — ESC is `Game.handleInput`'s sole business (see `game.ts` above): `toggle` registers itself with `overlay-manager.ts`'s `OverlayManager` under id `'pause-menu'` (`kind: 'modal'`, `gatesInput: false` so background map/camera input still passes while paused, `exclusiveGroup: 'system-modal'` so opening `SaveBrowser`/the help panel evicts it and vice versa), and `Game.handleInput` closes whichever registered overlay is topmost, falling back to `toggle(true)` only once nothing was open to close), `context-menu.ts` (`ContextMenu<T>` — a generic screen-positioned popup that holds the target `T` it was opened against, so `onSelect(act, target)` always reports back which item was chosen for which target without the caller tracking that itself; shared by `MapContextActions` (`ContextMenu<MapPickable>`, the `'empty-space'` right-click menu only — every other `MapPickable` kind, including combat-view enemies/self-flown ships, opens a `property-window.ts` `PropertyWindow` instead, see `map-context-actions.ts` above), `NavTarget` (`ContextMenu<Base>`, the combat-view base right-click menu), `NodeGizmo` (`ContextMenu<number>`, over a node index), and `ViewBadge` (`ContextMenu<true, ViewId>` — no real target, so `true` stands in for it; `ContextMenu` only fires `onSelect` when its target is non-`null`, which is the one thing a target-less menu has to route around). DOM/event handling only. Each item's `shortcut` (a `KeyboardEvent.code`, shown via `shortcutKeyLabel`) is also stored on its row's `dataset` so `handleShortcut(code)` — the `OverlayHandle` method `OverlayManager.dispatchShortcut` calls — can find and select it through the same close→`onSelect` path a click takes; `ContextMenu` itself owns neither a `window` keydown nor a `document` pointerdown listener, since it registers with `OverlayManager` on `open()`/`close()` (`kind: 'popup'`, `closeOnEscape: true`, `closeOnOutsideClick: true`) for both that and ESC/outside-click closing), `property-window.ts` (`PropertyWindow<A>` — the draggable, clippable property/action window, opened per selected map object by `MapContextActions` and as the load-inspection window by `perf-meter.ts`'s `PerfMeter` (`rows` alone, `items` empty); unlike `ContextMenu` it is instantiated fresh per window rather than reused, since several can be on screen at once, and it is appended as a child of the `window` overlay layer (its root argument, from `overlay-layer.ts` via `Hud.layers.window`) rather than `document.body` — every one of its own CSS selectors is scoped with a leading `#hud` so it outranks the `#hud, #hud * { margin: 0; padding: 0 }` reset by ID specificity. Display-only: the constructor takes a `PropertyWindowContent` (title/subtitle/rows/items) and builds the header DOM once; `syncHeader(title, subtitle)`, `syncRows(rows)` and `syncItems(items)` are the per-frame entry points, each diffing (by row `key` for rows, by a serialized `act`/`label`/`shortcut` string for items) so an unchanged value never touches the DOM and an unchanged shape never rebuilds it (same reasoning as `ObjectListPanel`/`StageStatusPanel` — a full rebuild would drop the item-click/header-drag/clip-button listeners). A `PropertyRow`'s `group` sorts it into a named, collapsed-by-default section drawn first in the row list (`map-context-actions.ts`'s `orbitRows` puts every ship/base/ammo/celestial-body orbital-element row under `'軌道'`, which is how map view shows an object's orbital elements — the ORBIT panel is combat-view-only, see `panel.ts` above), and its `collapsible` flag instead sorts a group-less row under a single "▸ 詳細を表示" toggle at the end of the row list — the expand/collapse state of both is this class's own (`groupExpanded`/`collapsibleExpanded`), not passed in, since it's pure display state no caller needs to know about; `map-context-actions.ts`'s row builders leave the combat-relevant fields (HP, distance, temperature, power, ammo) always visible — `syncItems` exists because the action set itself is state-dependent (e.g. a ship's "操作対象にする"/"削除" presence, or a follow-toggle's label) and `MapContextActions.sync` calls it every frame alongside `syncRows`, not just once at open time. Either sync reclamps the window into the viewport (`reclamp()`, a private wrapper around `moveTo` using the window's own current position) whenever its shape changed, since content growing after the window opened at a viewport edge would otherwise push it off-screen. Header-drag (pointer capture, `CLICK_MOVE_THRESHOLD` latch shared with `Input.pushIfClick` — no second threshold) and the 📌 clip toggle are the only interactive surface besides the `onSelect`/`onClose`/`onClipChange` callbacks. The constructor takes an `overlay-manager.ts` `OverlayManager` and an optional `tempWindowGroup` string; `PropertyWindow` implements `OverlayHandle` itself (`contains`/`close`) and registers/reconfigures its own `OverlaySpec` on open and on every clip toggle (`kind: 'window'`, `closeOnEscape`/`closeOnOutsideClick`/`exclusiveGroup` all `!clipped && tempWindowGroup !== undefined` — a clipped window, or one built with no `tempWindowGroup` at all (`perf-meter.ts`'s load-inspection window), never auto-closes and never joins an exclusivity group) — so "at most one temp window per group" and ESC/outside-click closing are OverlayManager's job, not a caller's callback. `close()` always does what the ✕ button does (`dispose()` then `onClose?.()`), so every closing path — ✕, ESC, outside click, a caller's own explicit `.close()` — notifies the same way. `shortcut` on a `PropertyWindowItem` is a `KeyboardEvent.code`, shown via `shortcutKeyLabel` and also matched by `handleShortcut(code)` — the `OverlayHandle` method `OverlayManager.dispatchShortcut` calls — which refuses (returns `false`) while `_clipped`, the same rule `currentSpec()`'s `isTemp` already applies to ESC/outside-click: a clipped window neither auto-closes nor answers a shortcut, so with at most one window ever unclipped there's no ambiguity about which one a keypress reaches. Under `MQ_COMPACT` the window is a bottom sheet instead of a draggable small window — CSS caps it to the bottom 40% of the viewport (`right:0; bottom:0; max-height:40dvh`), `moveTo` becomes a no-op that only clears any stale non-compact `left`/`top`, and `handleHeaderPointerDown` returns immediately (`isCompactViewport()`) so dragging never starts — clipping (📌) still works exactly as before, so several clipped sheets can still exist, just stacked rather than scattered. `moveTo(clientX, clientY)` is public so a caller can re-home an existing window (`MapContextActions`'s "already open, move to click position" path) as well as re-clamp on its own `viewport.ts` `onViewportChange` subscription; `bringToFront()` just re-`appendChild`s to its root, since siblings at the same fixed `z-index` stack by DOM order — no per-window z-index counter. When `content.onRename` is supplied, an extra ✎ button appears beside 📌/✕ and swaps `titleMainEl` for an `<input>` (`startRename`); Enter or blur commits (empty/unchanged input is a no-op) and calls `onRename(name)`, Escape cancels — either way the input is swapped back for `titleMainEl` without waiting for the next `syncHeader`. The input's own `keydown` stops propagation, the element-level exception `widgets/value-input.ts`'s `ValueInput` also follows, so a rename-in-progress keystroke never reaches `Input`'s window-level edge queue), `layout.ts` (`clampOverlayPosition`/`Point2` — the pure viewport-clamping arithmetic shared by `PropertyWindow`'s drag/resize and `ContextMenu`'s open-position clamp, so the two popups can't drift to different clamping rules), `shortcut-hint.ts` (`shortcutKeyLabel` — the pure `Escape`→`ESC`/`Delete`→`DEL`/else-uppercase conversion behind both popups' `[KEY]` item hints), `view-badge.ts` (`ViewBadge` — the top-right badge showing the game title/version, `Mode: <selectLabel, title-cased>`, and a `View: <Combat|Map|Dock> ▾` dropdown built on the shared `ContextMenu`; owned and `sync`'d by `Game` rather than by `Hud` itself, since it needs a live reference to `ViewManager` that `Hud` is constructed too early to hold — see `game.ts` above for why `Hud` predates `Game`. It holds **no view state of its own**: the label reads `ViewManager.current` and the menu is exactly `ViewManager.selectableViews()`, which already excludes the current view and anything unreachable, so a listed item can never be refused after being picked), `result-screen.ts` (win/lose/score-attack end-screen assembly; `ResultScreen implements OverlayHandle` holds the `#hud-result` DOM and a `show(result: StageResult)` method that rewrites it and registers with `OverlayManager` under id `'result'` — `kind: 'modal'`, `gatesInput: true`, but `closeOnEscape`/`closeOnOutsideClick` both `false` since the only ways off this screen are the 再出撃/タイトルへ actions already on it (`RunTransitions.restart`/`returnToTitle`, both satisfied by `Launcher`); `close()` hides the DOM and deregisters it, idempotent, called by `Launcher.endRun()` at the start of every run transition so a decided screen never survives into the next one — constructed once by `Launcher` itself (`new ResultScreen(hud, this)`), not a module-level singleton), `tick-scale.ts` (`chooseTickInterval`/`buildTicks` — the 表示時刻 slider's tick marks: pick the smallest interval from a fixed 10min→1year ladder whose tick count fits the panel, then place ticks at that interval's multiples; pure, DOM-free), `calendar-ticks.ts` (`calendarBoundaries`/`tickLabel` — the planned trajectory's ruler-style tick marks (`plan-display.ts` above): a fixed rank ladder of calendar units (1時間/3時間/6時間/12時間/1日/1月/1年), `calendarBoundaries(fromUnix, toUnix, maxCount, hourFamilyMaxCount)` picking the finest rank whose boundary count over that span still fits its own budget and enumerating that rank's boundaries, each tagged with the *coarsest* rank it actually satisfies (an ordinary midnight is a day boundary, but a month-start midnight is also a month boundary, so it's tagged the coarser way) — the budget is `maxCount` (`const.ts`'s `PLAN_TICK_MAX_COUNT`) for the day/month/year ranks but the separately, more generously tuned `hourFamilyMaxCount` (`PLAN_TICK_HOUR_FAMILY_MAX_COUNT`) for the hour ranks (1/3/6/12時間), since each hour-family step's boundary set is already a superset of every coarser hour-family step's (1時間ごとの列挙は3/6/12時間ごとの境界を全て含む) — without the separate, larger budget, a segment long enough to force a coarser hour-family step would lose the finer one's candidates outright for its whole length rather than the two coexisting and thinning gradually against each other the way `syncTickMarkers`'s screen-distance pass already does once both are present in the candidate list. month/year boundaries step through `Date.UTC`'s own month/year index rather than a fixed second count, since neither unit is a fixed duration. `tickLabel(unix, rank, mode, referenceUnix)` formats the matching string: `mode: TickLabelMode` = `'absolute'` gives the calendar form per rank (`HH:00`/`M/D`/`M月`/year), `'relative'` the signed elapsed time from `referenceUnix` (`T+3h`/`T-2d`), built on `hud/utils.ts`'s own `fmtDuration` so there is no second duration format. Which mode is in force comes in as an argument (`DisplayWindow.tickLabelMode`'s value) rather than being read here, keeping this module free of any `display-window-manager.ts` import; `calendarBoundaries`'s candidate set is the same either way, since only the label text differs. This is a separate module from `tick-scale.ts` rather than a second mode of it: that one places ticks at even fractions of an *elapsed* span with a relative `T+` label, which has no notion of a calendar-absolute month or year boundary — the two modules answer genuinely different questions, not the same question for two callers), `utils.ts` (shared formatters `fmtDist`/`fmtSpeed`/`fmtTime`/`fmtDuration`/`fmtMarkerDist`/`fmtDateTime`, plus `SIM_EPOCH_SEC` — `fmtDateTime` renders a Unix timestamp as ISO 8601 UTC (`2026-08-08T14:16:00`) by reading the date's components rather than `toISOString()`, and `SIM_EPOCH_SEC` parses `const.ts`'s `SIM_EPOCH_UTC` with its own regex rather than `Date.parse`; both exist because the sim epoch sits in a far-future year whose 5-digit form `Date.parse` rejects outright and `toISOString()` renders in the signed extended-year notation. The pair turns `SIM_EPOCH_UTC` + `simTime` into the global status bar's absolute-date readout and `plan-display.ts`'s UTC day-boundary ticks; it is deliberately independent of `Ephemeris`'s phase offsets, since those are random per-game initial phases and `SIM_EPOCH_UTC` is a fixed display anchor with no bearing on where the sun/moon actually are) — **flat dark theme: monotone grays on near-black panels (1px light edge, no shadows) with a saturated orange primary accent** (`theme.ts`'s `ACCENT` = `#ff6a00`, plus its `ACCENT_FILL_*`/`ACCENT_EDGE_*` ramps for selected/hovered/pressed states); markers and orbit lines follow the same rule — monotone base (player orbit light gray, planned orbit white, prograde/boresight light gray) with orange reserved for attention targets (target marker+orbit, lead, maneuver node/BURN, ammo pickups). The one deliberate second color is cyan (`ACCENT_SECONDARY` = `#00c8ff`, `theme.ts`) for the secondary target only — chosen specifically to be unmistakable against orange, since the two can appear on screen together (secondary orbit line, enemy-list row, marker glyph). `DANGER` (`theme.ts`) is the HUD's one warning/error color, shared by both a critically-low readout and the target-lock highlight rather than carrying two separate reds.
- `src/game/marker/` — the screen-projected marker **mechanism**, plus the few marker sets that no single object can own. `marker-glyphs.ts` holds the glyph vocabulary, split into three families by **the question a reader asks first of any symbol on the map — is it a thing, a direction, or a point on an orbit?**: `ENTITY_GLYPH` (filled: ship `▲`, body `●`, plan ghost `⬢`, placement preview `▷`), `DIRECTION_GLYPH` (arrows: prograde/retrograde `⊙`/`⊗`, normal/antinormal `⇧`/`⇩`, radial out/in `◎`/`◉`, target/anti-target `⇨`/`⇦`, off-screen bearing `↑`, grid axis `⇕`) and `ORBIT_POINT_GLYPH` (hollow: apsis `◇`, AN/DN `△`/`▽`, maneuver node `◈`, burn point `⬡`, impact `✕`). It holds **only the choices** — which glyph a given marker uses stays with that marker's owner, since nothing here knows about marker keys — so the vocabulary can be kept consistent without centralizing the decisions. The HUD's vertical fold arrows (`hud/hud-root.ts`'s `▾`/`▸`) are deliberately outside all three families, so a fold can't read as a direction marker. `marker-manager.ts` (`MarkerManager` — element pooling keyed by id, `set`/`setPosition`/`setDirection`/`setBearing`/`headingRotationDeg`/`hide`/`remove` (`hide` keeps the pooled element for keys drawn from a fixed set; `remove` drops the DOM node for keys that grow per target — enemy/LEAD markers, which would otherwise accumulate forever), label-collision relaxation and the SVG leader lines; `setBearing` is the shared "off-screen target → arrow on a ring at the screen edge" placement, used by `GroupedMarkers` (enemies), `EntityMarker` (ammo pickups and bases) and `PlanGuide` (burn direction), all with the one `DIRECTION_GLYPH.bearing` `↑` — the caller supplies an *up-pointing* glyph because the ring angle is applied as a rotation, and an off-screen hint is a direction rather than the object itself whatever the object is; **owned by `Game`, not `Hud`**, and shared by reference with every module that emits markers; `dispose()` empties the pools rather than removing `layers.marker`/`svgOverlay` themselves, which are `Hud`'s, and cancels every pending occlusion-fade `setTimeout` first — a timer left armed would fire against an element that is no longer there). `headingRotationDeg(worldPos, vel, project, scale)` is the sole place the "rotate an up-pointing glyph to face `vel`" assembly lives — it projects `worldPos` and a point `MARKER_HEADING_PROBE_PX` pixels ahead along `vel`, turns the atan2 angle between them into the +90°-corrected rotation `set`'s `rotationDeg` expects, and returns `undefined` — meaning "don't rotate" — when the projected difference falls under `MARKER_HEADING_DEGENERATE_PX`, i.e. the velocity is nearly along the view axis and the direction is numerically unreliable. `set` treats `rotationDeg === undefined` as "leave the glyph's current rotation alone" rather than resetting it to 0°, so a marker passing through that degenerate moment holds its last heading instead of visibly snapping to unrotated and back; a marker that never rotates (the combat-view direction markers, the boresight) simply never receives a defined `rotationDeg` and stays unrotated forever. **The rule is that each object syncs its own markers** — a module must not expose both `sync` and a separate `syncMarker`; the marker update belongs inside its `sync`. So the orbital-direction/boresight/`self` markers live in `player/player-markers.ts` (owned by `Player`, driven from `syncPlayer`), the target bearing + board-crossing marks in `targeter.ts`, the relative AN/DN markers in `nav-target.ts`, an object's own position marker in `marker/entity-marker.ts` (held by the object as `GameEntity.marker`), the celestial labels in `camera/focus-markers.ts`, NODE/BURN in `plan/plan-guide.ts`, and the periapsis/apoapsis icons + the planned-position ghost in `plan/plan-display.ts`. Two sets stay in `marker/` because no single object can decide them, and `MarkerManager` constructs and owns both itself as public readonly fields (`combatMarkers`/`leadMarkers`, built in `MarkerManager`'s own constructor) rather than `Game` constructing them separately. A `TODO` on those fields flags this as a staging point, not their final home: the two are a different classification from `MarkerManager`'s own "display mechanism" responsibility, and belong under a proper owner once one is found.
  - `grouped-markers.ts` (`GroupedMarkers`) [`MarkerManager`-owned as `combatMarkers`] — takes a set of `GroupedMarkerItem`s (key/class/symbol/world position/velocity/priority/label parts/bearing color) and does the things that need the whole set: collapsing screen-adjacent markers into one representative (highest `priority` wins; the rest lose their label), in combat view only, replacing off-screen ones with a screen-edge bearing arrow (`sync`'s `overviewMode` argument hides it on the map, same as `Logistics`/`CreativeStage`'s own bearing arrows — a map-mode object is already visible in the overview, so a bearing hint is redundant there), and, in map view only, rotating each marker to face its own `vel` via `MarkerManager.headingRotationDeg` (`sync`'s `scale` argument — a `ScaleFn` — is what that conversion needs; combat view leaves `rotationDeg` unset, since there the mesh itself already shows attitude). Target-agnostic and reusable; `targeter.ts`'s `Targeter.syncTargetMarkers` (below) is what supplies the set's content, from `Enemy.markerItem()`/`Player.markerItem()`.
  - `lead-markers.ts` (`LeadMarkers`) [`MarkerManager`-owned] — the ✛ LEAD markers, which depend on the player *and* the enemy and so belong to neither. Owns the per-enemy last-locked time (rebuilt each frame from the live enemy list, so it can't leak) that keeps a LEAD marker up for `LEAD_HOLD_SEC` after the target moves on. Hidden entirely in map mode / when the player is dead. Driven by `Targeter.syncTargetMarkers` too, alongside `combatMarkers`.
  - `entity-marker.ts` (`EntityMarker`) [one per `GameEntity`, held as `GameEntity.marker`] — the marker that points at **the object itself**, plus its screen-edge bearing arrow. The owner builds it in its own constructor with the DOM class, the combat-view glyph it wants, and whether the map-view label carries a distance (`Ammo`: `mk-ammo` + `▣`, no map-view distance — a pickup is found by its position on the orbit, not by chasing a shrinking number; `Base`: `mk-base` + `▲`, distance shown in both views) and everything else is one shared rule: **map view draws `ENTITY_GLYPH.ship` `▲` rotated to face the object's own ECI velocity** (a heading is only readable on an up-pointing glyph, and the overview already shows where things are, so no bearing arrow there), **combat view draws the owner's own glyph plus a `↑` `setBearing` arrow while the object is off screen**. The label is the owner's `name`, followed by the distance from `viewerPos` in combat view always and in map view only when the owner opted in. `sync` reads `displayState(displayTime)`, so the marker sits at the same future-ghost position as the object's own mesh and hides once that is `null` beyond its prediction horizon; a `MapVisibility` whose `pickable` is false hides it outright, and `icon`/`label` blank the two halves independently. `EntityManager.syncMarkers(project, scale, displayTime, overviewMode, viewerPos, visibilityPolicy)` is the single driver, walking `ammos` and `bases` with each kind's own visibility.
  - `equator-node-marker-pair.ts` (`EquatorNodeMarkerPair`) [one per `GameEntity`, held as `GameEntity.equatorNodes`] — the △▽ EqAN/EqDN icons showing where **one** object's orbit crosses its center body's equatorial plane. `GameEntity.ensureEquatorNodes(markerManager)` lazily creates the pair and returns it, so whichever module decides an object needs the icons is the one that brings it into being; `Base` instead builds its own in its constructor, since a base always shows them. `GameEntity.dispose()` disposes it, and `EntityManager.parkPlayer` (docking) disposes and drops it, since a ship out of `players` no longer reaches the per-frame sync. **`update(frame, displayTime, ephemeris, state = owner.state, samples = null)` produces the frame's two icons and `sync(project, show, cameraPos)` consumes them** — placing the markers and then dropping the icons, so a pair nobody updated this frame hides itself. That is what removes the need for a layer that collects "which objects show them" this frame: each selector just calls `update` on its own selection, and the four that do are `PlanEditor.update` (the operated ship), `Targeter.updateEquatorNodes` (the combat target), `EntityManager.updateBaseEquatorNodes` (every living base — a base is a permanent orbital fixture and approaching/docking to it is itself a plane-matching maneuver, so it shows them regardless of selection) and `NavTarget.update` (whatever entity its own `resolveEntity` finds for the nav-target id; celestial bodies and Lagrange points resolve to nothing, since they aren't themselves an orbiting "object"). `update` resolves the state's own strongest attractor and produces nothing when that body has no `degree2.pole` (sun/jupiter — no modeled equator) or the orbit plane is ~coincident with it. **`samples` decides how the crossings are found, and the split is deliberate rather than uniform**: only `PlanEditor` passes them (the plan's trailing `FinalSegment`'s `state0`/`samples`), so only the operated ship's crossings are found by scanning the integrated series (`physics/trajectory-features.ts`'s `findEquatorCrossings`) — the same reasoning `plan-display.ts`'s apsis icons follow, since the ship's planned orbit is drawn as `PlanArc`'s integrated polyline and an analytically-derived icon would drift off it under J2's epoch-dependent osculating elements. Every other object keeps the analytic `nodeAnomalies(el, eqNormal)` path, because those orbits are drawn as `OrbitLine`'s analytic ellipse — an icon derived any other way would be the one not on the line. Positions are baked/un-baked into the display `frame` self-contained (no `plan-path.ts` dependency, since the object need not be on the plan at all). `mapPickables()` (`kind: 'eqnode'`) is what `MapPickables.refresh` gathers off every entity into its candidate set. Marker keys are `eqan-${owner.id}`/`eqdn-${owner.id}`, and a point `physics/occlusion.ts`'s `isOccluded` finds behind a body (off the `Attractor[]` `update` cached at `displayTime`) is `hide`d, since the pair itself hasn't disappeared. `EntityManager.syncEquatorNodes(project, show, cameraPos)` is the single sync driver, looping `all()` so every pair is reached whichever module created it.

  `markerManager.resolveCollisions()` is the one call that must see every marker, so `Game.sync` calls it last.
- `src/game/navball/navball.ts` — `Navball`: owned by `Game`, holds `gridVisibility: CelestialGridVisibility` (the celestial-grid toggle state, loaded from/saved to `localStorage` under `tepui.gridVisibility`) and nothing else — its DOM is `hud/view-options-panel.ts`'s `ViewOptionsPanel` (the 表示 panel's 天球グリッド section, alongside the per-class icon/label/orbit-line toggles — see `camera-system.ts` above), so there is one "what does the map show" panel rather than a second one just for the grid. The constructor takes that already-built panel (`CameraSystem.viewOptionsPanel`, constructed before `Navball` — see `game.ts` above) and only wires `onGridToggle`/calls `setGridVisibility`, split on the usual build/sync line the same way every other panel-backed state owner is.

  **`gridVisibility: CelestialGridVisibility` is the source of truth for the six celestial-grid toggles** — `Navball` holds it (flipped by its own panel's `Button` rows) and `Game.sync` reads it once, passing it as `EnvironmentScene.sync`'s own `gridVisibility` argument for `render/celestial-grid.ts`'s `CelestialGrid.sync` to consume; `CelestialGrid` itself holds no visibility state of its own (see `game/celestial/environment-scene.ts` below). Grouping the grid toggles inside the 表示 panel rather than a separate window is a spatial choice only. Its initial value is read from `localStorage` (`tepui.gridVisibility`, `loadGridVisibility`) and written back on every toggle (`saveGridVisibility`), so it survives a page reload.

`src/game/celestial/` — the meshes/lights/labels for each individual celestial body (THREE-dependent), as opposed to `physics/ephemeris.ts`'s "where is it" (THREE-free). Moved here from `render/` because it already depended on `game/camera/`, `game/floating-origin` and `game/const` — a `render/` regulation violation this move corrects.
- `celestial-body.ts` — the abstract `CelestialBody`: `id: AttractorId`, `build(scene)` (one-time mesh construction), `sync(fo, displayTime, cameraSystem, ephemeris)` (per-frame placement). Holds no position/velocity of its own — `Ephemeris` is the only source of truth, read fresh every `sync`.
- `src/game/celestial/point-field.ts` — **THREE-free** generation and evaluation of the solar system's diffuse point clouds (main belt, Jupiter's L4/L5 Trojans, the Hilda group, the Kuiper belt's cold/hot populations, the scattered disk) as plain orbital elements, so it's a `physics/`-style pure module covered by `test:physics` (`tests/physics/point-field.test.ts`) despite living under `game/celestial/` (display-only — these points are never a gravity source, pick target, or focus target, so they don't belong in `physics/attractor.ts`'s `AttractorId` registry). Each group is one `PointFieldDef` in the `POINT_FIELD_DEFS` array — a size distribution (`'semiMajor'` with a range plus optional Kirkwood-style Gaussian-rejected gaps, or `'perihelion'` for the scattered disk, whose q clusters at 30–40 AU rather than its a) crossed with an eccentricity/inclination range, plus an optional `ResonanceDistribution` for a group locked to a Jupiter mean-motion resonance. `generatePointField(seed = ASTEROID_SEED)` is deterministic (`mulberry32`, not `Math.random`, precisely so a save/replay reproduces the same field) and returns one `PointFieldGroup` per def; `allPoints` flattens them. A resonant group's `l0`/`lonPeri`/`meanMotion` are solved from the resonant angle σ = p·λ_J − q·λ_H − (p−q)·ϖ_H staying near one of `librationCentersDeg`: the 1:1 Trojans (p=q) have no ϖ_H term, so σ fixes `l0` directly and `meanMotion` is Jupiter's own `lRate`; the 3:2 Hilda group (p=3, q=2) instead draws `l0` freely and solves for `lonPeri`, with three libration centers (0°/120°/−120°) reproducing the real Hilda group's three-lobed clustering relative to Jupiter — a single center only reproduces one lobe, since nothing else about the model forces the other two to be occupied. `PointElements` carries a precomputed `meanMotion` alongside the usual elements specifically because `pointPositionAt(el, t)` runs across the whole field every frame update and a `sqrt` per point per call would be wasted work. `pointPositionAt` returns a **sun-centered** position (via `elements.ts`'s Kepler solve + `Q_ECLY_TO_ECI`) — ECI-izing by adding the sun's own position is the caller's job, same split `elements.ts` itself follows elsewhere.
- `src/game/celestial/point-field-view.ts` — `PointFieldView`: the `THREE.InstancedMesh` draw side, one `InstancedMesh` **per group** (a `GROUP_VIEW` table of draw radius/color keyed by `PointFieldDef.id`, since the Kuiper belt/scattered disk sit an order of magnitude farther out than the main belt/Trojans/Hildas and need a larger draw radius to stay visible at that zoom — this table is this module's own, since `point-field.ts` stays THREE-free), owned by `environment-scene.ts`'s `EnvironmentScene`. Geometry is `TetrahedronGeometry`, not a flat billboard/plane — a flat shape has a normal, and at some camera angle every instance would go edge-on and the whole field would flicker out at once; a tetrahedron always presents some visible face. `update(t, overviewMode, ephemeris)` is a no-op while `!overviewMode` (never drawn in combat view, so there's nothing to evaluate) and otherwise re-evaluates only **1/8 of the points per frame** (`UPDATE_FRACTION`, round-robin via a persistent `cursor` per group) — justified because the fastest possible orbital period in the field is several years, so a few frames of position lag is sub-pixel at any map zoom. Positions are cached sun-centered (`positions: Vec3[]`, mirroring `pointPositionAt`'s own convention) because the sun's own ECI position moves every frame regardless of whether a given point's orbital position was just refreshed. `sync(fo, overviewMode)` rewrites every instance's `instanceMatrix` unconditionally each frame (not just the ones `update` refreshed) — the floating origin itself moves every frame, so even an unrefreshed point's on-screen position must be re-baked.
- `earth-body.ts` — `EarthBody`: wraps `render/earth.ts`'s `Earth` (self-rotation angle, sun direction, aurora/atmosphere `tick`). `phase0` (the spin's initial phase, `0` until set — the random draw itself lives in `main.ts`, the sole `Math.random()` call for it) is mutable and exposed via `spinPhase0()`/`setSpinPhase0()` so it can round-trip through a save the same way `Ephemeris`'s own phase offsets do — see `GameSaveData.earthSpinPhase0` below.
- `sun-body.ts` — `SunBody`: the sun's two views (`render/stars.ts`'s `Sun`, holding both). Combat view keeps the camera-relative glow billboard (a flat sprite reads fine at that scale); the overview camera instead places the real `Sun.mesh` — a self-lit `MeshBasicMaterial` sphere, since the sun is a light source and must not shade under the scene's own lighting — at its true ECI position and `R_SUN` radius, the same "real position/radius on the map" rule `SphereBody` follows for the moon/Jupiter; whichever mode isn't active hides its own visual. It owns no light: the scene's one `DirectionalLight` illuminates only what is drawn near the render origin, so it belongs to `EnvironmentScene` alongside the ambient light rather than to the star's own view.
- `sphere-body.ts` — `SphereBody`: the shared "textured sphere at a compressed display distance" view for any orbiting body that needs nothing more (the moon and Jupiter). The one place the `visDist * radius / trueDist` scaling formula lives, so every body drawn this way keeps its true angular size — a shaped body scales all 3 axes by the same factor, so the property holds unchanged. **The sphere mesh rides `screen-lod.ts`'s `SPHERE_LOD_LADDER`, the same as `render/earth.ts`**: the constructor calls `buildSurface(level)` once per ladder tier, collecting the resulting `CelestialSurface`s' meshes (initially hidden) into a `THREE.Group` — since WebGPU never allows swapping a live mesh's own geometry, tessellation is instead varied by building every tier's mesh once and toggling `visible` (private `syncLod`, mirroring `Earth.syncSurfaceLod`); `build(scene)` adds that one group to the scene, and every per-frame transform (`position`/`scale`/`quaternion`) is written once on the group rather than on an individual mesh, propagating to whichever tier is currently visible. `sync` derives the apparent diameter from the body's **true** ECI position (`Ephemeris.positionOf`, not the compressed draw position) and `CameraSystem.activeCameraScale`, so the LOD/visibility choice reflects true angular size in both views alike; below `screen-lod.ts`'s `showsPhysicalSphere` threshold the whole group (and any ring) is hidden outright — the threshold compares against `outerRadius` (the ring's own outer edge when the body has one, so a small body's still-visible ring doesn't vanish with it), while the LOD tier itself is chosen from the body's own `radius` alone. The constructor resolves `solar-system.ts`'s `shapeAxes(radius, shape)` once into a local `THREE.Vector3 axes` (`shape` an optional `ShapeDef` argument, `undefined` = a sphere of all 3 axes = `radius`); `sync` writes the group's `scale` from `axes` (map view) or `axes` scaled by `visDist/dist` (combat view) instead of `setScalar`. Because THREE composes a matrix from position/quaternion/scale independent of assignment order, `axes` is always interpreted in the body's own (pre-spin) local frame regardless of which of `quaternion`/`scale` is written first — a shape is a fact about the body, not about ECI, so it must not rotate with the spin write. Orientation is not `Object3D.lookAt` — `sync` reads `ephemeris.poleAt(this.id, displayTime)` and, when non-`null`, sets the group's quaternion straight from `body-orientation.ts`'s `spinOrientation(axis, spinAngle)`; a body with no `pole` model simply keeps whatever orientation it already had. The Moon's tidal lock therefore falls out of its synchronous spin phase rather than a per-frame "face the parent" override (verified over a full sidereal-month sweep: the modeled meridian tracks the true Earth direction to within ~10.1°, the size of lunar libration). An optional constructor argument `rings?: RingSystemDef` builds a `game/celestial/ring-view.ts` `RingView` in `build()`, added to the scene **as a sibling of the body group, not a child** — see `ring-view.ts` below for why. `sync` scales the group by `axes × (scaleFactor/radius)` (3 axes, so a shaped body's flattening survives the map/combat distance switch) but hands the ring only the uniform `scaleFactor` itself (a ring is a circle in the equatorial plane, so it takes no flattening of its own even when the body it orbits does), and passes the resolved pole axis (`null` for a body with no `pole` model, in which case the ring is left unrotated by this class — `RingView.sync` itself no-ops on a `null` axis).
- `body-class.ts` — `BodyClass` (`'star' | 'planet' | 'dwarf' | 'satellite' | 'smallBody'`) and `BODY_CLASSES: Record<SolarSystemId, BodyClass>`. **A display-importance judgment, deliberately separate from `solar-system.ts`'s `kind`** — `kind` says what a body orbits (a dynamical fact), this says whether the map shows it by default, which is what splits dwarf planets and comet nuclei out of the `kind: 'planet'` crowd. Kept THREE-free (unlike `celestial-registry.ts`) so the visibility rules below can be evaluated — and tested — with no renderer. `bodyClassOf(registry, id)` falls back to the dynamical `kind` for an id a custom registry invented.
- `body-visibility.ts` — `BodyClassToggles`/`DEFAULT_BODY_CLASS_TOGGLES`/`bodyClassVisible(cls, toggles)`/`alwaysFullyVisibleIds(registry, focusId, nearbyIds, toggles)`/`bodyIconLabel(registry, toggles, id)`: the toggle table and the per-class/per-id predicates `map-visibility.ts`'s `MapVisibilityPolicy` (below) composes into the actual map answer. `focusId: AttractorId | undefined` — `undefined` is the camera fixed on a `FocusTarget` with no corresponding body (a `'point'` target baked into a coordinate system rather than following a specific object), in which case `alwaysFullyVisibleIds` falls back to the star plus whatever `nearbyIds` the caller resolved from the camera's own position. `alwaysFullyVisibleIds` is the toggle-independent part: every star, plus the camera's current system (`nearbyIds`), plus (once `focusId` is defined) the focused body's ancestor, child, or **planetary-system sibling** (sibling only below the star, since every heliocentric body is a sibling of every other and the star already covers that layer) — so focusing Jupiter surfaces the Galilean moons and leaving it puts them away again; each of those still has to clear its own class's category toggle, so an always-visible exception can't resurrect a category the player switched off. **Icon (the marker dot) and Label (the name) are independent per class** (`planetIcon`/`planetLabel`/`dwarfIcon`/`dwarfLabel`/`satelliteIcon`/`satelliteLabel`/`smallBodyIcon`/`smallBodyLabel`/`lagrangeIcon`/`lagrangeLabel`) — wanting a body's position without its name (or the reverse) is a real request, and `bodyIconLabel(registry, toggles, id)` is the per-id answer a caller already inside `alwaysFullyVisibleIds` must not call (that case is always icon:true/label:true, decided by the caller before falling back to this function). `planetOrbit`/`dwarfOrbit`/`smallBodyOrbit`/`satelliteOrbit` are a third, orbit-line axis independent of both — `bodyIconLabel` never reads them; `MapVisibilityPolicy.body(id).orbit` does, and `environment-scene.ts`'s reference lines read that. `satelliteOrbit` is additionally gated (in `map-visibility.ts`'s own `orbitForBody`, not in `bodyIconLabel`) on the camera/focus being in that satellite's own planetary system — Earth's moon excepted, always shown — since every satellite's ellipse drawn at once buries the inner solar system; `satelliteIcon`/`satelliteLabel` carry no such gate, since `FocusMarkers`' crowding relaxation (screen-space label thinning, not a per-system toggle) already keeps a map full of satellite markers legible. `lagrange` has no orbit flag at all (a Lagrange point has no orbit to draw). Defaults: every `Orbit` flag off except `planetOrbit` (drawing every dwarf's/small body's/satellite's ellipse at once buries the inner solar system; planets are few and form its skeleton) — and every `Icon`/`Label` flag on, since crowding relaxation keeps a full marker set legible regardless of count. **Proximity is deliberately not an axis**: camera distance changes continuously, so a distance threshold would make labels and list rows flicker mid-zoom; the focus parent/child relation is the discrete stand-in that only changes when the player asks it to. `systemChainAt(registry, cameraPos, attractors)` is the one exception, and deliberately so: it walks from whichever body most strongly pulls at `cameraPos` (`attractor.ts`'s `strongestAttractor`) up through its ancestors — a continuous quantity, camera position, driving a discrete result — because it answers "what system is the camera physically in" rather than gating what's shown; a body not in the active registry (a living gravity-bearing `GameEntity`) returns a single-element chain rather than an ancestor walk, since it has none. `systemMembersAt(registry, cameraPos, attractors)` builds on it for the UI's actual need: the chain plus each chain body's own children (skipping a star's children, or a Jupiter-chain list would pull in every other planet) — a fixed near-to-far, body-then-its-children order so the caller's choices don't reorder frame to frame. `frame-controls.ts`'s `AnchorZone`/`RotationZone` use it to build their quick-pick/rotation-target lists for "the system I'm currently near."
- `map-visibility.ts` — `MapVisibilityPolicy`: **the single answer to "what does the map show, and what may be selected", for celestial bodies and for game entities alike.** Constructed from `(registry, toggles, focusId, nearbyIds)` — a plain per-frame value, not stored state — **once per frame, by `MapPickables.refresh` in the update phase** (`MapPickables.visibilityPolicy`), and handed from there to everything else that judges the same frame: `FocusMarkers.update` takes it as an argument, and `Game.sync` reads it back off `MapPickables` and passes it to `EnvironmentScene.sync`/its reference lines and on to `Stage`/`Logistics`/`CreativeStage`/`Targeter` for their own markers and orbit lines. Each of those still accepts an absent policy and builds its own from the same four inputs, which is what keeps them callable outside the map path rather than a second live judgment — one shared instance is what guarantees the pick set and the drawn set can't be answered from different focus ids or camera positions. It answers one struct, `MapVisibility {category, icon, label, orbit, pickable}`: `category` is the class's own on/off (the thing a hidden class fails first, short-circuiting everything else to false), `icon`/`label` are the marker dot and the name independently, `orbit` is the orbit/reference line, and `pickable` is `icon || label` — **an object nobody can see must not be clickable**, which is exactly what a separate hit-test filter would let drift. `body(id)` resolves a Lagrange point (id suffix `-l1`…`-l5`) off the `lagrange*` toggles, an unregistered id to all-false, and anything else through `bodyClassOf` → the class toggles, with `alwaysFullyVisibleIds` forcing `icon`/`label` true for the star, the focused body's immediate family, and the camera's current system; `orbit` additionally applies the satellite/focus-system rule above. `entity(kind, isActivePlayer)` does the same for `'player' | 'ship' | 'ammo' | 'base'` off their own four toggles apiece — with one exception: the **operated** ship keeps `category`/`icon` true even with its category switched off, since losing your own position on the map is never a display preference; its label and orbit line still follow the toggles, so the exception can't quietly re-enable what was turned off.
